# PROJECT / PROGRAMME SUMMARY

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE A OF 86

### A2. Project / Programme Executive Summary (max 300 words)

Modelling of climate change scenarios for Pakistan shows that if agriculture and water management in the Indus River Basin continue to be managed as they are, significant changes in precipitation will pose serious threats to the future livelihoods of farmers and to the Pakistani agricultural sector. This project was designed to change this reality by developing a national framework for climate-resilient agriculture and water management, with an emphasis on producers successfully adapting to climate change and able to sustain their livelihoods.

The project objective is to develop a national framework for climate-resilient agriculture and water management by putting in place state-of-the-art technology (Component 1); building farmers' resilience to climate change through the adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices, including improved irrigation and water management (Component 2); and strengthening Government's capacity to support their communities to adapt. To do this, the project will develop the country's first national framework for climate-resilient agriculture and water management, with a focus on climate adaptation and expanded sustainable uptake of climate-resilient approaches (Component 3). The project will be implemented in four pilot districts in Punjab province, where farmers are most vulnerable to climate change. The Ministry of Climate Change, currently Pakistan's National Designated Authority, will provide general oversight while selected provincial government departments will provide technical support for the implementation and monitoring activities. They will collaborate with partners from civil society and the private sector, particularly at the field-level, to provide training and capacity building in climate-resilient agriculture and water management practices for project beneficiaries, including women farmers as well as professionals involved in project capacity development.

The project will have a 20-year lifespan of project outcomes. The project is subject to approval by the Board of Directors upon request of the Government of Pakistan. FAO will function as both the Accredited Entity and Executing Entity for the project.

### C1. Strategic Context

#### Pakistan’s Vulnerability to Climate Change – Agriculture in the Indus River Basin

1. **Pakistan was ranked 7th**, just after the Philippines and Bangladesh, on the 2017 Global Long-Term Climate Risk Index published by German Watch and it is expected to be severely impacted by the negative effects of climate change.
2. **Agriculture** in Pakistan is highly vulnerable due to its heavy dependence on a single river system (the Indus River) for water supply, agricultural productivity, and food security. The Indus River Basin provides 95 percent of the country’s total cultivated land area, employs more than half of the labour force, produces more than 90 percent of the country's food supply and generates 75 percent of the country's export earnings.
3. **In the context of a changing climate**, the nexus between water and agriculture is of primary importance for Pakistan as it is one of the most vulnerable countries to climate change.

#### About 94 percent of the surface dedicated to agriculture in Pakistan is irrigated agriculture, and about 90 percent of the country's agriculture in terms of land area takes place in the Indus River Basin. The Indus River Basin is a complex system involving interactions between snowmelt from the Himalayas, rainfall, groundwater, and agriculture. Agriculture in the basin is governed by a complex set of interactions involving the timing and volume of meltwater discharge from snow and glacial sources, patterns and location of precipitation and agricultural seasonality.

#### Climate Change Scenarios and Impacts

1. **Various emission scenarios predict that climate change will have an increasingly serious effect on the hydrology of the Indus Basin**, largely as a result of rising temperatures and changing patterns of precipitation.
2. **Rising temperatures** are expected to lead to increased evaporation, reduced snow cover, earlier onset of winter months and in mountainous northern areas that are the source of most meltwater discharge (Ismail et al., 2016).
3. **Analysis of available research data indicates that climate change manifested through significant temperature increases will have a major impact on the Indus Basin’s hydrology** and agricultural productivity.
4. **A detailed overview of these climate change projections and anticipated impacts – summarized in Table 5.1, Section 5.2.1 of this document and Appendix B – is provided in Section 5.2 of this document and its Appendices. Seven of these impacts can be considered to be especially significant for the basin’s factors**. They are briefly summarised below and elaborated in more detail in the Feasibility Study.

#### First, temperature increase will shift the proportion of precipitation falling as snow versus rainfall. The projected change is from 50 percent (1971–2000) to between 68 percent and 75 percent in 2017–2100 (Palazzi et al., 2014; Luzi et al., 2016). This shift is of particular concern given the basin’s reliance on snowmelt for the slow replenishment of river flow during the agricultural season.

# DETAILED PROJECT / PROGRAMME DESCRIPTION

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL) | PAGE 6 OF 98

* **Sixth:** temperature increases will cause snow and glacial melting to start earlier and continue later each year, augmenting stream flow in the early spring and autumn – an increase in flow that is out of sync with the agricultural calendar.
* **Third:** temperature increase is projected to significantly reduce glacier ice extent by 2071-2100, eventually reducing the contribution of glacial melt and therefore total water availability across the entire basin (Lutz et al., 2016).
* **Fourth:** the location and timing of water availability within the Basin will shift with the availability in upstream areas decreasing while downstream areas become more reliable. The overall volume of water available is decreasing, with more acute decreases from spring through to mid-summer (Ali et al., 2015; Lutz et al., 2016).
* **Fifth:** temperature increase will increase basinwide evapotranspiration and decrease diurnal temperature range, resulting in increased crop water stress.
* **Sixth:** temperature increase will reduce the number of days with maximum temperatures above high thresholds. An analysis of historical data shows that the frequency of hot days has already increased by 10-25 percent over most parts of Pakistan (see Appendix 2 of the Feasibility Study for more information).
* **Seventh:** there is an increased risk of extreme weather events due to the highly uncertain nature of precipitation projections and the complex hydrological regime in the Indus Basin. It is difficult to predict the frequency, intensity or duration of such events.
* *These changes will significantly impact the yields of the main staple crops grown in the Indus Basin: cotton, wheat, rice, sugarcane, and maize. The impacts on cotton production are described in more detail in Section 2.C below.* A more detailed analysis of how these cropping systems are likely to be affected by the aforementioned climate change challenges is outlined in Section B.4 of the Feasibility Study.
* **The impacts of climate change on agriculture could also have serious socio-economic implications for at least 80 million people living in the Indus Basin, who depend almost entirely upon agriculture and predominantly tenant farmers.* Around 70 percent of these individuals are women, and about 43 percent are managing agricultural land. The majority of these farmers lack access to irrigation water, fertilizers, seeds, and other inputs; they also lack assets to buffer shocks and limited access to the information, new technologies, finance and government services needed for adaptation. These factors, combined with a lack of infrastructure development in the region, limit their access to resources, which can in turn amplify social friction leading to instability, conflict, displacement of people and changes in migration patterns.
* **In recognition of the threats that climate change poses to the country’s economy,** the Ministry of Planning, Development and Reforms explicitly proposed to “climate-proof economic growth from the impacts of climate change” as a key objective for Pakistan. In 2011, in line with this objective, the Government published the Pakistan Framework for Economic Growth developed in 2011. Towards this goal, the government issued a National Climate Change Policy (NCCP) and a policy document on agriculture development in 2012.* At the 22nd Conference of the Parties (COP-22) to the UNFCCC held in Marrakech, Morocco from November 7–18, 2016, Pakistan highlighted its National Climate Change Policy, its National Disaster Risk Reduction Policy, and its commitments under the Paris Agreement. In December 2016, the Government approved the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) for Pakistan. The Government approved the Pakistan Climate Change Act with provisions for the Pakistan Climate Change Council headed by the Prime Minister.

# DETAILED PROJECT / PROGRAMME DESCRIPTION

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 7 OF 86

### Key climate change (CC) Impacts identified by the project

1. Temperature increase will reduce the proportion of precipitation falling as snow.
2. Temperature increase will cause snow and glacial melting to start earlier in the year.
3. Temperature increase will significantly reduce glacier extent by 2050.
4. The location and timing of water availability within the Basin will shift.
5. Temperature increase will increase lake evaporation, decrease diurnal temperature range, resulting in increased crop water requirements.
6. Temperature increase and associated heat stress will decrease crop yields.
7. There is an increased risk of extreme weather events (droughts, floods).

### Major challenges associated with key CC impacts

1. India Basin farmers will need to manage and use water for agriculture more efficiently.
2. Short/medium-term changes in the timing, distribution and availability of water resources may affect agriculture even if the overall volume is not necessarily declining in this time period (long-term water scarcity 2071-2100).
3. Farmers (and their farming systems) must contend with:
   - heat stress and its negative impacts on crops;
   - increased transpiration and crop water requirements at higher temperatures; if the water supply is not declining, there will be less water in the Basin in the short run); and
   - increased risk of extreme weather events.

### Immediate response measures needed

1. Support India Basin stakeholders – including local authorities – to adopt cutting-edge systems for monitoring and modeling water resources, and build their capacity to use such systems for information sharing and farm management decisions.
2. Farmers (and their farming systems) must contend with:
   - more resilient crops in the face of heat stress;
   - retain/increase soil moisture; and
   - select crop varieties that produce the necessary agricultural output; and
   - are more resilient to extreme weather events.

# C2. Project / Programme Objective against Baseline

The project aims to reduce climate change among the most vulnerable farmers in the India Basin and to strengthen government's capacity to support communities in adapting to climate change.

The project outcomes that will result in the achievement of this objective are:

- Strengthened institutional and regulatory systems for climate-resilient planning and development (A5.0)
- Increased access to information, knowledge and skills for better decision-making (66.0)
- Strengthened adaptive capacity and reduced exposure to climate threats on farm (A7.0)
- Strengthened awareness of climate change threats and risk reduction policies (A8.0)

These are directly aligned with the outcomes of the GCF Adaptation Performance Measurement Framework, as numbered above.

## Baseline Agriculture, Support Services and Livelihoods in the Target Districts

1. The project area is composed of the districts of Dera Ghazi Khan, Khairpur, Lodhran, Multan and Muzaffargarh in Punjab Province, and Badin, Sanghar and Umer Kot in Sindh.
2. The vulnerability to climate change of households, natural resources and socio-economic conditions in the project area at baseline is high. The project area was defined and drawn up in accordance with the most vulnerable districts within the India Basin considering the vulnerability of the people and their agricultural systems.

3. The number of vulnerable households in the project area is estimated at 2.6 million, with a total of about 16.1 million people living on less than $2 per day (PPP) income, which is considered to be below the poverty line.
4. About 39 percent of the households in the Punjab project districts fall into the lowest quintile in terms of household wealth and are therefore considered extremely poor according to the Pakistan Household Expenditure Survey.
5. According to the National Nutrition Survey (2011), Sindh is the poorest and most food-deprived province in Pakistan. In the project area, 48 percent of children under five years old were found to be stunted or malnourished; 37 percent experienced moderate or severe hunger.
6. In Punjab, almost 60 percent of households were classified as food insecure, and about 25 percent of these households were severely food insecure. The poverty rate in the project area is low compared with other parts of Pakistan but still high at 41.8 percent (Sindh) and 39.7 percent (Punjab).
7. Women are particularly low among women, at 30.5 percent in Punjab and 16.5 percent in Sindh.
8. The absence of children from school was also found to be high with a rate of 24.5 percent for girls and 29.5 percent for boys.
9. Children working in the form of child labour – considered detrimental to children according to the International Labour Organization (ILO) and the United Nations Children's Fund (UNICEF) definitions – are or continuing work and school.

10. In the project area, an estimated 63-85 percent of farmers work small farms.
11. A large number of these small farms are less than five acres in size, which is common on small and medium-sized parcels of land in Pakistan and usually provide the landowner with half of the total farm income. The remaining income comes from livestock or other sources such as agriculture.
12. Small farmers generally rent their land to tenant farmers; 30-45 percent of the land in Sindh is rented out, while only 38.6 percent of farmers in Punjab and 22.7 percent in Sindh report to own agricultural land.
13. Women are more likely than men to be involved in agriculture in Sindh and 98.2 percent in Punjab – own some livestock, typically 1-2 animals including cattle, buffalos, sheep or goats.

## Farming work is done by both men and women

14. Farming work is done by both men and women, and can involve landowners and tenants as well as general labourers not living on the farm.
15. Both men and women practice integrated farming as a main source of livelihood. Livestock rearing is also an important activity for many farmers in the project area; however, livestock are generally not considered to be farmers despite their widespread presence in farming work. In terms of the division of farm labour, women are more likely than men to be involved in activities such as sowing and harvesting crops, especially planting, in particular sugarcane, as well as harvesting crops, especially cotton.
16. Women generally do not have ownership over their own productive assets and rarely access household income derived from their labour inputs to farm work.


---
**Page 15**
---

15. The total cropped area of the eight districts is about 3 million hectares, which is equivalent to about 40 percent of the total area (about 8 million hectares) in the target districts. Wheat, rice, cotton and sugarcane are among the most common crops grown in the targeted districts. A detailed overview of the predominance of these crops within each district is included as Annexure B.
16. Agricultural development in the Indus Basin is shaped by a range of extension and advisory services. Extension methods continue to rely heavily on face-to-face interactions with farmers, particularly for smallholder farmers. These methods include On-Farm Water Management workshops that provide important extension and advisory services to farmers related to irrigation management, water use efficiency, and other aspects of sustainable agriculture. However, outreach to vulnerable smallholder farmers has been limited, in part because of staffing constraints among some service providers. Additional information about extension services (and gaps therein) is included in Section 3.2 of the Feasibility Study, as well as in Annexes A.4: Options for Extension and Advisory Services and A.5: Mainstreamed Improved Practices.
17. The availability of financial services has had a significant impact on levels of agricultural development in the Indus Basin. In particular, access to Pakistan’s agricultural credit market is around 60 percent of its agricultural credit. The State Bank of Pakistan – a key actor in this area – has set a target of USD 1 billion in agricultural credit for the Indus Basin districts over the next three years. This represents an increase from the current level of around 3.27 million farmers across Pakistan, small and vulnerable farmers - who comprise a large share of the agricultural population - continue to rely on (often exploitative) middlemen, who are often among the few available sources of credit for remote rural areas. A detailed overview of financial services in agriculture in the Indus Basin is included in Section III of the Feasibility Study, as well as in Annexes B: Financial Services and the Enabling Environment.
18. Within the eight target districts, baseline agriculture and on-farm water management practices leave the producers vulnerable to crops highly sensitive to the anticipated impacts of climate change. Particularly problematic are increasing temperatures and changing hydrological conditions, which will reduce soil moisture and increase evaporation rates, leading to a higher risk of crop failure. As ranked by the National Drought Monitoring Center in PMD, taking into consideration the magnitude, duration, intensity, and spatial extent of droughts, districts such as Thatta, Dadu, Sindh, Sukkur, Jacobabad, Sanghar and drought and flood mints. Sanghar in Sindh, and to a lesser extent Muzaffargarh in Punjab, are especially prone to flooding during the monsoon season. Smallholder farmers living in the eight target districts; they apply agricultural production throughout the Indus River Basin. Given the vulnerability of these crops and practices to climate change impacts, as well as their contribution to Pakistan’s economy and food security – there is an urgent need to support farmers to transition away from current baseline approaches and toward climate resilient alternatives.
A Climate Resilient Development Pathway for Indus Basin Agriculture
19. As part of Pakistan's broader effort to adapt to a changing climate, there is a pronounced and urgent need to shift the baseline agriculture and on-farm water management practices that are currently in place towards those that are more resilient to the anticipated impacts of climate change. Given the baseline crops and practices that currently predominate in the Indus Basin, as well as their specific vulnerabilities, an initial set of climate-resilient cropping systems and practices is outlined below (see Section C.3). These will be supported by a suite of technical assistance and capacity building activities to ensure their adoption at scale. The following section outlines 13 Climate Resilient Agriculture in Pakistan - Selected Practices. As outlined in Section C.3 (see sub-component 2.1), the government has identified these practices as those that are most likely to be adopted by farmers, given the types of cropping systems and practices to be supported through the project, ensuring all supported practices are aligned with the overall project objective.
20. Enabling sustained and widespread adoption of climate-adapted agriculture and water management practices throughout the Indus Basin also requires working beyond the farm level. Public sector planning and


---
**Page 16**
---

support is essential to changing behaviour and practices in agriculture. Similarly, public and private providers of extension, advisory, financial and other support services play a key role in shaping patterns of agricultural investment and development. At present, these entities not only face barriers that prevent them from mainstreaming climate change into their activities but also have limited capacity to respond effectively to the challenges posed by climate change on agriculture and water in the Indus Basin are extremely complex and will vary over time. There is also a high degree of vulnerability among smallholder farmers who are least able to adapt to these changes.

Any effort to support widespread adaptation in the Indus Basin must address these challenges. The Theory of Change (ToC) presented below outlines how the project aims to achieve this. It identifies the key actors and stakeholders with the information services, systems and capacity needed to continuously monitor and assess climate change impacts on agriculture and water resources in the Indus Basin. It also highlights the role that can be played by farmers and extension services to build-on farm-resilience. Thus, it will improve the enabling environment to ensure agricultural advisory, financial and support services facilitate the necessary changes in behaviour and practices at farm level.

21. In responding to the above-mentioned needs, the proposed project aims to go beyond achieving a one-off impact to catalyse a broader process of sector transformation in the Indus Basin. It will initiate this process of transformation by addressing the barriers that prevent stakeholders from adapting to climate change impacts and medium-term climate change challenges on the ground, while also equipping government and other stakeholders with the systems, services and capacity needed to sustain and scale up this sector transformation beyond project closure.

**Barriers to adaptation**

22. There are a number of barriers preventing stakeholders in the Indus River Basin from adapting to the climate change impacts and medium-term climate change challenges on agriculture and water resources. Responsibilities for hydrologic information collection, monitoring and communication are spread across various institutions including the Pakistan Meteorological Department (PMD), National Water Resources, Water and Power Development Authority, Indus River System Authority, National Disaster Management Authority, Irrigation and Drainage Department, Federal Board of Revenue, Flood Commission of Pakistan, and Provincial Irrigation Departments. Limited coordination across institutions hampers the ability to provide timely information on climate change impacts and medium-term climate change challenges at the provincial level has historically been difficult to regulate due to outdated policies, limited integration of water monitoring efforts and other institutional barriers. The lack of a coordinated approach to climate change adaptation in the Indus Basin has further impeded the transition toward climate-resilient agriculture and water management in the Indus Basin (as explained in Appendix 17 of the Feasibility Study).

23. Another barrier is the fact that, at the provincial level, agricultural extension suffers from insufficient numbers of personnel for a proven mechanism to reach the vast number of farmers, and a very low ratio of female agriculture extension staff to support women. In addition, current extension personnel possess an inadequate understanding of climate change impacts on agriculture and water resources in the Indus Basin. Extension service providers lack policy-to-practice guidelines and know how for mainstreaming and disseminating the notion that climate change is real and will have significant implications for agriculture and water resources in the Indus Basin to the vulnerable groups of society, who require support specifically tailored to them. Limited mobility of extension staff also hampers their ability to reach farmers.

24. Private sector operators, including input suppliers, traders and financial service providers also face barriers to change. These entities are increasingly aware of growing constraints linked to climate change. They have also shown signs that they are willing to respond. However, market signals for technologies that would facilitate adaptation are not yet clear. Similarly, private sector operators lack the capacity to assess their own vulnerability to climate change impacts and have little knowledge of what to expect in the future and what their options might be. They have limited access to information on climate change and its implications for agriculture and water resources in light of the climate information that is available to them from direct experience or from other stakeholders.

**Complementary baseline projects**

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Green Climate Fund (GCF) Project Proposal

### Assistance Provided by the GCF Project

The proposed GCF project has been designed in consultation with responsible provincial-level government institutions implementing these projects to provide technology and software that will be complementary. It includes:

- **Information resource materials, public services and skills** - which will increase the benefits from those larger scale infrastructure investments.
- **Improvement of water use efficiency through a multi-pronged approach:** (i) The Sindhi Irrigated Agriculture-Piped Water Improvement Project (PIPIP), which is working to improve the productivity of water use in major irrigation canals and distributaries by improving the design, construction, and maintenance of these canals; (ii) Improving the efficiency and effectiveness of irrigation water distribution through key hydraulic works; and (iii) The Sindhi Irrigated Agriculture-Piped Water Improvement Project (PIPIP), which is working to improve the productivity of water use in major irrigation canals and distributaries by improving the design, construction, and maintenance of these canals.

### Project Scope

By supporting stakeholders in the Indus Basin to be more resilient, the proposed project will enable Pakistan to mitigate the negative impacts of climate change that would otherwise undermine agriculture, food security, water availability, and human health. The Indus River is the lifeline for 200 million people living throughout the Indus Basin who remain poorly equipped to cope with the cascading impacts of climate change on the Basin's already complex and sensitive hydrology. Shifts in the timing and amount of rainfall as well as projected increases in temperature, evaporation rates, and salinity levels will have a significant impact on yields and livelihoods.

### C3. Project / Programme Description

#### Component 1: Enhancing Information Services for Climate Change Adaptation in the Water and Agriculture Sectors

The purpose of this component is to develop capacity in Pakistan to use state-of-the-art technology to cope with climate change impacts, including developing a national information system that provides access to and dissemination of information on climate, water, and agriculture. It is aligned with GCF objective A6: Increased generation and use of climate-related information for decision-making.

#### Component 2: Building On-Farm Resilience to Climate Change

The component will support the development of a national framework for building resilience in smallholder farmers characterized by high levels of poverty, food insecurity and malnutrition. The corresponding impacts at the macro level could be significant as the agricultural sector is Pakistan's largest employer (40% of total employment) and climate change will steadily undermine Pakistan's trade position, as well as its overall GDP, agriculture GDP and household income.

#### Component 3: Creating an Enabling Environment for Continued Transformation

The component aims to support the development of a national framework for building government capacity to operate key technologies - those involved in the Activities under sub-components 1.1-1.3, as described below – to enable them to monitor climate change and its impacts on water and agriculture. Importantly, each activity under this component will not only help establish/strengthen key systems and technologies, but will also train about 30 national experts (including from...

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND (FUNDING PROPOSAL), PAGE 12 OF 96

The Agriculture and Irrigation Departments in Punjab and Sindh, BDA, PWRD, PCWRR, and relevant universities/water centers will build their capacity to use/pilot such systems and technologies during and after the six-year project implementation period. Under this Component, technical assistance will be provided to the Professional Beneficiaries through training workshops and field visits conducted by FAO staff and international consultants procured by FAO.

The component is comprised of three sub-components:

### 1: Developing a Water Accounting System

#### Sub-component 1.1: Developing a Water Accounting System

The outcome of this sub-component will be that relevant institutions in Pakistan are using a scientifically sound water accounting system to systematically monitor, predict, and canal system levels that is compliant to international standards and based on satellite technology and ground measurements.

##### Activity 1.1.1: Develop and establish a water accounting system, including the equipment needed to operate this system in two canals

The project will support the development of a water accounting system for the Tareen Canal (TC) and the Kala Chitta Canal (KCC). The TC is located within the Indus River System Authority (IRSA). Four water accounting assessments will be undertaken [two in each province] at provincial level, with technical assistance provided by FAO. This activity is part of the Agriculture Cooperation Programme (TCP) project 1 in Punjab, the Department of Irrigation and, in Smith the Department of Irrigation and Drainage (DID), Pakistan. The TC is located within the Indus River System Authority (IRSA). Four water accounting assessments will be undertaken [two in each province] at provincial level, with technical assistance provided by FAO. This activity is part of the Agriculture Cooperation Programme (TCP) project 1 in Punjab, the Department of Irrigation and, in Smith the Department of Irrigation and Drainage (DID), Pakistan.

##### Activity 1.1.2: Conduct risk-based assessments of aquifer vulnerability, and procure and install automatic groundwater level sensors to ensure continued monitoring

For more proactive aquifer management planning, it is necessary to assess the vulnerability of the aquifers in the Project Area based on hydro-geological mapping and improved monitoring and modeling of the aquifer systems. The project will conduct risk-based assessments of aquifer vulnerability using existing data and new groundwater level sensors in four districts (Khairwad and Dera Gazi Khan in Punjab, and Umer Kot and Sanghar in Sindh) covering the Project Area. This activity is part of the Agriculture Cooperation Programme (TCP) project 1 in Punjab, the Department of Irrigation and Drainage (DID), Pakistan.

The project will also procure and install automatic groundwater level sensors to ensure continued monitoring. The project will engage with relevant national experts (including from the provincial Departments of Agriculture and Irrigation in Punjab and Sindh) to identify suitable locations for installing these sensors. These sensors will be used to deploy in precision-based irrigation techniques and complement aquifer management to reduce pumping intensity and skin thicknesses in aquifers otherwise vulnerable to saline intrusion.

##### Activity 1.1.3: Support the development of local aquifer management plans under Activity 1.1.2, to develop local aquifer management plans in the districts of Khairwad and Dera Gazi Khan in Punjab, and Umer Kot and Sanghar in Sindh

The project will conduct groundwater monitoring and wherewithal farmers will be trained in hydrology monitoring, including providing them with a basic understanding of the principles of groundwater monitoring. The project will also provide training to local water users on how to use the automatic sensors (Aquifer Management Plan Beneficiaries). These Aquifer Management Plan Beneficiaries will be selected by FAO among the farmers who have been trained in hydrology monitoring.

Farmers must be located in close proximity to one of the vulnerable aquifers for which a management plan is developed, and

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Sub-component 1.2: Establishing an Evapotranspiration (ET) based Water Management System

### Activity 1.2.1: Develop and deploy ET monitoring system.

The activity will procure and set up the software applications/tools for planning at project and field levels, and the Departments of Irrigation in Punjab and Sindh. The activity will also calibrate and validate this software using a combination of freely available satellite imagery (30m or 10m resolution) and ground truth data from existing weather stations, including those to be procured and installed under this Activity. The software will generate ET information at basin level and for each command area. This will be done by combining the ET model with satellite images (30m or 10m) in the selected canal command areas (the same canals supported in Activity 1.1.1)—one in each district.

### Activity 1.2.2: Design, develop and build capacity to use ET-based water management software applications/tools for guide planning at project and field levels.

Once the ET monitoring system is fully validated and operational, the activity will provide training on how to use this system and how to set up the ET monitoring data. The project will provide technical assistance for the design and development of the ET-management software applications. It will also provide training to the Departments of Irrigation, as well as Districts of Punjab and Sindh, on how to use the ET-monitoring system. This will be done in collaboration with Water Farmers' Organizations and Water User Associations to use the data generated from the system to undertake water management activities at project and field levels.

### Activity 1.2.3: Strengthen the network of agrometeorological stations in the Project Area by installing new weather stations, upgrading existing ones, and providing training on ET monitoring data, water accounting, and irrigation planning.

The activity will install new weather stations (including lysimeters) to provide real-time data for ET monitoring, water accounting, and irrigation planning. It will also upgrade two existing weather stations (by procuring and installing lysimeters), serving the project districts and improving the current scant coverage for the Basin overall. The locations to install these stations are: Bhakkar, Dera Ismail Khan, Jhelum, Muzaffargarh, Multan, Nankana Sahib, Nowshera, Peshawar, Muzaffargarh, Lodhran, Mutian, Dera Gazi Khan, Khewaian, and Sanghar. Meanwhile, one existing station will be upgraded (i.e., procurement and installation of lysimeters) in each of the following districts: Sindh and Upper Kot.

These agrometeorological stations will be connected to the already existing network of weather and hydro-meteorological stations across the country through the PMD. The information generated by these stations will be shared with stakeholders working on the early warning system, and irrigation advice services.

### Sub-component 1.3: Improving Availability and Use of Information Services

The outcome of this sub-component will be that real-time data and analysis are readily available to the people managing water resources in the Basin. This will ensure that the information, tools, and knowledge made available create climate-related change information in the country is abundant and

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Activity 1.3-1: Train Professional Beneficiaries in the water and agriculture sectors to use IT tools to generate climate change adaptation information for farmers

The project will train professional beneficiaries (PBBs) from the water and agriculture sectors on how to use a series of IT tools that have been developed by FAO and are freely available. These tools include crop suitability maps, soil moisture monitoring systems, and early warning systems with global near-real-time information about drought.

## Activity 1.3-2: Develop an Agri-Climate Water (ACWA) Information Portal

The project will develop a portal that integrates the outputs of the project into one platform using the above-mentioned technologies, display data and results prepared by the project through a user-friendly dashboard. The ACWA portal will be used to disseminate climate change adaptation information for vulnerability assessments, aquifer management, ET monitoring and ET management.

## Activity 1.3-3: Develop a Coordination for Information Management and the ACWA Portal

A coordination mechanism will be set up for governance and collaboration in the operation of the portal, emitting support from various stakeholders including the provincial governments, research institutions such as CIDA, NARC, PRCBWR, CAIR, and key research institutes and universities. Training activities and other knowledge sharing events will be organized to ensure that all users are familiar with the ACWA tools and contents.

## Component 2: Building on-Farm Resilience to Climate Change

The purpose of this component is to build on-farm resilience to climate change by supporting farmers to acquire skills in climate resilient agriculture (CRA) and on-farm water management (OFWM). It will adopt technologies and engage with various stakeholders, including the provincial governments, research institutions such as CIDA, NARC, PRCBWR, CAIR, and key research institutes and universities. Training activities and other knowledge sharing events will be organized to ensure that all users are familiar with the ACWA tools and contents.

### Sub-component 2.1: Improving Practices for Climate Resilience

At project start-up, the project will put in place arrangements with subject matter specialists, research institutes and universities (to be selected by FAO) to form a team to develop training materials on the specific site-based demonstration plots and/or demonstration sites (selected in accordance with the description above) will be established under Activity 2.1.2. ### Activity 2.1.2: Set up demonstration plots and support adaptive research to underpin delivery of climate-informed extension services.

The project will finance the establishment of on-farm demonstration plots selected by farmers in each agro-ecological zone (AEZ) within the Project Area, where they can observe the key technologies on CROA and OFWIM for public use. During the first year, the project will develop 16 demonstration plots on Farmer Beneficiaries' land (with their consent): two per district: one for CBRs and the other for OFWIM. These plots will be established in collaboration with farmers, extension workers, and other stakeholders. In order to sustain the demonstration plots and ensure that circumstances are close to real farming conditions, the project will provide training on how to manage these plots. The plots will be used for training of extension workers under sub-component 2.2. The number of plots will increase each year as more farmers become involved in the project. These plots will represent the key agro-ecological zones (and distinct conditions of these zones) within the Project Area.

### Activity 2.1.3: Develop CROA and OFWIM practice repositories – the Pakistan Climate Agriculture Information System (PC AIS).

Training materials produced under Activity 2.1.2 as well as data and analysis gathered through adaptive research under Activity 2.1.2 will be made available through the ACWA. The project will contribute to the development of a climate change information system for agriculture, known as the Pakistan Climate Agriculture Information System (PC AIS), which will constitute a unit of the ACWA.

### Sub-component 2.2: Training of Trainers on CROA and OFWIM

The project will train skilled professionals working in the Project Area who will be able to promote CROA and OFWIM practices. The project will establish a team of 40 full-time facilitators – 6 in each district of the Project Area, procured by FAO in accordance with its procurement policy. These facilitators will conduct Training of Trainers (ToT) workshops for extension workers and other stakeholders under sub-component 2.1 (see also the Feasibility Study). They will be trained in training methods and CROA and OFWIM. As a result, they will be in a position to train an estimated 300 extension workers directly reaching about 150,000 farmers during the project implementation period.

### Activity 2.1.1: Train the trainers who will lead climate-informed extension services delivery in each district of the Project Area

This Activity will train 48 facilitators (one per district within the Project Area) who will constitute the core facilitators in the Project Area for the duration of this project ("Facilitators"). These facilitators will be trained by two types of trainers: (i) Women Open School (WOS) Facilitators; and (ii) the Women Partnership Facilitators. Under sub-component 2.1, the WOS Facilitators will train the core facilitators in the climate change programme carried out during the first year through establishing and working on the project demonstration plots. The WOS Facilitators will also be trained to deliver training of trainers (ToT) workshops for extension workers and other stakeholders under sub-component 2.1, using CROA, OFWIM, Business FFS, use of ICT, monitoring methods, and others. This staff training will be used to establish the training materials as well as the proposed CROA and OFWIM technologies.

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Activity 2.2: Training of Trainers (ToT) on CRA and OFWM Practices

The project full-time facilitators will conduct ToT for extension officers, staff and/or employees from Partner Organizations (POs) that are interested in collaborating with the project. The POs include government departments (e.g., Agriculture, Forestry, Water Resources), private sector entities (e.g., Farmers' Workers'). Training will be provided to Extension Workers from partner entities only if the participating entities are in a position to provide adequate resources for their participation and if they agree to adopt the knowledge sharing mechanism, the project policy and the implementation procedure. As part of these agreements, the project will provide training on CRA and OFWM practices, as well as on the use of ICS tools, including field visits to demonstration plots. Selection of government extension staff to be trained under this Activity will focus first on the Extension Officers who are already involved in the project (e.g., those working with the WOS). The second group will be sub-component 2.3 (which themselves will be determined during project inception as outlined further above). Such groups may include: i) government departments (e.g., Agriculture, Forestry, Water Resources); ii) NGOs; iii) non-governmental organizations (NGOs): iv) on-going projects; v) universities; vi) research institutes; and/or vii) private sector entities. The training will be conducted in two phases. The first phase of the training will start from the second year after the project is ready to launch with the 48 Facilitators who will serve as the ToT for the extension officers. The second phase of the training will be conducted during the field visits and demonstrations during the project implementation period through agreements that will be made with the project. The aim of this activity is to train a sufficient number of Extension Officers in CRA and OFWM practices, so they can reach out to farmers in their networks. It will also contribute to the sustainability of the training benefits as the people doing the training under agreements with this project will remain in the Project Area and will be able to carry on with the training of other extension officers and/or farmers.

## Sub-component 2.3: Developing Farmers' Capacity to Transform Agricultural Practices with CRA and OFWM

### Outcome of the sub-component
The outcome of this sub-component will be that farmers are skilled in using CRA and OFWM practices suited to their specific farming systems, and they have access to information on climate change adaptation strategies.

### Beneficiaries
Beneficiaries will be directly reached through this structured extension support delivered through Activities 2.1-2.3. In addition, the project will also provide training for a minimum of 400 farmers (at least 50% women) during field visits to these CFBS and WOS sites and demonstration plots through field days organized under Activity 2.3.4 ("Field Days"). The project will also train at least 100 extension officers (at least 50% women) from the POs (Partner Organizations), of whom at least 48 are women (about 24,000 trained through WOS organized under Activity 2.3.2 ("Training of Extension Officers")). The project will also train at least 100 farmers' facilitators (at least 50% women) from the POs (Partner Organizations), who will be responsible for training and mentoring a minimum of 400 farmers (at least 50% women) through CFBS and WOS sites and demonstration plots. The project will also train at least 100 extension officers (at least 50% women) from the POs (Partner Organizations), who will be responsible for training and mentoring a minimum of 400 farmers (at least 50% women) through CFBS and WOS sites and demonstration plots. The project will also train at least 100 extension officers (at least 50% women) from the POs (Partner Organizations), who will be responsible for training and mentoring a minimum of 400 farmers (at least 50% women) through CFBS and WOS sites and demonstration plots.

### Activity 2.3.1: Train and Support Farmer Beneficiaries to Adopt CRA and OFWM Through Climate and Rainfall Information Systems (CRS)

The project will adopt the FFS approach. CRBSs will be set up using a participatory adult learning methodology where participants will observe weather, soil characteristics, plant development, costs and yield. The project will also provide training on climate change adaptation strategies, including improved farm management and marketing skills, nursery and financial literacy, adopting FAO's methodology of on-ground deduction, observation and analysis. The project will also train farmers in the use of ICS tools. The project will also introduce participants to private sector partners that are providing services that can help them take advantage of climate change adaptation strategies. This includes training on the selection and use of seeds needed for climate change adaptations, where FAO also has other on-going initiatives. To facilitate adoption of CRA and OFWM practices, the project will provide a seedling nursery service as well as a seedling service. Initially, the CRBSs will be implemented by project staff, but from the third year onwards, more and more CRBSs will be run by farmer-facilitators under the supervision of project staff. ### Activity 2.3.2: Train and Support Women Farmer Beneficiaries to adopt CRA and OFRM through Women Open Schools (WOS)

As cultural norms make it difficult for women to train men and men together, an approach to receive training is through Women Open Schools. These schools have a long history of working with women in Pakistan. Using their network and existing human resources, the project will establish 10 WOSs in each of the 24 districts where the project is participating women. Through this activity, the project will directly train approximately 24,000 Women Farmer Beneficiaries (WFB) through these schools. The training will be conducted by the WOS teachers and will focus on agricultural activities, with a strong focus on improving resilience by raising income and family health through animal husbandry, vegetable production and fruit trees.

The project will support one home garden per WOS, providing initial inputs such as seeds, compost and small livestock such as chickens and goats.

### Activity 2.3.1: Training of Farmer Beneficiaries on CRA and OFRM practices by partners on their demonstration plots

As farmer beneficiaries in the Indus Basin have not yet been trained on these two components, this activity will be open to all organizations, including government extension departments, non-governmental organizations (NGOs), private sector companies, and other civil society organizations working with the project. Partners will be given training on CRA and OFRM, under sub-component 2.2. The project will provide training materials and resources for partners to use in their own training sessions. Partners will also receive a copy of the Field Day Farmers’ Handbook (see weekly) training sessions on their plots. Prior to engaging in activities, the project and partner organizations will sign an agreement that outlines the responsibilities of each party.

### Activity 2.3.4: Organize field days for additional farmers to visit CRA/OFM and demonstration plots

To saturate the use of CRA and OFRM practices (‘Field Day Farmer Participants’), information on the extension activities will be included in the mass media and mobile phone-based campaigns under sub-component 1.2. Field Day Farmers will also be invited to participate in these events, which will reach out beyond target areas following regular training and will invite Field Day Farmer Participants in the vicinity to visit the CRA/OFRM demonstration plots at their respective project sites through a pre-existing ongoing system. It is estimated that another 148,000 Field Day Farmer Participants, of whom at least 24,000 will be Women Field Day Farmer Participants (invited to visit WOSs), will be reached through such occasional visits.

## Component 3: Creating an Enabling Environment for Continued Transformation

The purpose of this component is to create a wider enabling environment for the sustained uptake of climate resilient practices in the Indus Basin. This will be achieved by strengthening institutional and regulatory systems in the Indus Basin agriculture to a progressively more climate-resilient pathway in the future.

### Sub-component 3.1: Improving Information and Awareness Raising Campaigns

The project will run information and awareness raising campaigns on the impact of climate change in the Indus Basin region, with an emphasis on the need for adaptation. A multi-stakeholder approach campaign will be developed at federal, provincial and district level with a mix of communication channels.

### Sub-component 3.2: Strengthening Institutional and Regulatory Systems by Federal and Provincial Governments

## Activity 3.1.1: Organize and launch a multimedia information and awareness raising campaign on climate change, CRA and OFVM

This activity will start with multimedia content creation. This will include the preparation of audio-visual materials such as videos, animations, infographics, posters, print media, SMS and voice and audio dramas on key themes. These will be coupled with SMS and voice messages for knowledge dissemination to farmers in the Project Area.

The targeted mobile phone campaign using SMS and voice messages will also be linked to the demonstration plots implemented under Component 2. These will be used in the radio campaigns as well as by extension workers during face-to-face sessions, SMS and voice messages, as well as audio programmes are also used to lead farmers to other information services.

To enable providers of phone-based information and advisory services to include more content related to climate change, actionable information will be provided user-oriented displays and formats. This will include information from the ACWA under Component 1, proven climate resilient agricultural practices from Component 2, and activities generated by the upgraded agro-met and flux instruments and ET monitoring in Component 1. It will be used to improve the quality of services offered through mobile phones and other ICT platforms. The service providers also include updates on project activities – including invitations to farmers to visit demonstration plots in their vicinity. Further, it is envisaged that the information provided by the service providers will be more relevant for farmers on climate resilient practices to improve their technical advice to the farmers. Youth in the villages will be mobilized as volunteers to disseminate this information through door-to-door visits and other means using mobile and ICT platforms. Links with the CABI Plantwise knowledge bank and plant doctors will also be established.

## Sub-component 3.2: Supporting Policy Implementation by Federal and Provincial Governments

41. This sub-component will support policymakers to make use of project results and data from Component 1 for evidence-based decision making to support the transition of the agriculture sector in the Indus Basin toward the necessary climate-resilient agricultural practices. The activities under this sub-component are aligned with the Pakistan Climate Change Policy, approved in 2018 into operational policy and procedures, and thus also respond to the existing National Climate Change Plan (NCCP). This sub-component will support the implementation of the Pakistan Climate Change Policy and its associated action plan. It will facilitate the strengthening of institutional arrangements needed to: (a) upscale beyond the Project Area; (b) ensure a more coherent and responsive development planning, and (c) build capacity for evidence-based decision making.

42. This sub-component will contribute to the realisation of GCF outcomes A5.0 Strengthened government institutional and regulatory frameworks for climate-responsive development planning, and A6.0 Increased generation and use of climate information in decision-making.

## Activity 3.2.1: Increasing Collaboration between Institutions through Knowledge Sharing for Evidence-based Policymaking

The project will facilitate collaboration among relevant government departments working on water resources management and agriculture to ensure that the agricultural sector is better integrated with other sectors, thereby greater collaboration between different departments and wings that are necessary for adequate policy responses. The project will also support knowledge sharing and harmonisation of activities across the Ministry of Agriculture, Department of Irrigation, Livestock and Women’s Development in each province, including support in mainstreaming climate change adaptation into policies and plans. This will be done by building bridges between Pakistan's policies and objectives on climate change issues. This will include knowledge sharing, work planning and harmonisation of activities across the Ministry of Agriculture, Department of Irrigation, Livestock and Women’s Development in each province.

43. The project will also support knowledge sharing among provincial agriculture departments to ensure harmonisation of objectives and activities on climate change adaptation and steps to promote resilience-focused agricultural practices. This will be done by building bridges between Pakistan's policies and objectives of the Provincial Departments of Agriculture. The project will connect the relevant government departments with organisations working in the field of water resources management, irrigation and agriculture so that they can benefit from their knowledge and better respond to their needs.

This activity will support evidence-based policymaking for climate change adaptation and assist policymakers to use the elements of the ACWA Portal developed under Component 1. Under this activity, the project will share analytical findings with UNDP and the staff working on the GLOF Project, and with the World Bank and WFP. The project will also share analytical findings with the Ministry of Agriculture, Irrigation and Drainage as Outbound Flood Risk Reduction Phase II project financed by GCF (GLOF Project), and with the World Bank and WFP.

In the context of knowledge sharing, the project will facilitate discussions on approaches to water charges based on the water accounting system and actual water consumed. A review of subsidies and price support programmes will ensure that they are aligned with the principles of cost recovery and sustainability.

## Activity 3.2.2: Mainstreaming Climate Resilient Agriculture and Water Management across All Policy Areas

A special effort will be made to further mainstream policies in support of climate-resilient agriculture across a wider range of policy areas, including those related to food security, trade, environment, land use, etc., by building collaboration amongst government institutions and partners working with the project, and to share information on best practices. The project will also facilitate discussions among policymakers at the national level in the climate policy forum to feed into directly existing budgeting and/or reform processes (measures to be held annually). This will include a review of institutional arrangements for mainstreaming climate-resilient agriculture by the ministries to identify opportunities for modifying managerial functions, working structures, norms, job descriptions, institutional configuration, personnel and financial assets, to better address climate-resilient agriculture. Furthermore, this activity will support the development of policy briefs on climate change adaptation policies and laws (not just agriculture and water policy but also policies related to food security, environment, trade, etc.) and consider how these can be mainstreamed into other policy areas.

1 This project will share analytical findings with UNDP and the staff working on the GLOF Project. Beyond this, additional linkages are not foreseen; as the two projects respond directly to different climate change challenges in different geographic areas.

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Sub-component 3.3: Developing Services that Enable Farmers to Adopt Climate Resilient Practices

### Activity 3.3.1: Develop a searchable online database (and associated application and map) of locally available financial and value chain services to support adoption and sustained use of CRA and QFPM practices

This activity will develop an online database, application, and map that provides farmers with access to the agricultural sector for uses that can improve on-farm resilience to climate change in the Project Area. The project will also conduct financial and value-chain literacy training under the CBF 5 component (see Activity 3.1) and provide technical assistance, the use of ACWA and tools (Activity 1.3.1), and knowledge sharing to them with findings from sub-component 2.1 and the data collected under sub-component 2.1 in addition, the project will train non-farm households on how to access credit and insurance services. The project will also collect the mentioned data and analyze into their credit risk for farmers who use project-promoted CRA and QFPM practices.

### Activity 3.3.2: Support financial service providers to access and apply climate information and knowledge of CRA and QFPM practices

The project will provide training on climate change adaptation, including the use of climate information, to financial service providers in an effort to make them more responsive to the needs of farmers facing the threats of climate change. The project will also conduct a baseline survey of financial service providers in the Bank of Pakistan in accordance with the following criteria: (1) their presence in the Project Area (based on mapping done under sub-component 2.1), and (2) their willingness to provide technical assistance, the use of ACWA and tools (Activity 1.3.1) and knowledge sharing to them with findings from sub-component 2.1 as well as the data collected under sub-component 2.1 in addition, the project will train non-farm households on how to access credit and insurance services. The project will also collect the mentioned data and analyze into their credit risk for farmers who use project-promoted CRA and QFPM practices.

### Activity 3.3.3: Train and support local companies, young people and women to deliver services to farmers to facilitate adaptation to climate change

The project will also take steps to foster the development of new insurance products that are tailored to meet the needs of smallholder farmers in the Project Area. The project will work with local companies, processors and other buyers to enable them to adapt their product range or their sourcing strategies to better match the changing climate conditions. This activity will also support young people and practices have access to appropriate inputs and still find buyers. The project will prioritize working with locally.

*In Pakistan, the administrative structure is as follows: each province and territory are sub-divided into multiple divisions, which themselves are sub-divided into districts. Each district comprises multiple tehsils/tehsils, while each tehsil/tehsil comprises multiple union councils.*

#### Project Management

43. The purpose of this work is to ensure the smooth implementation of all investments and activities undertaken in connection with the Project. This includes ensuring that all investments are implemented for which they were intended. To achieve this, a central Project Management Unit (PMU) will be set up in Islamabad. Provincial Project Implementation Units (PPIUs) will be set up in each Province, and District Project Implementation Units (DPIUs) will be established at the district level to ensure that all project activities are implemented on time and reporting. The PMU will prepare the project Annual Work Plan and Budget (AWPB) for clearance by a Project Oversight Committee (POC). The POC will review and approve the AWPB, which will then be submitted annually basis.

These will feed into the Annual Performance Reports and financial management reports that FAO (as contracted under this Project) will submit to the Government of Pakistan. The PMU will also procure services, equipment and materials required for project implementation. PMU procurement activities will also include ensuring that all investments are implemented in accordance with the approved AWPB. The PMU will also implement a project monitoring system to monitor planned outputs, outcomes and objectives based on the project Logic Framework (LF). An Information Management System (IMS) will be created under this component for use by the PMU, PPIUs, and DPIUs to track and compile information on project implementation, physical and financial progress, and outcomes.

### Farmer Beneficiaries (Activities under sub-component 2.3)

Farmers who will directly benefit from project training/support ("Farmer Beneficiaries" under sub-component 2.3) collectively comprise a large proportion of the project's direct beneficiaries, as outlined in Section E.1 of the Project Document. The selection process for these Farmer Beneficiaries is detailed below.

**Selection Criteria:**

- Farmers must be willing to participate in weekly sessions for the duration of the training (e.g., 20 weeks) and be able to commit time limited to a single crop).
- Farmers should be willing to work in a group, and share knowledge with their neighbors.

In the case of Women Open Schools organized under Activity 2.3.2 (which will exclusively benefit women), the project will also prioritize the selection of women from women-headed households in addition to applying the above criteria.


### Youth Beneficiaries (Activity 3.3.3)

Youth (15-29 years old, in accordance with government definitions) who will directly benefit from project training/support under Activity 3.3.3 will be selected by FAO according to the following criteria ("Youth Beneficiaries"):

- Youth must apply for available spots on each training.
- Project staff and training facilitators will select candidates based on the alignment between their profiles and the trainings to be provided (i.e., relevance of their past training (if any), skills, and experience).

## C.4. Background Information on Project Sponsor (Executing Entity)

### 45. FAO was created as a specialized UN agency in 1947, at which time Pakistan was a founding Member State. It supports the Government of Pakistan to achieve its development goals by implementing projects and programmes covering 20 projects throughout the country, with an annual budget of US$ 20 million. In more than 70 years since FAO was established, it has supported the Government of Pakistan in achieving its development goals through various projects. It has administrative staff including administration; office support, information resources and programmes who provide backstopping, support and supervision to the administrative staff of individual projects.

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## FAO Country Office in Pakistan

### Staffing and Partnerships

The FAO country office in Pakistan is staffed with 116 technical officers in a wide range of fields. Subject matter specialists in countries that can provide expertise and materials pertinent to this project include those from natural resource management, water resources, agriculture, fisheries, forestry, and rural development.

Furthermore, FAO has project offices and national staff currently located in Peshawar, Quetta, Karachi, Multan, Badin, Chaghi, and Gilgit. The contact details for these offices are provided in Annex 2 of the Project Document, which is included as an attachment to this document.

The relationships of the FAO Country Office with federal and provincial governments, development partners, and civil society organizations can be put to use in the implementation of this project and in securing continuity and sustainability of its results. The country office has a strong network of contacts at all levels of government.

### Regional and Technical Staff

FAO has technical specialists based in its headquarters (HQ) in Rome and Regional Office for Asia and the Pacific (ROAP). Technical staff members at HQ and ROAP will be able to reinforce its regional and national offices with expertise on climate change adaptation, water resources management, and food security. The FAO team is currently working with its Member States to design countries' understanding of the implications and options available under the Green Climate Fund (GCF) for their specific needs in Pakistan.

The GCF has a strong focus on agriculture and rural development, which are key sectors in Pakistan. The GCF can offer an EE, so too can the technology applied in the Pakistan Indus Basin under this project be available as an option to countries that wish to access the GCF. FAO will also be able to provide technical support for the GCF regional initiatives operational in Pakistan on food security and nutrition, namely the Asia and Pacific Zero Hunger Challenge (ZHC) initiative, which is consistent with which there are direct synergies in the planned awareness raising and household-level food security activities.

### Knowledge Assets

Among FAO's many knowledge assets developed for applications in the context of adaptation to climate change that have already been incorporated in the design of this project in Pakistan are ASAT and MOSAIC. Planners at the Provincial Agriculture Departments (PADs) will be trained on these tools as part of their training under the GCF. PARC, NARC, and PCW/R will be trained on these tools in the context of policy support activities. Other assets include, for example, the Pakistan Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan (PCCSAP), which is being developed with the Climate Initiative of the German Ministry for Environment, to combine very high spatial resolution with very high temporal resolution imagery for assessment of land-use changes.

### Global Farmer Field School

This project will also benefit from the Global Farmer Field School Platform launched by FAO in early 2018. In collaboration with the Pakistan Agricultural Research Council (PARC), a network of practitioners and organizations using Farmer Field Schools, an element proposed here to achieve the intended GCF outcome of Strengthened adaptive capacity and enhanced resilience of smallholder farmers in Pakistan is the establishment of a national farmer field school platform as a participatory approach pioneered by FAO in 1989 for integrated pest management in rice production in Asia. It is now recognized that this approach can be used to address other issues, including climate change adaptation.

The GFFS Platform will be established with the support of international and national research institutions and private sector stakeholders in Pakistan that are connected with the Global Farmer Field School Platform, ensuring that it is tailored for the local context before customizing its application within the country in the service of climate change adaptation in the Indus Basin.

### Engagement with Stakeholders

FAO has also closely engaged with development partner stakeholders in Pakistan working in agriculture and water management. The project will benefit from their insights to ensure that the proposed activities are relevant for adaptation and sustainable development, as well as investment opportunities. Among these partners, including ADIS, CDAI, DFGZ, IFPRR; and the World Bank, FAO has been most closely aligned with CIAT, IFPRI and the World Bank in this project in the context of the Global Water Partnership (GWP) Pakistan. The GWP Pakistan is a multi-stakeholder platform for dialogue in connection with the design and implementation of World Bank projects under the Cooperative Programme between the World Bank and FAO. The GWP Pakistan has been instrumental in supporting the development of this project.

The Government and the World Bank are eager that the outputs of this project be made available to them so that they may be used as inputs for their own climate change adaptation initiatives.

## C.7. Institutional / Implementation Arrangements

52. FAO will serve as both the Accredited Entity (AE) and Executing Entity (EE) for this project with a set-up that supports the implementation of the project in all three provinces of Pakistan. The Government of Pakistan will be the implementing entity for the project. The government and FAO will jointly form the project implementation units at three levels: (i) the Project Management Unit (PMU), (ii) Provincial Project Implementation Units (PPIUs), and (iii) District Project Implementation Units (DPIUs). These implementation units will comprise both government staff members and project-related staff. FAO will also provide technical and administrative support to the government and these project implementation units.

* **High technical standards are adhered throughout project implementation:** Due to the highly technical nature of the project activities, the project will need to avail itself of FAO’s in-country expertise to undertake day-to-day project management tasks. This will be achieved through a dedicated team of FAO staff who will work directly with the government and other stakeholders to support project delivery. The PMU will also provide direct oversight on the quality of technical monitoring, as well as the timeliness and quality of reporting to the GCF.

* **Project delivery can proceed efficiently despite the complex governance framework:** Under the post-devolution national governance structures in Pakistan, provincial governments are responsible for agricultural development in most parts of the country. This means that the project will be implemented across three provinces, which also means working with three separate governments. Each of these governments will also be represented by multiple ministries and departments. The PMU will work closely with all relevant stakeholders to establish a single delivery team that is anchored in FAO-Pakistan to lead project implementation—an arrangement that the Government of Pakistan has explicitly requested.

* **Government partners still play a leading role in project delivery and capacity development:** The PMU, PPIUs and DPIUs will be responsible for ensuring that Responsible Entities and Implementation Partners deliver project support, achieve results and ensure benefit streams are sustained beyond project closure. In so doing, the project will build the capacity of government staff to manage projects independently and effectively.

54. To perform these Accredited Entity functions, FAO will set up a dedicated FAO-GCF project supervision team comprising a Project Director (PD) based in Islamabad, Pakistan; a Supervision Team Leader (STL) based in Bangkok, Thailand; and GCF Headquarters in Rome. The project supervision team will remain independent of the Executing Entities but also perform all functions as per GCF (see below). In line with the GCF policy on access adopted through GCF Board Decision 18/09, FAO will ensure that the project supervision team is able to operate independently and effectively to perform the types of Accredited Entity functions listed in the GCF General principles and associated policies. The project supervision team will be supervised by the GCF Secretariat in accordance with GCF Board decision 18/09.

Executing Entity Role

53. As the designated Executing Entity, FAO will be responsible for the overall management of this project, including: (i) all aspects of project appraisal; (ii) administrative, financial and technical oversight and supervision throughout project implementation; (iii) ensuring that the project is delivered in accordance with the approved GCF proposal, as well as the quality of project monitoring, as well as the timeliness and quality of reporting to the GCF; (iv) project closure and disbursement of funds in accordance with the detailed provisions outlined in the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) between FAO and GCF.

#### FAO Pakistan will bear the overall responsibility for fulfilling the Executing Entity Functions in this project.

This PMU will coordinate the work of two PPRUs and eight DPPUs, all of which will include a combination of project-execution functions and financial management functions. The PMU will also be responsible for the preparation of the Project Implementation Plan (PIP) on this project, including (inter alia) preparation of annual work plans and budgets (AWPBs) in collaboration with key implementing partners. The PMU will also be responsible for the overall supervision of the project implementation.

The PMU will be supported by a Project Office (PO), which is described in Section 4 of this document, and in Section 5 of the Feasibility Study. Along with specialised GCF technical experts who will directly backstop the team, the PO will also include a Project Director (PD) to lead the project implementation.

This project delivery team will lead the execution of all activities included in this project using the GCF grant funding and any co-financing provided by other donors. The PMU will be responsible for ensuring that the project is implemented within the approved budget, including financial management and procurement. FAO will ensure that there is no direct overlap between (i) the staff who comprise the project implementation team and (ii) the staff who comprise the project supervision team and full FAO's Accredited Entity functions.

This will ensure build upon project oversight and supervisory functions.

### Project Governance Structure

The governance structure of the project will be as follows:

* POC, which will be chaired by the Government of Pakistan (GoP), and co-chaired by FAO. The Secretary MiCCD will act as the Chairperson of the Committee.
* The Committee will be accountable to the Government of Pakistan and the FAO project supervision team for ensuring that the project is implemented in accordance with the approved PIP, including financial management and procurement.

### Provincial Level Governance Structure

For each province, a PTTC will be put in place as a Provincial Project Technical Committee (PTTC) to oversee and monitor the implementation of the project activities in its province. The respective PTTCs will be chaired by the Planning and Development Department (PDD), with representation from other provincial government departments.

The primary functions of the Provincial Project Technical Committees will be as follows:

* Ensuring coordination of the project amongst federal government partners, provincial government partners, and provincial stakeholders.
* Ensuring that the project is implemented in accordance with the approved PIP at the provincial level; and
* Reviewing AWPBs and reviewing project progress at the provincial level.

The Federal MiCCD, FAO, and GCF will be represented on the PTTC. The Chair of each PTTC will have the option of inviting other experts as the need arises. The PTTC will also have the option to invite other experts as the need arises. Minutes of PTTC meetings will be made publicly available and circulated to all Committee members and all other project stakeholders.

### Project Implementation Arrangements

The project implementation arrangements described in more detail below) will ensure support is delivered in close collaboration with key federal, provincial and district entities, which are regarded as 'Responsible Entities' (i.e. government institutions) that are vital to the effective delivery and sustainability of this project. Each project activity is owned by one or more Responsible Entities throughout project implementation; these Responsible Entities will work in a manner that is complementary to the project.

### PROJECT GOVERNANCE AND IMPLEMENTATION STRUCTURE

#### Project Management Unit (PMU)
- A PMU will be established in Islamabad to be located at the FAO Country Office.
- The PMU will be headed by a full-time project-recruited Project Coordinator who will be responsible for project planning and coordination with all stakeholders, including the Government of Pakistan, donors, implementing partners, and other stakeholders.
- The PMU will also execute activities at federal level (in national policy formulation, advocacy, etc.).
- The government counterpart to the Project Coordinator will be the (part-time) Project Director, to be appointed by the MoCC and recruited through a separate process.

- In each province, there are two PPUs that will serve as operational arms of the PMU.

- Under the PPUs there will be a DPU for each of the eight districts. These will be the operational arm of the project for district-level activities.
