- The project will recruit six extension workers in each county to support the DPU. These extension workers will be selected to serve as the District Project Coordinator tasked with leading field activities at district level.

### D.1. Value Added for GCF Involvement

The prospect of GCF financing has already been catalytic in stimulating the NDA and MoCC to Pakistan with FAD to look into how climate change scenarios are going to affect the agricultural sector and what the implications will be for the country. The project is expected to mobilize more than 100 million USD, which is a significant amount of money at all levels. The prospect of GCF support has also led both FAD and MoCC to contribute more than USD 500 000 each towards the cost of this project. This has allowed for the development of a detailed work plan and budget for the design of this project. This has culminated in a commitment by the Provincial Governments of Punjab and Sindh to allow for the mobilization of resources from their respective budgets, which would not have been possible without the GCF support. The provincial governments have also committed to allowing for the mobilization of resources from these stakeholders into a programme with the large outreach and potential impact that this project will have.

With the investment of a critical mass of resources coming from GCF, federal and provincial governments and FAD, this investment in public goods will further catalyse widespread household-level investments in adaptation across all areas of the Basin. The expected results of this project are as follows: improved access to information on climate change; increased use of information services; increased knowledge, governance capacity and trust that the grass roots level have the potential to mobilize investments in resilience building activities by households. MoCo Finance has also committed to providing USD 10 million for the design phase.

It is important to note that GCF will add value to this project that would be difficult for the Government to match on its entirety for two main reasons. The first is because of the low level of human development in the Indus Basin and the relatively small size of the population living there, which makes it difficult for the provincial governments to make budgetary arrangements for public sector investment between the provincial and federal levels. Investments in the project will be made at a national level where the most efficient use can be made by the central government. The second reason is because of the limited capacity that exists within the provincial governments to provide services by investing in physical and human capital in one central location for information services that can serve the entire province. This is because the current structure of service provision does not allow for the delivery of project-related services at the provinces or facilitate pooling of resources for activities. GCF’s support to this project will work to turn this constraint into an opportunity to provide evidence of the usefulness of selected federal government initiatives that provide services across more administrative units and to mobilize increases in budgetary allocations for needed services in the future.

### D.2. Exit Strategy

The exit strategy for this project consists of elements designed to result in the sustainability of selected outcomes upon completion of the project. These include:

- Improving Knowledge Skills and Competencies on the Job: The project includes a series of training and capacity development activities for men and women who are government officials, professional staff, extension workers, farmers, and other stakeholders involved with the project. The project will also make explicit efforts to include people from a segment of society that is vulnerable to climate change such as poor, women and minority groups, to build skills and knowledge that will continue to be applied after project closure.
- Capacity Development of Local Entities for Sustained Interactions with Target Farmers: The project has proposed the identification of suitable local agencies as implementing partners in introducing and applying climate-smart agricultural practices. By building on the existing capacity of these agencies and by strengthening the capacity of these local agencies, the project will enable them to continue supporting and interacting with target farmers after the project is completed. This will ensure that the knowledge and skills acquired by the farmers need to continue to communicate about the climate resilient practices after the termination of the project. As an example, the project proposes to work closely with the Pakistan Agricultural Research Council (PARC) in working with vulnerable farmers to sustain services and other forms of support after project closure.

# RATIONALE FOR GCF INVOLVEMENT

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 31 OF 86

### Incremental Costs for New Services Assumed by Government during Implementation
All activities financed with GCF funds, commencing new services will be set up together with staff in the institutions that will carry on their implementation after project closure. These mostly revolve around services for measurement and scoring of crops, training of farmers, and other technical assistance to farmers. The provincial governments of both Punjab and Sindh have already indicated the availability of resources that can be provided by them to assist in the scaling up of these new services. Therefore, the project will work with assistant responsible institutions in order to ensure that the incremental costs are covered within the framework presented in revised budget requirements to provincial authorities to cover these costs after project completion.

### Ensuring Good Financial Returns for Producers
Drawing from tested technologies that are being developed in Pakistan with technical assistance from FAO and others, this project will demonstrate CRA and OFWFM practices. These practices will be demonstrated on a pilot basis at the farm level. The project will also ensure that the prospects of increased income rather than avoiding losses is in a without project scenario.

### Sustainable Learning Impacts through the Creation of an Enabling Environment
Technology transfer is not enough to ensure adoption of new technologies and practices by farmers. They need to be supported by other services, such as financial, value-chain and quality inputs service providers. The proposed project envisages that these services will be provided in a coordinated manner within the framework of the project. This approach creates an enabling environment for farmers to adopt CRA and OFWFM practices. As shown in Figure 2, the learning environment is not only created by the project but also complemented by other supporting services which will become a part of the daily enabling environment.

### Figure 2: Schematic Representation of the Enabling Environment

| Component | Description |
| --- | --- |
| **Farmer** | The farmer is at the center of the enabling environment. |
| **Farmer’s family and community** | This includes family members, friends, neighbors, and other social networks that provide support to farmers in their daily lives. |
| **Farmer’s inputs and services provider** | This includes service providers such as input suppliers, credit agencies, and extension workers who provide the necessary inputs and services to farmers. |
| **Farmer’s outputs and market access provider** | This includes service providers such as marketing agents, processors, and traders who help farmers sell their products in the market. |
| **Farmer’s information and communication technology (ICT) provider** | This includes service providers such as internet service providers, mobile phone operators, and other ICT service providers who provide the necessary infrastructure for farmers to access information and communicate with each other. |

### Directly involving the private sector
Collaboration with the private sector is part of this project's strategy to forge relationships with producers, who will continue to access inputs and services needed for improved practices after project completion. The private sector will also provide technical assistance in building business relationships that will continue after project completion as much as they effectively serve the interests of concerned farmers. This will be achieved through a partnership model where the private sector will train farmers of good farming practices for increased and higher quality outputs. On the farmer side of the partnership model, farmers will pay a fee to access inputs and services from the private sector.

# RATIONALE FOR GCF INVOLVEMENT

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 32 OF 86

### Information and Communication Technology (ICT) in Awareness Raising and Extension.

One traditional mode of transferring knowledge to producers has been extension by field workers. However, the benefits of this approach are limited due to the physical limitations of field workers reaching only a small percentage of the vast number of farmers. In order to overcome this physical limitation, "e-agriculture" or "ICT in agriculture," will be promoted as an alternative mode of transferring knowledge and information. E-agriculture is considered to have considerable potential in offering a wide range of solutions to agricultural challenges (World Bank, 2017; FAO, 2014). It can provide farmers with access to a wide range of information through the use of modern and up-to-date state-of-the-art digital technology. Several different types of ICTs will assist a wide range of stakeholders with sharing knowledge and information, building relationships and undertaking transactions (see Appendix 15 of the Feasibility Study), which will ensure sustainable communication after project closure.

# E.1 Impact Potential

## E.1.1 Key project impact potential

The project impact at the household level will increase the resilience of men and women's livelihoods. At project completion, approximately 200,000 households (638,000 people) in rural areas will have benefited from the use of new adaptive technologies and practices through direct interaction with the project. This constitutes approximately 8 percent of the total population of rural households in the eight districts covered by the project.

The majority of direct support and hands-on activities will have been targeted. These people are counted as direct beneficiaries, while those who benefit indirectly from these activities are counted as indirect beneficiaries, including field visits and demonstrations. An additional 10 million people living in rural households in the eight districts covered by the project will be indirectly benefited through increased agricultural production, processing and input trade, from greater knowledge about weather and climate, and greater access to markets for their produce. This constitutes approximately 45 percent of the total population of rural households in the eight project districts.

Furthermore, provincial government institutions that work with these rural people, and local businesses and organizations that provide better services as a result of enhanced information and skills that they have acquired through the project will also be indirectly benefited.

It is estimated that a total of around 250,000 farmers will benefit directly from the project (201,600 male and 48,900 female farmers). At an average family size of six.3, this means that the project will reach 200,000 households. With an average family size of 6.3, the number of total beneficiaries is around 1.3 million.

### Other relevant indicators
- Strengthened institutional and regulatory systems for climate-responsive planning development.
- Increased generation and use of climate information in decision-making.
- Improved access to climate data, tools, and services.
- Strengthened awareness of climate change trends and risk reduction processes.

#### Baseline survey
A baseline survey will be implemented under the supervision of the project monitoring specialists in project year 1 on order to quantify the actual levels of resilience, vulnerability, food security, awareness and adaptive capacity. This will allow for a comparison with the expected outcomes at the end of the project period. The impact of the project will be measured on the following core impact outcomes and expectations from the use of GCF resources:

A0.1 Increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods of the most vulnerable people, communities and regions.
A0.2 Increased resilience of health and well-being and food security.
A0.3 Strengthened adaptive capacity and reduced exposure to climate risks.
A0.4 Strengthened institutional and regulatory systems for climate-responsive planning development.
A0.5 Strengthened awareness of climate change trends and risk reduction processes.

#### Project monitoring specialists
The project monitoring specialists, together with subject matter specialists and the international TA team, will also implement a qualitative baseline study of institutions, specifically of PMD, IRSA, the Department of Irrigation Punjab, and other relevant government agencies. This will be done in collaboration with the local communities and selected Water User Associations in each of the project districts. It will allow the project to document the degree of achievement of the outcomes being sought by GCF terms of:

A0.5 Strengthened institutional and regulatory systems for climate-responsive planning development.
A0.6 Increased generation and use of climate information in decision-making.

### E.Z. Paradigm Shift Potential
Degree to which the proposed activity can catalyze impact beyond one-off project/programme investment

#### Project will shift Pakistan and Indus Basin agriculture from its current situation of high vulnerability toward an alternative paradigm wherein better information and farming practices will significantly increase resilience to climate change, and transform the agricultural sector in a manner that is more sustainable and resilient than it currently is. The Indus Basin.
Changing climatic conditions will require changes in practices across the agricultural sector, which employs 25 million people directly or indirectly. This transformation of agriculture in Pakistan’s Indus Basin is an enormous challenge, and limited educational background—is an enormous task that the Government cannot achieve on its own. External support is essential to ensure that the necessary knowledge and skills are available for farmers to make informed decisions as their agricultural output is produced—and its vulnerability to climate change make this process of transformation an absolute necessity.

#### Given the scale of this endeavour, the project alone cannot transform Indus Basin agriculture.
It will rather set this transformational process in motion, and equip government, farmers and other stakeholders to continue driving this process. The project will catalyze impact beyond the project investments and project closure. There are four primary ways in which the project will do this:

a. The project will establish cutting-edge systems and build capacities to generate, disseminate and utilize vital data on climate change impacts and adaptation strategies for agriculture in Pakistan’s Indus Basin.
The project assigns particular importance to enabling accurate measurement, monitoring and modelling of hydrologic systems in the Indus Basin, taking into account the impacts of climate change. In so doing, the project will equip farmers with the knowledge they need to make informed decisions about their farming practices and land management decisions well into the future. This is particularly important in the context of Indus Basin

# EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 50 OF 86

### b. The project will strengthen the extension system in the Indus Basin to ensure it is better able to support adoption of locally appropriate Climate Resilient Agriculture (CRA) and On-Farm Water Management (OFWM) practices by a critical mass of farmers, including smallholder farmers, women-headed households, marginal farmers, farmer organizations and partner projects, all of whom play a key role in delivering extension services in Punjab and Sindh. The project will also ensure that the extension system is better able to provide access to information and relevant training materials, to enable them to promote adoption of CRA and OFWM practices at a considerable scale within the eight targeted districts. To achieve this, the project will:

- Ensure extension service providers have access to new information – including that which is generated under Component 3 (see below) – and relevant training materials.
- Also ensure extension service providers have access to new information – including that which is generated under Component 3 (see below) – and relevant training materials.

The project will not only strengthen these technical elements of the extension system, but also improve its overall effectiveness by ensuring it is better able to reach remote areas in Punjab and Sindh. The project will do this through a combination of improved access and literacy. And the extension directorates within the Departments of Agriculture in Punjab and Sindh – key project partners – will be strengthened with new staff, equipment and resources so that they can provide more informed extension services to reach additional districts beyond the eight targeted by this project.

### c. The project will support adoption of CRA and OFWM practices among a critical mass of farmers, which will further increase local awareness of – and the demand for – these climate resilient approaches to farming. The project will do so through a combination of:

- A series of pilot projects in each of the eight districts.
- Collaboration with public and private sector actors who work directly with those producers.

In this way, the project will generate new information on how best to promote CRA and OFWM practices at scale within local communities. These positive results and benefits will be further promoted through word-of-mouth communication among farmers and other stakeholders in each of the eight districts, as well as through campaigns organized as part of the project’s agriculture strategy (Component 3). The project will thereby enhance demand for these climate-resilient approaches to farming not only within the eight targeted districts (which are home to 16 million rural people) but did not directly benefit from this project. This in turn will increase demand among non-state actors, including local government officials and other public sector providers (as described above), as well as advisory and service provision services (as described below) – within and beyond the targeted districts in Punjab and Sindh.

### d. The project will strive to create an environment where entire state and non-state actors are better able to sustainably adopt CRA and OFWM practices, and contribute to the process of transforming Indus Basin agriculture beyond project closure. The project will work with government stakeholders to promote greater use of new information and training materials by extension service providers in Punjab and Sindh. This is expected to improve vulnerable farmers’ access to credit and insurance products that would enable adoption (and continued adoption) of CRA and OFWM practices, as well as the ability of extension service providers and active financial service providers throughout the eight targeted districts. It will also support these service providers with new information and training materials on how best to promote CRA and OFWM practices – and the enhanced understanding into their products and pricing – so that they directly respond to these entities’ needs and demands for such services. The project will also work closely with government officials, as well as local agricultural service providers (e.g., input providers, processors and young agro-technicians) to understand and respond to their needs in order to better support them in promoting CRA and OFWM practices in addition to being better capacitated to provide support for locally appropriate CRA and OFWM practices. The project will also work with government officials to incentivize them to provide such services to respond to increasing demand from farmers (as outlined above).

**Page 42**
---

75. The success of this project will provide a possibility of scaling up the benefits by the Government, International Financial Institutions (especially the World Bank), and the private sector to all 16 million rural people living in the target provinces. This is because the proposed project builds on existing climate change adaptation strategies that are instrumental as it complements the new Government investment plan in agriculture in small machinery such as laser-levelled ploughs, drip irrigation systems, and solar-powered pumps. The project will also contribute to a paradigm transformation while addressing agriculture and water issues. Ultimately, there is also potential for replication of climate-smart agricultural practices in other provinces of Pakistan including Balochistan, Khyber Pakhtunkhwa, Afghanistan and India. Pakistan participates in a number of multilateral regional efforts such as the Indus Basin Initiative being implemented by the World Bank and the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD). The Indus Basin Knowledge Platform supported by the International Water Management Institute (IWMI), which offer excellent opportunities to share knowledge and experiences in this project that will help to extend the reach of possible benefits beyond national boundaries.

76. The combined effect of institution building activities and the availability of accurate real time information will transform decision making in participating institutions, which has the potential to spill over to other provinces in the Indus Basin region.

77. In providing this support, the project would catalyse a shift – in terms of the prevailing sectorial paradigm, the ways in which information and data are disseminated and used, and how sectorial support services are structured and provided – from one that is based on traditional approaches to one that is more responsive to the changing climate. The proposed GCF project would therefore also constitute a marked departure from previous projects and concepts of extension and dissemination of agricultural technologies in Pakistan as well as other countries. For example, the proposed project places a strong emphasis on strengthening capacity to provide climate-informed extension services at all levels – from national to provincial to district to sub-district to village level – that has not been provided. This is part of the project’s broader approach to emphasizing farmer learning and face-to-face interaction with extension officers in order to ensure that farmers are able to make informed decisions about their agricultural practices, including those related to climate change adaptation at farm level. This too represents a departure from other approaches that focus more on providing farming inputs and other services to farmers rather than empowering them to make informed choices.

Furthermore, the proposed project will establish new and innovative extension and information dissemination tools – these are not available in Pakistan or any of its provinces including Balochistan, Khyber Pakhtunkhwa, Punjab and Sindh, and offer considerable potential to expand the reach of climate-informed extension and advisory services. Finally, the project will establish cutting-edge systems that are new to the Indus Basin, and will use satellite imagery and other data to enable more holistic monitoring of cropping patterns, water use and climate change impacts.

#### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 78 OF 96

**E**

78. At present there is not enough knowledge of how the climate is changing in the Indus River Basin, how the changes are occurring and affecting agriculture, what changes are expected in the future and how they are likely to affect agriculture, and what can be done to best respond to those changes so as to create the conditions for sustainable adaptation. The project will address this knowledge gap by acquiring and disseminating information. Therefore, the investments in this project focus on acquiring and disseminating knowledge on how the climate is changing in the Indus River Basin, and what can be done to respond or adapt to those changes.

79. In Component 1, the project puts in place equipment and procedures to acquire data and information, transform that data into useful information for decision making by farmers, and make it available through a web portal with rainfall probability, timing, and intensity. In Component 2, vulnerable agricultural producers will learn skills to access and use this information. The project will work closely with the provincial Departments of Agriculture and Irrigation. The information made available through Component 1 about water and weather conditions in the Indus River Basin will be used by farmers to make decisions on when to plant and their crops in Component 2 activities under the project’s agriculture approach. In Component 3, the project activities on mobilizing resources for climate change adaptation will provide training and other support services to farmers to acquire knowledge that they can use to reduce risk and lower their costs, thus enabling them to provide financial services that are better suited to changing local conditions.

80. The knowledge and learning that will take place across different groups during the project will be the force underpinning the work of the Pakistan Agricultural Research Council in the Indus Basin. The knowledge of how the climate is changing in the Indus Basin will have applications, for example, in work to reform water distribution policies by providing information on the likely future changes in rainfall and river flows. It will also provide information on seed breeding and selection by the Pakistan Agricultural Research Centre or by seed suppliers like SANFIA Agri Services. Knowledge of how climate change is affecting agriculture will be shared with farmers through training, which will change their way of working as the project promotes the sharing of skills and resources amongst staff and counterparts across the region.

81 Key knowledge outputs generated by this project – in the form of training materials, including videos, and online services for farmers – will be available to other provinces through the ACWA portal. Knowledge from the project will reach a more general wider audience in the public awareness raising campaigns foreseen on radio and mobile phone platforms. The project will also work with government stakeholders at national level as well as staff and government stakeholders in regional events like the Indus River Basin Forum sponsored by IWMI in 2017.

82. The process of transforming India Basin agriculture that is set in motion by this project can only be sustained if agricultural policy and support services enable the activity to scale up into this program. In addition, these new proposed measures are conducive to effective and sustained participation of both public and private sector actors to continue supporting the climate-resilient agriculture beyond the project. These measures are also, to a large extent, the ones that are described in section D.2 Exit Strategy.

83. Component 3 aims to create an enabling environment in part by equipping relevant stakeholders to address issues related to access to information and services (e.g., lack of awareness about climate-resilient agriculture or lack of access to information). Rather than set up new systems and services, the project will work with the numerous relevant actors that are already in place. The project will also support the development of a network – with the right information and support - can play a key role in supporting the uptake of climate-resilient agriculture among farmers throughout the India Basin. The key is the networking and exchanging ideas and experiences.

84. The project will enhance the Government’s capacity to put into action its NDCP and NDC, as well as the Pakistan Climate Change Strategy (PCCS). It will do so by supporting the development of a national ICT platform that will so far be facilitating both Federal and Provincial Governments to use project results and data contained in the AAWA portal (Component 2) for decision-making. The project will also support the Government’s capacity to make informed allocation decisions with federal and provincial governments to ensure continued delivery of much-needed support and enabling conditions required by farmers throughout the process of transforming India Basin agriculture from a climate-vulnerable to a climate-resilient development pathway.

85. Given the efficiency of the private sector generally, especially with respect to technological development and service delivery, it is important that the project supports the private sector in providing services as efficiently as possible. It also favours arrangements whereby private sector partners will have incentives to work with government (this has been a key lesson learned from the AAWA project). The project will support the private sector by ensuring that it has access to the necessary financial resources available to producers and other private sector actors on a sustained basis, including by ensuring that the public sector provides adequate services. This will create a wider enabling environment where different actors complement each other to meet the demands of farmers.

86. The project aims to build the capacity not only of men who are farming but also of women so that communities as a whole may adopt climate resilient practices, as will be required given the interrelated nature of farm activities and family livelihoods in this region. It is important for the project to ensure the active participation of women and the involvement of women and minorities, so that they can continue to guide farmers where necessary and integrate farmers’ needs into the project.

87. There are a wide variety of other project activities that will improve the enabling environment for supporting agricultural producers to transition to climate resilient agriculture. This includes public education that will create a broad, common understanding among all stakeholders about what is needed in order to make this transition happen and how it can be achieved. It also includes technical assistance for provincial authorities to set water allocation policies that respond to changing climatic conditions. Other important measures include helping banks make crop insurance and credit available to farmers; providing weather forecasts and advice on when to put in place free phone-based services for timely weather forecasts needed around planting and harvesting.

#### E.3 Sustainable Development Potential: Water benefits and priorities

The project will deliver environmental co-benefits in the form of climate change mitigation. Using Bi-Arte Carbon Sequestration, the project will increase soil organic carbon content by up to 10% through improved land practices, improve soil quality and higher productivity of water in groundwater ecosystems will lower greenhouse gas emissions by approximately 0.3 tons per hectare per year in this project area. This will have a climate change mitigation impact of 250,000 tonnes CO₂ equivalent over the life of the project (19 years). If improved practices were adopted on 40 percent of the surface area to be covered by the project.

The project will generate social benefits from the explicit inclusion in technical training in field and demonstration plots. The project will also provide a range of other social benefits, including increased access to clean water for consumption for the 72 percent of households in South and 60 percent in Punjab that currently fall short of reaching four-star standards. The project will also provide direct nutrition support through the distribution of fortified rice and wheat flour. This will reduce food insecurity and malnutrition on a household basis, as well as improve access to nutritious foods for children under five years old.

### E.4. Needs of the Recipient

#### Vulnerability and financing needs of the beneficiary country and population (Adaptation only)

94. Pakistan's total population is now reported at 207 million people, with 132 million in rural areas, according to the 2017 Population Census. The majority of these live in the provinces of Punjab and Sindh. To put this in perspective, this will over the combined total of 65 million people who are exposed to climate change risks and living in at least 48 countries.

95. As noted above, Pakistan is highly vulnerable to climate change. According to the UNDP Multidimensional Poverty Index, 44 percent of Pakistan's total population and 68 percent of the rural population lives in poverty. The country has a Human Development Index (HDI) ranking of 137 out of 189 countries, which places it among the bottom third of the world’s 189 countries. That is lower than both India and Bangladesh. The low status in terms of human development means that Pakistan is less able to adapt its economy and society to climate change particularly acute and generally constrains people's adaptive capacity and ability to be resilient.

96. The intended beneficiaries of this project, within Punjab and Sindh Provinces in the Indus River Basin, are small farmers who own or rent land for their livelihoods. In 2017, 58 percent of households in the Indus Basin in the selected districts do not have a secure food supply. About 60 percent of people in Punjab districts and 72 percent in Sindh districts experience food insecurity. The majority of the rural population in these districts are illiterate with approximately 43 percent unable to read or write.

97. Most agricultural producers are landless, largely tenant farmers. Only 22 percent of households in the project area in Punjab and 36 percent in Punjab own the agricultural land that they farm. The extent of bonded labour and child labour in the area led the Government of Pakistan and the International Labour Organisation (ILO) to initiate a joint project for the eradication of bonded labour in the Punjab districts of Khairpur, Mianwali, and Dera Ghazi Khan. All three districts are part of this project's target area.

98. In such conditions, the resilience of rural households is limited and their vulnerability to changing climatic conditions is high. The majority of these rural households live in poverty. The vulnerability of the large proportion of the population in these two quite large provinces makes the nation itself quite vulnerable overall.

#### E.4.2. Financial, economic, social and institutional needs

99. Pakistan's financial needs with respect to adaptation are very high. The Government of Pakistan’s First National Communication (2017) estimated that by 2030 it will require between USD 7-14 billion per annum to meet its adaptation needs. These would be required to protect public and private resources that are the source of economic growth, such as water infrastructure, agriculture, and energy. The bulk of these costs is expected to fall on the private sector. However, resources to provide public goods and services such as technology, knowledge and information systems will also need to be provided.

100. In 2016, in spite of Pakistan’s financial constraints, estimated federal climate-related expenditure was between 5.8-7.8 percent of the total expenditures in the federal budget. Pakistan is committed to increasing that amount to 9.3-14.5 percent by 2020. This increase will be required for the maintenance and rehabilitation of security and servicing-related defence costs of the federal government. At the same time provincial governments have very little capacity to meet their climate change adaptation needs, due to current cost expenditures in health, education and communications due to urbanisation and demographic changes.

101. At present, the federal government is not in a position to raise additional resources domestically through increased domestic revenues borrowing due to negative impacts on the economy of high tariffs. It has sought support from its interest-related partners for climate change adaptation projects as substitutes to address agriculture and water issues in the Indus Basin. However, increasing future debt servicing obligations remains

### EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

#### E.5. Country Ownership

**E.5.1. National Climate Policy (i.e., ownership of, and capacity to implement, a funded project or programme)**

The Ministry of Planning, Development and Reforms developed the Pakistan Framework for Economic Growth in 2014, which sets out the impact of climate change in the country. It proposes to “stimulate green economic growth from the impacts of climate change,” paying particular attention to the agricultural, water and energy sectors. Furthermore, Pakistan has signed the Paris Agreement on Climate Change (UNFCCC) in 2015 and is a signatory to the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction, both of which place an emphasis on agricultural development.

At the 22nd Session of the Conference of the Parties (COP22) to the UN Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), Pakistan highlighted its National Climate Change Policy as National Disaster Risk Reduction.

E.5.3 Engagement with NGOs, civil society organizations and other relevant stakeholders

111. This proposal was prepared in response to the expression of interest by Pakistan’s NDA at the MoCC for assistance in adaptation work in water management and the agricultural sector. Meetings began with the NDA in April 2017, and continued through May 2018. The objective was to identify key stakeholders and understand the needs of the stakeholders who will be concerned by investments in this area. Key stakeholders were identified within MoCC, in the Ministry of Water and Power (MWP), the Ministry of Agriculture (MoA) and the Pakistan Meteorological Department (PMD). FAO gathered technical inputs and institutional background on the mandate, role, capacity and work plans of these key stakeholders to understand their needs and how they could be addressed. The objectives and scope of the proposed project were outlined in meetings with those agencies. Individual interviews took place with officials in the Punjab and Sindh Provincial Departments of Irrigation and Departments of Agriculture.

### E.6.1 Cost-effectiveness and efficiency

#### 119. The most direct quantitative information for project design was used for the calculations in the economic and financial analysis of the planned investment for this project. A likely 4 percent decline in yields of wheat, rice, cotton and sugarcane due to climate change would be expected to decrease net profit of targeted farmers in the project area by about USD 12 million over a 20-year period. This is equivalent to an estimated USD 600 per hectare loss that could be avoided. New practices adopted on 5 percent of agricultural land would eventually increase yield to up to 40 percent higher than current yields, and thus reduce the projected decline in net profit by about 83 percent. The early adopters will take up these new practices and after initial positive results more and more farmers will adopt them. This is expected to be further supported by the project’s dissemination of best practice and end-of-month recommendations among farmers and villages. This would generate an additional net cash flow for producers of USD 10 million over a 20-year period, which could be used to pay off loans or invest in other activities that return to family labour as the result of increased yields and lower production costs. The return on the investment of the estimated USD 37 per beneficiary is expected to be about four times higher than the cost.

#### 118. Additional financial and economic growth will be generated by the project as the turnover of agricultural sector businesses including input suppliers, traders and processing facilities will increase due to increased output volumes, and additional employment opportunities in the project area will be created for up to 20 percent of all rural households. This would build the strength and resilience of the local economy to cope with incremental changes due to longer-term climate change impacts, and also with other shocks.

#### 119. The cost of the overall investment for the project would be about USD 37 per beneficiary, counting 1.3 million direct beneficiaries that will be reached, which represents 8 percent of the rural population in the project area. The reach is expected to be achieved through a combination of direct and indirect benefits from the project’s co-investment, such as the recently approved Responding to the Increasing Risk of Drought Building Gender-responsive Resilience of Most Vulnerable Communities in Ethiopia project, reaching an estimated 1.3 million beneficiaries at a total cost of USD 50 million; the Water Management Project for the Community-based Water Users’ Association and Water Management Practices for Vulnerable Communities in La Mojica at a total cost of USD 117 million is expected to reach about 24 percent.

#### 120. In terms of efficiency, this project would compare very favourably with on-going investments being made in the LA basin by other development assistance agencies, with estimated costs per beneficiary ranging from USD 26 for the SGRF-funded Water Conservation Project in Morocco to USD 358 for the recently approved GCF-financed Salais Water Conservation Project for Morocco estimated at a total cost of USD 209.67 million or about USD 14,000 per beneficiary.

#### 121. The project would also compare very favourably with other projects in the LA basin that are currently favoured to the Bhutan for Life project at a total cost of USD 118 million or about USD 422 per beneficiary for the total planned investment.

#### 122. The return on GCF resources will be enhanced by the mobilization of USD 12.7 million in Government co-financing that will cover about one quarter of the total project costs.

#### E.8.4. Application of best practices

127. Best practices have been applied to the design of this project, and will be applied in its implementation, in all facets of the project technologies and practices that have proven to be successful elsewhere and that also fit the given context of this project.

128. One of the most innovative and valuable technologies to be put in place under the project will be the ET monitor. The ET monitor is a new technology that has been developed by the University of California, Davis for determining real use and needs of plants for water in agriculture. The technology is recognised for its accuracy and the data it provides can be used to determine the optimal irrigation schedule for crops, which will help farmers achieve the highest possible productivity of water based on actual plant physiological requirements. It has been successfully used in China for precise monitoring and water allocation on large areas.

129. The project will also bring a set of FAO-developed technologies that are very relevant to climate change analysis and adaptation, such as Collected Earth, Earth Map, and MODISdata. The project will develop the national capacity to use these technologies.

130. The proposed CRA and OFWM practices (see sub-component 2.1) are all tested in Pakistan. As demonstrated in the literature review, these practices have been shown to be effective in increasing yields of wheat and rice by up to 5% yield increase. These practices will be disseminated using FAO's FDS extension platform, which is widely recognised as one of the most successful platforms for agricultural extension.

131. The project will also put the best practices of Pakistan’s own communications industry leaders who distribute content over several formats including its print, radio, mobile phone messages and online platforms in the service of climate change adaptation. These practices have been used by the project partner with Pakistan's own successful mobile money service providers to extend their reach into the country's most vulnerable areas. The project will also use these best practices for the dissemination of information on how to provide opportunities for synergy with the on-going collaboration in Sindh for simple means of electronic registration of children and women, as well as the provision of financial services to women and children in the project area, described in the Environmental and Social Management Framework (Annex 4).

132. Building on the best practices from elsewhere in South Asia and in East Africa, distance learning for illiterate agricultural producers will be organised through interactive delivery of selected technical messages through video

133. Other best practices – no less important – range to the opposite end of the spectrum. The project will also provide transport for women and day care for children while women attend training events.

134. Another best practice that will be applied in the project relates to training certificates as means to validate skills acquired by water user associations, small-scale irrigation systems, and agrotechnicians. The project will adopt this approach through the hiring of agrotechnicians who will provide some services to water user associations and trainees that will enable them to maintain and repair equipment and small-scale machines, like pumps.

135. On still another level the project incorporates best practices with respect to the delivery of development assistance in a way that ensures sustainability. The project is designed to gradually assume any recurrent costs that they may incur as the result of new equipment, staff or activities introduced by the project that will be sustained after project completion. This includes the cost of running the ET monitoring systems required in project year 4, project year 5 and project year 6 to cover the costs of running the ET monitoring systems.

# F.1 Economic and Financial Analysis

## 140. On a per acre basis, net profits were calculated for each crop and new each practice. Five main practices are included in the analysis: (i) wheat – zero tillage; (ii) rice – alternate wetting and dry with direct seeding; (iii) cotton – ridge seeding; (iv) maize – ridge seeding; (v) sorghum – ridge seeding. The results show that the new practices are more profitable than the current practices, especially for crops like wheat and cotton. These systems can better withstand climate change induced events.

## 140. In order to adequately reflect changes on the ground, the influence of climate change on crop yields has been built into the analysis. A yield reduction scenario is assumed in which a 25% decrease in average yield decline is assumed with both the with and without project scenarios. Farmers, for example, would face a loss of around 10% to 30% of their income if they were not able to adapt to climate change. The occurrence of extreme weather events (e.g., flood or drought) in the Indus Basin have also been considered. Every fifth year, there is an increase in rainfall and every third year there is a decrease in rainfall. This results in a recuperation period until yields bounce back to the base case in the third year after the extreme weather event. The same applies for temperature changes. These scenarios are designed to reflect the worst-case scenario but also provide some resilience due to project activities. Stronger and more stable livelihoods mean that households will be better positioned to withstand future climate change impacts.

### Economic Analysis

143. The economic analysis not only includes benefits directly related to the on-farm activities, but also environmental co-benefits benefiting the society as a whole. Therefore, the analysis focuses on a main case A which includes environmental co-benefits. A sensitivity analysis was also conducted for a secondary case B that excludes environmental co-benefits.

144. The environmental co-benefits are quantified through the FAO's EX-ACT tool that estimates the impact of the agriculture practices on the carbon-balance. The carbon-balance is defined as the net balance from all GHGs expressed in CO2 equivalent (CO2e) released or sequestered by agricultural activities over a period of time.

145. In the main case A, the project has an overall positive impact on the environment and society. For the project, the baseline is due to first (i) reduced CH4 emissions from the shift to continuously flooded to intermittently flooded rice production systems and secondly (ii) increased CO2 sequestration in the soil of continuous flooded rice production systems and sugarcane. This translates into -0.15tCO2e for wheelchairs, -0.16 tCO2-e for sugarcane and + 1.29 t CO2e for rice per year, per hectare. The project results in a price of USD 40 per tonne of CO2e, in case of the baseline scenario (i.e., the current market price of Carbon Prices), and USD 58 per tonne of CO2e under the "BCC" (but) a sensitivity analysis shows that the project remains profitable with a carbon price of USD 5 per tCO2e (as outlined in Table 4).

146. The analysis uses the economic NPV, the benefit-cost ratio and EIRR as a measurement for success. Economic values (costs and benefits) are all measured in real terms of 2017. The financial price for traded agricultural inputs and outputs is based on the average market prices during the project period and reflect parity payments for traded goods and commodities were converted to economic prices using a conversion factor of 0.83. Economic costs of the project are net of taxes, duties, subsidies and price contingencies. In addition, it is assumed that benefits would be realized in the year following their occurrence (i.e., the year after year 20). Project costs are also net of taxes and occur during the project implementation period in the first years.

147. The deposit interest rate in Pakistan in 2018 is 5.75 percent and the discount rate at which it lends to commercial banks is 15 percent. Hence, the project is using a computed social discount rate of 10 percent for the analysis.

A sensitivity analysis was also conducted to test the robustness of the project, including project cost escalation, reduction of benefits, delays of incremental benefits to take place, exclusion of environmental benefits and different social discount rates. Sensitivity increased in investment costs were estimated at 110 percent, 120 percent and 150 percent of the base scenario.

151. The projects maintains above 10 percent returns even when the investment cost increases by 50 percent. Compared to the cost increase scenario, the decrease in benefits is relatively more sensitive, and the economic return is more sensitive than the delay in incremental benefits. A reduction of 20% in benefits would reduce EIRR to 8.4 percent. With a 50 percent decrease in benefits, the project could no longer maintain above 10 percent of economic returns.

152. One of the parameters that was tested in the sensitivity analysis was the exclusion of the environmental co-benefits. Excluding those benefits, the EIRR is 16.8 percent, the NPV and the Cost-Benefit ratio is 1.36 points, all at a social discount rate of 5%.

## F.2 Technical Evaluation

### Option Considered but Rejected: Irrigated Agriculture in the Indus Basin Area
The Government and FAO examined a wide range of options for supporting Pakistan to effectively transform the agricultural sector and water management in the Indus Basin to become climate resilient. The focus was on irrigated agriculture, which is more productive than rainfed agriculture and hence are often more vulnerable to climate change impacts. However, the option to address the impact of climate change on irrigated agriculture was retained, given the importance of the irrigated agriculture sector in the Indus Basin in terms of food security and economic development.

### Investments in Irrigation Infrastructure
The focus on irrigated agriculture is due to efforts made by the basin’s irrigation department to improve water delivery functions. However, there are some limitations in access and availability of irrigation water from the canal system. These current limitations in access and availability of irrigation water from the canal system mean that investments in irrigation infrastructure such as canals and distribution channels will not be directly made and would help farmers adapt to climate change.

### Increased Temperatures
Increased temperatures leading to increased evapotranspiration will increase crop water requirements and necessitate improved efficiency in water delivery and use. Planned project activities to establish a water accounting system for the Indus Basin Irrigation System (IBIS) will be scaled up as a result of this GCF-financed project.

### Dissipation of Resources over Large Area Coverage
The Government and FAO have proposed to cover all of the irrigated agriculture area in the Indus Basin, including provinces and the lower regions of irrigated districts in Balochistan and Khyber Pakhtunkhwa. Equally important was the need to limit the area where the project would be implemented so that farmers, extension officers, and service providers who work with them needed to achieve a real transformation in production practices in a given area.

### Technical Evaluation
- **Selection of Crops and Production System**: The rice-wheat-cotton-holin and sugarcane cropping systems were selected as they are widely grown crops and for their economic importance. In addition, multiple cropping of fruits and vegetables was included as a climate resilient practice.

The project will use the FFS approach, which promotes farm-based experimentation, group organization and decision-making processes that allow farmers to identify their own priorities, needs and adopt improved practices, and increasing farmers’ analytical capacities which will also improve their capacity to respond to future – as yet unknown – climate change conditions.

# F3. Environmental, Social Assessment, Including Gender Considerations

162. An Environmental and Social Assessment was undertaken during project preparation according to the International Finance Corporation (IFC) Performance Standards and FAO Environmental and Social Management Guidelines.

163. Assessments Findings: Overall, the environmental and social impacts of the Project will be positive. The project is expected to improve the natural resources and agricultural land upon which farmers work, based on improved climate resilience, increased productivity, and better access to markets. This will lead to a more productive farming system at basin. Better functioning ecosystems will positively affect human health and well-being in the long run. Socially, the project will engage women through a Gender Action Plan that ensures proactive mainstreaming of women into all activities. The project is expected to increase the number of farmers who are able to access markets, which would increase adaptive capacities within the target communities. Investments in machinery and equipment, technology, and high-yielding varieties will lead to increased agricultural productivity and production. The establishment of the ACAW portal is expected to positively impact governance at all levels by providing a platform for information sharing between farmers, government officials, and agricultural extension services with a climate focus. In addition to these positive impacts, the project incorporates special provisions in its Gender Action Plan (GAP) to ensure that women are not disproportionately affected. The GAP also ensures that farmers are able to proactively enhance their livelihoods in ways that would not have occurred in a without project scenario. For example, Integrated Pest Management (IPM) practices will be used under the project in order to promote sustainable pest management and reduce the use of pesticides.

164. Given that the water management techniques promoted through the project under Component 1 will be based on rigorous water accounting and ET monitoring, conflict pertaining to water is not foreseen as a result of the project. On-farm irrigation systems are expected to increase access to water for smallholder farmers and other agricultural users based on increased withdrawals or sequestration of water. The project’s inclusion of landless farmers and tenants means there is a potential risk of changes in tenurial relationships with landlords due to increased land value resulting from increased productivity, which could lead to conflicts between the two parties. A Gender Action Plan will be developed on the Responsible Governance of Tenure of Land, Fisheries and Forests. Sensitisation will also be offered on safe, decent and dignified work for women in agriculture, including through the project’s Gender Action Plan and/or respective families. Increased production may result in the generation of new environmental waste for example from slow-release fertilisers or increased use of pesticides. However, this is expected to be mitigated by reduced pesticide use, even if the pesticide use is indirect and not promoted under the project. As mentioned with the positive impacts of the project, indirect, increased use of pesticides will be mitigated by training on IPM. In instances where pesticide use is promoted over other varieties, it is expected that this will be done in a manner which would be promoted over other varieties. There will be no pesticide procurement under the project and high-hazardous pesticides are not included as part of any component of the project. The project will also ensure that appropriate construction activities may be pursued for the sake of establishing new agro-setstlements. Due to the small size of such settlements, it is expected that there would be no significant environmental impacts from their establishment including installation, air pollution due to dust, and health/safety risks during installation of agro-settlements are envisaged to be low-to-moderate, localised and temporary.

165. According to IFC and FAO Guidelines followed in this assessment, the project has been categorised as a Category 2 project. The main reason for this is the inequality in the labour market and presence of landless farmers in the project area who will be included in project activities. Results of the assessment in terms of safeguards triggered, as a result of both existing conditions in the project area and of the planned project activities are shown here

### 160 Environmental and Social Safeguard Management Framework (ESMF) Approach

As called for by the FAO and IFC guidelines, the project will be using an ESMF approach as the exact beneficiaries can only be known at the time of implementation. The ESMF is attached as a part of the ESFM document. A summary of safeguard-related consultations undertaken to date is included in Attachment 6 of this funding proposal.

#### Gender Analysis

An analysis of the gender-related aspects of the project in the project area was also undertaken and is reflected in the Gender Assessment and Action Plan (Annex 5). The analysis showed that equality is provided for in various forms in legal documents, including the Constitution of Pakistan. However, women have not been able to benefit from these provisions or the first goals, but women have generally not benefited from these declarations. De facto, an inferior status is accorded to women and girls due to their socio-economic circumstances, especially in rural areas. Segmentation of men and women is a dominant factor both for private and public life that accentuates differences in gender-based roles and responsibilities. In terms of literacy rate, rural women are less literate than urban women in Pakistan. The project area literacy level is very low with only 33 percent of women in Punjab and 16 percent in Sindh able to read. Women's standard of health is low by comparison with men; for example, women in the project area are more likely to be anaemic and twice as likely to be malnourished compared to their counterparts in rural Nigeria. In agriculture, women are fully

## F4. Financial Management and Procurement

### 169. Financial management and procurement under this project will be guided by relevant FAO rules and regulations, as well as relevant provisions in the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) signed by FAO and the GCF.

#### 170. The project will be executed directly by FAO in accordance with its rules, regulations, policies and procedures.

- **171.** FAO has deployed an Oracle-based Enterprise Resource Planning (ERP) system called "Global Resources Management System" (GRMS). This system provides all FAO employees around the world with travel, human resources, procurement, financial management, and reporting functionalities. It also supports internal control, financial monitoring and reporting, increases transparency and visibility, and strengthens internal control. FAO maintains a comprehensive database of all project-related information, including the estimation of income and expenditure by donor and project and it provides a standardized coding structure that enables data to be coded, classified and summarized to facilitate internal management and external reporting requirements.

- **172.** Direct procurement by FAO is done in accordance with the FAO Manual Section 502: "Procurement of Goods Works and Services". The Procurement Unit within the Operations Division manages this function. FAO operates in accordance with its Manual Section 507, "Letters of Agreement". Such services are managed under the FAO Procurement Policy and Procedures. FAO also follows the GCF's procurement policy and procedures for organizing activities to ensure the Organization procures goods, works and services based on "Best Value for Money" principles.

- **173.** To sub-contract delivery for agreed results, FAO operates in accordance with its Manual Section 701, "Operational Partners' Agreement".

### The project will be subject to GCF's audit regime, including the external audit and internal audit function.

# G2 Risk Factors and Mitigation Measures

There are eight selected risk factors identified.

## Selected Risk Factor 1

### Description
Security: Operations in the field are disrupted due to deteriorating political and social stability jeopardizing the safety of field staff and making it difficult to implement planned activities.

### Risk category
Technical and operational

### Level of impact
High (20% of project value)

### Probability of risk occurring
Medium

### Mitigation Measure(s)
Work programming at the level of field activities will be undertaken with guidance from the country-level authorities of the United Nations Department of Safety and Security. Every year, in collaboration with local authorities, the project management team will identify and address security issues in specific areas that are likely to affect staff or producers at the time of the preparation of the project annual work plans and budget.

## Selected Risk Factor 2

### Description
Government implementing partners, conditioned by past practices of working independently, each in their own domain, will find it cumbersome to establish a collaborative approach.

### Risk category
Technical and operational

### Level of impact
Medium (5-10% of project value)

### Probability of risk occurring
Medium

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 58 OF 96

### Selected Risk Factor 1

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Land users may return to unsustainable practices after project has ended. | Social and environmental | Medium (5-20% of project value) | Low |

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
The proposed project will promote only economically viable agriculture practices that have been developed and tested with farmers in the provinces of Sindh and Punjab, and for which they will have access to any necessary inputs and services required for their completion as the result of project activities and investments.

### Selected Risk Factor 4

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Traditional relationships with landlords and middlemen may be disrupted, leading smallholder farmers to adopt climate resilient agriculture practices and on-farm water management. | Social and environmental | Low (<5% of project value) | Low |

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
The proposed project will ensure economic benefits and lower the labor-intensity of climate resilient agriculture practices and on-farm water management and demonstrate the financial profitability of their adoption. It will also assist farmers to know about other sources of livelihood in the form of micro-finance, insurance and other production financing that is available to them in the project area.

### Selected Risk Factor 5

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Limited awareness and preparation of communities and local government authorities for adoption of the technologies, practices, and information advanced through the project. | Technical and operational | Low (<5% of project value) | Low |

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
As part of the project design, there are different capacity development and training initiatives for community members, community leaders and local government authorities to impede adoption. FAO Pakistan has a proven record of social mobilisation in the project areas.

### Selected Risk Factor 6

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Climate shocks generally can lead to a risk of damages to the project investments affecting implementation. | Social and environmental | High (20% of project value) | Low |

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 57 OF 86

### Mitigation Measure(s)
Special mitigation measures to cope with shocks such as droughts and floods will be part of the project proposal so as to minimise the impact on project implementation.

#### Selected Risk Factor 7
- **Description:** Private sector may not have the same goals or interests (driven by profit) as other stakeholders, leading to misalignment in priorities to address resilience objective of the project.
- **Risk category:** Social and environmental
- **Level of impact:** Medium (5% - 20% of project value)
- **Probability of risk occurring:** Medium

#### Selected Risk Factor 8
- **Description:** Changes in Pakistan's status vis-à-vis the Financial Action Task Force (FATF) may pose financial risks, including possible impacts on local prices.
- **Risk category:** Financial
- **Level of impact:** Low (5% - 20% of project value)
- **Probability of risk occurring:** Medium

#### Mitigation measure(s):
FAO considers that the probability that Pakistan's position vis-à-vis the FATF will worsen during project implementation is low, in part because the current government has reinforced the country's commitment to uphold the integrity of the international financial system. The potential risks associated with this risk factor are mitigated by the fact that (i) the proposed project will involve minimal procurement of farming inputs and if any лицо involved in such procurements are subject to FATF sanctions, they would be excluded from the project as per its legal status and associated privileges and immunities; FAO is able to operate in contexts even where sanctions have been imposed on individuals or entities, as outlined above).

#### Other Potential Risks in the Horizon
- **Project Starting Months:** The proposed five cropping systems are all based on the cropping calendar, with April 2018 set as the starting month. Delays in approval or any other project start date would mean that the project team learns work practices later than planned and may need to adapt its approach for new practices. Similarly, depending on the extent of the delays, the project implementation team may be required to perform a second analysis of the project areas in order to determine any changes in climate-change needs and/or institutional arrangements.

## Component 2: Building on Existing Climate Change Interventions

### Sub-component 2.1: Scaling up climate-resilient agricultural practices
- **Farmers understand and apply climate-resilient agricultural practices (CRAs) and water management (DWPm) practices**
    - **Change in farmers' understanding of climate-resilient agricultural practices as a result of project activities:** 0
    - **Post and post-training assessments by farmers participating in project trainings:**
        - 80% of farmers trained by extension staff, partner field staff and facilitators.
        - Farmers' PYS score better than pre-training assessments (3.562 vs 4.17) on post training assessments.
    - **Farmers are skilled in using climate-resilient agricultural practices:**
        - Assessments of farmers trained by trainers of CRAs and DWPm actions during (and at least one week after) project trainings:
            - At least 70% of the targeted farmers involved in post-training assessments begin to apply CRAs and DWPm actions on their farms.
            - Randomized site visits to farmers' fields by project training specialists.
        - At least 50% of the targeted farmers involved in post-training assessments begin to apply CRAs and DWPm actions on their farms at the completion of their trainings.

## H2 Arrangements for Monitoring, Reporting and Evaluation

### 178. In this role will be involved Entity FAO (specifically the GEF-QCT project supervision team) will oversee and supervise the implementation of this project in accordance with the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) signed between FAO and the GCF, as per the GCF Monitoring and Accountability Framework, and in accordance with the AMA, FAO will be responsible for the following: a) The preparation of an Annual Progress Report, a Semi-Annual Project Report, a Project Closure Report and an Independent Final Evaluation report. FAO will also provide semi-annual and annual Financial Statements.

### 180. In accordance with the AMA between FAO and GCF, the FAO Office of Evaluation will be responsible for the independent interim and final evaluations. The evaluations will be conducted using a question-driven approach, and may include desk reviews, interviews, focus group discussions, field visits, among others. The purpose of each evaluation will be instrumental in contributing – through operational and strategic recommendations – to improve implementation, setting out any necessary corrective measures for the remaining part of the project. The final evaluation will also assess the extent to which the intervention has achieved its intended results, including the identification of potential risks, differential impacts and lessons learned. The evaluation should also assess the extent to which the intervention has contributed to the achievement of the GCF’s Strategic Objectives and the Sustainable Development Goals.

### 181. Responsibility for project-level monitoring will rest with the PJMU. The PJMU will set up a monitoring system to be staffed by responsible project officers in connection with the three core project components. The monitoring system will serve as an information management tool, which will provide data and information on the progress of activities and outputs, outcomes and impacts detailed in the results framework, shown above in Section II.1. Logic Framework is a deductive model that describes how the project will be implemented and what it aims to achieve. It will be described in a dedicated module of the project MIS to be developed and deployed by the PJMU. The monitoring team will work with several partners to develop tools for the project processes for data collection with mobile telephones with Collect Mobile, an innovative tool developed by FAO.

### 182. Before other project activities start, the project will launch a baseline survey, as already noted above in Section E1. Impact Potential. This work will draw on the Self-evaluation and Holistic Assessment of Climate Resilience of farmers and Pastoralists (SHARP) methodology developed by FAO and used for the participatory assessments of agro-ecosystem resilience, and on the work of the programme entitled Building Resilience and Adaptation to Climate Change in Semi-Arid Areas (BRACCA), funded by the Global Environment Facility Fund on number of people whose resilience has been improved as a result of support from the project. Using relevant data from the [baseline and completion] household surveys, the aforementioned approaches will enable the project to

# RESULTS MONITORING AND REPORTING

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL | PAGE 80 OF 96

### H

efficiency monitor household resilience at all the start and end of the project, and the improvements herein. This in turn will enable more robust monitoring and reporting against key indicators included in the logical framework (as outlined in Section H.1). FAO has considerable experience using such approaches, which have proven to be an effective and efficient means of collecting data and information on household resilience at baseline and endline for similar interventions of the project; special attention will be given to the gender-disaggregation of data and findings on results and outcomes.

Using these tools, the project monitoring staff will set a baseline for project progress and for subsequent evaluation. In addition, the project team will also use the same tools to monitor and evaluate the performance of the selected counterpart institutions in order to be able to monitor and evaluate achievement of key GCF Outcome indicators. The baseline data collected through this process will be used as part of the project’s monitoring framework. Baseline evaluations and institutional indicators will be re-assessed at the end of the project through a completion survey and associated evaluation. This will allow for an assessment of progress made against the project’s objectives, particularly most notably at the mid-term stage – using other means of verification to ensure the project is proceeding as anticipated and achieving its intended results.

The costs for all surveys and other means of data collection and analysis have been duly integrated into the project budget.


183. Project monitoring officers will monitor AWIPS implementation and the outputs that result from it, including those that are detailed in the results framework, on an ongoing basis. They will use the data and information from this monitoring to prepare regular progress reports for the POC and the FAO-GCF project supervision team (in its role as Accredited Entity). Using these inputs, the FAO-GCF project supervision team will bear overall responsibility for ensuring that all relevant reporting is provided in a timely manner. The POC will be responsible for ensuring that this information is shared appropriately within the organisations. The monitoring team will also share monitoring findings with the project management staff so that they display the progress of AWIPS and they arise. This will take place in the form of knowledge sharing events and briefings within the PMU, PPu and DPR management teams.

# Theory of Change Diagram

## Project Objective
To increase resilience to climate change among the most vulnerable sectors in the Indus Basin and Pakistan’s coastal zone through a project that supports communities in adapting.

## Expected Project Outcomes
1. Enhanced institutional and management capacity to practice climate-resilient agriculture and modern agriculture sector and value chain development.
2. Building on Farm Resilience and Climate Change Adaptation in the Water and Agriculture Sector.
3. Creating an Enabling Environment for Climate Transformation.

## Components
- Enhancing Institutional and Management Capacity to Practice Climate Resilient Agriculture and Adaptation in the Water and Agriculture Sector
- Building on Farm Resilience and Climate Change Adaptation
- Creating an Enabling Environment for Climate Transformation

## Actions to remove the Barriers
1. Developing a Master Plan
2. Improving Practices of Climate Resilient Agriculture
3. Improving Information and Communication Technology (ICT) Services in Water and Agriculture Sector

## Barriers to be addressed
- Weak institutional and technical capacity at all levels.
- Lack of integration of climate change adaptation into the planning, budgeting, and implementation processes.
- Limited access to and use of information on climate change impacts and adaptation opportunities.
- Lack of understanding of climate change issues among policy makers, planners, and other stakeholders.
- Lack of coordination between different sectors and actors in implementing climate change adaptation measures.
- Lack of communication strategies for raising awareness about the importance of climate change adaptation.

No-objection letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal point(s)

Government of Pakistan
Ministry of Climate Change
LG & RDP Complex, Sector G-5/2, Islamabad

ADDITIONAL SECRETARY
Islamabad, the 19th April 2019

To:
Green Climate Fund (GCF) Secretariat
Seoul
South Korea

Subject: Funding proposal for the GCF by FAO regarding "Transforming the Indus Basin with Climate Resilient Agriculture and Water Management".

We refer to the project "Transforming the Indus Basin with Climate Resilient Agriculture and Water Management" as included in the funding proposal submitted by FAO to us on 6th December 2018.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of the Ministry of Climate Change, the National Designated Authority/Focal Point of Pakistan. Pursuant to GCF decision R.08/10, the content of this letter constitutes our formal no-objection. We hereby communicate our no-objection to the project as included in the funding proposal.

3. By communicating our No-Objection, it is implied that:
a) The Government of Pakistan has no objection to the project as included in the funding proposal.
b) The project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with Pakistan's national policies and strategies.
c) In accordance with the GCF’s environmental and social safeguards, the project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.

4. We also confirm that our national process for ascertaining no-objection to the project as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.

5. We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

Yours sincerely,

(Babar Hayat Tarar)

Copy to:
1. FAO Country Office, Islamabad.
