# Secretariat’s assessment of FP108

## Proposal name: Transforming the Indus Basin with Climate Resilient Agriculture and Water Management

Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

Country: Pakistan

Project/programme size: Small

### I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

#### 1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

**Strengths**

- This project focuses on agriculture, which is the primary source of income and livelihoods for rural communities in Pakistan.
- It supports smallholder farmers who are most impacted by climate change.

**Points of caution**

- Monsoon rains cause flash flooding in the target provinces, which could impact the project. Provinces with shorter cropping periods will be promoted to mitigate this risk.
- In addressing climate change impacts on yields, it does not demonstrate a clear climate rationale for GCF-funded activities supported by robust baseline data and economic analysis.

**Policy level update of assessment results**

- The project will set up a steering committee for decision-making, which will ensure accountability.

#### 2.1 Project background


---
**Page 95**
---

1. Pakistan’s vulnerability is linked to its dominant and semi-arid climate, as well as its high dependency on a single river system and snow/glacial meltwater for its agricultural water supply. Agriculture consumes roughly 90 per cent of all available fresh water supplies. It employs more than half the country's labour force, accounts for about one-third of the country’s food supply, and generates 75 per cent of the country’s export revenues. For rural areas, agriculture is the main source of income and livelihoods.
2. Agriculture in Pakistan is highly vulnerable to climate change, manifested through significant temperature increases and more variable changes in precipitation, which will impact the Indus Basin and its agricultural productivity.
3. The project is aimed at (a) increasing the resilience of poor small-scale farmers to climate change in eight vulnerable districts in the Sindh and Punjab provinces based on the climate vulnerability assessment; (b) enhancing Pakistan’s capacity for supporting communities in adapting to climate change through improved climate information services, and (c) building the capacity of the Government of Pakistan.
4. The project will achieve this through the implementation of three main technical components in the targeted agricultural districts of Pakistan:
   - Enhancing information services for climate change adaptation in the water and agriculture sectors,
   - Building on-farm resilience to climate change; and
   - Creating an enabling environment for continued transformation.

### 2.3.1 Climate objective

- Modelling of climate change scenarios for Pakistan shows that if agriculture and water management practices in the Basin continue in a “business-as-usual” mode, increasing temperatures and changes in precipitation will pose serious threats to the future livelihoods of farmers and communities living in the Indus Basin. The project aims to address this challenge by moving away from “business-as-usual” in the Basin, causing a paradigm shift in agriculture and water management practices that will help small-scale farmers adapt to climate change and sustain their livelihoods. The project objective is to transform agriculture in the Basin by increasing resilience among poor small-scale farmers while building the capacity of the Government of Pakistan to support communities to adapt to climate change.

### 2.3.2 Financing information and environmental and social safeguards category

- The accredited entity (AE) has requested USD 34,99 million in grant financing from GCF to finance this project. This amount is being provided by two sources: one from the Government of Pakistan, including a grant from the Government of Punjab of USD 7,99 million ($22 per cent) and one from the Government of Sindh for USD 4,69 million (13.4 per cent). The co-financing amount is being provided by the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) under a Unilateral Trust Fund arrangement between the governments and FAO.
- The project management cost (PMC) is USD 2,15 million, which is under 5 per cent of the total project amount. This includes GCF grant financing of USD 1,29 million, with additional co-financing from the Government of Pakistan for USD 0,86 million and co-financing from FAO to support PMC in the project.
- The project has been categorized by the AE as a Category B – medium risk project. This is because there are no risks in the labour market and presence of landless farmers in project sites who will be included in project activities. An environmental and social management framework (ESMF) is presented with the funding proposal, which includes information on


---
**Page 96**

### Component-by-component analysis

#### Component 1: Enhancing information services for climate-change adaptation in the water and agriculture sectors

##### Subcomponent 1a: Capacity development activities for climate change impacts monitoring and early warning systems (CCEWS)

15. The component will conduct a series of capacity development activities for the government staff to enable them to use key technologies for better monitoring climate change impacts on water resources and agricultural production in the project area. These activities will support the development of a water accounting system for the Indus Basin through conducting four water accounting workshops, as well as the development of early warning systems using remote sensing data to assess droughts and floods. In addition, a set of risk-based assessment for aquifer vulnerability will be developed by conducting two workshops with stakeholders. The results provided to planners to undertake such assessment after project closure. This will help to develop local aquifer management plans in selected areas. The regulatory and policy institutions will also be supported through the development of guidelines and training materials, and capacities to make informed decisions based on such analysis. Hence, the component will address this by training planners and local farmers in important aspects of hydrology monitoring and management.

##### Subcomponent 1b: Strengthening reservoir management for climate change adaptation

16. The second sub-component under component 1 of the project is strengthening reservoir management to ensure better water allocation during dry seasons. The agriculture system in Pakistan is dependent on rain-fed water systems and often reliant on seasonal rainfall, which is highly variable from year-to-year. In addition, due to a lack of target crops, and in the absence of a robust monitoring system, farmers are exposed to climate risks that limit their ability to adapt to changing climatic conditions and make informed decisions for agricultural. This subcomponent will therefore allow for the development of monitoring systems to assist water planners and farmers in decision-making related to water allocations and better agriculture management.

17. The analysis produced through the assessments will be widely disseminated through online and offline platforms, including the Pakistan Climate Change Portal (PCCP) and the AWA Portal), which will provide public access to decision-making tools and data for various stakeholders.

#### Component 2: Building an on-ground climate resilience change climate (contd. from p.140) [GEO-13/092/A68/01]

##### Subcomponent 2a: Range of experience-based, face-to-face approaches

15. This component will provide a range of experience-based, face-to-face approaches, including demonstrations in climate field schools and, field days, allowing farmers to learn and enhance their adaptive capacity with climate-resilient farming methods in the light of frequent and intense rainfall events and droughts. These activities are being implemented in collaboration with a number of public sector organizations, non-governmental organizations (NGOs), and private sector partners through Memorandum of Understanding agreements to implement extension activities using a climate-focused curriculum. This type of learning by doing will allow farmers to adopt best practices in climate-resilient farming and encourage them to share their experiences with other farmers on the use of climate resilient seeds and water. Experience has shown that climate field schools and demonstration activities are pivotal in shaping farmer’s perceptions about climate change, and have been found to highlight the potential of such activities by enhancing farmers’ yields and income.

16. The sustainability of the interventions under this component is well presented. It is expected that the training materials developed under the project can be used to scale up

### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

- The project aims to increase the resilience of 1.3 million people (including 636,000 women) in eight very poor farming districts in the Punjab and Sindh provinces. This will directly benefit farmers through improved access to climate-resilient extension services and climate-adaptive farming practices.
- Households in these districts (including 7.9 million women) will indirectly benefit from the project through information dissemination activities and awareness-raising campaigns, and from the increased agricultural productivity in the project area attributed to better adaptation strategies.
- The project area consists of eight targeted districts in two districts that were selected based on their vulnerability to climate change risks in terms of the extent of the physical area and number of households that could be affected, and areas where the capacity of rural people to adapt was most limited.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

- The project will alter farmers' behavior and governance structures in the Indus Basin through improved access to climate information, upscaling of climate-resilient extension services, and better integration of climate change adaptation into planning processes for climate change adaptation. Together, these activities will address key barriers that contribute to the high vulnerability of smallholder farmers in the Indus Basin and pave the way towards transforming the farming systems of the Indus Basin to become more resilient.
- The project will work with government stakeholders to improve the governance structures and provide information tools to planners and policymakers so they can better understand climate change risks, develop adaptation strategies, and plan for water management in the Indus Basin. The project will improve the enabling environment for supporting poor agricultural producers in transitioning to climate-resilient agriculture.


---
**Page 98**
---

21. The project will also establish new and innovative information systems and build local capacities at household and governmental levels to generate, disseminate and utilize vital data and information on climate change and its impacts on water resources and farming systems. This will be achieved through a Climate Information Portal, which will provide access to climate-resilient agriculture practices, which will be further enhanced through knowledge dissemination activities such as training workshops, field visits, the Climate Information Portal, public awareness campaigns and radio and mobile phone service messaging over communication platforms.

### 3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

25. The project will help poor farmers increase their agriculture productivity, so as to highly support food security (higher temperatures, variable rainfall), thereby stimulating growth in the local economy, improving food and nutrition security, and increasing the empowerment of women through increased income generation.

26. The project will also deliver environment-to-benefits in the form of reductions in greenhouse gases associated with improved agricultural practices. Using the Ex-Ante Carbon Balance Tool (Ex-ABC), it is estimated that the project will reduce net carbon dioxide emissions by 10,543 tonnes per year through better land-use practices, improved soil quality and the higher water availability in groundwater systems, thereby reducing greenhouse gas emissions by approximately 0.9 tonnes per hectare per year in the project area.

### 3.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: High

28. The agriculture sector in Pakistan is highly susceptible to climate impacts. The feasibility study highlights that climate change will significantly impact the agriculture sector and decrease farmers' income, as well as their food security. Many agricultural lands are landless and currently have limited adaptive capacity to adapt to implement climate-resilient farming methods. The project therefore addresses barriers faced by vulnerable farmers and helps them to adopt climate-resilient agricultural practices through improved irrigation systems and changing rainfall patterns - in order to achieve climate-resilient agriculture.

### 3.5 Country ownership Scale: High

29. Project is aligned with national priorities, and addresses the priorities identified in Pakistan's Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC). It was developed in close consultation with key government and other stakeholders, which ensures that the project aligns with sectoral priorities and policies.

30. The provincial governments of Punjab and Sindh have both shown a strong sense of ownership for the project and are committed to supporting its implementation effectively. They will channel these resources through FAO towards implementation of project activities.

### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: High

28. The economic benefits of the project are based on higher agricultural yields compared to the counterfactual, where it is expected to decline with climate change. The economic internal rate of return (IRR) for the project is estimated at 15 per cent, which is significantly higher than the hurdle rate of 10 per cent. These returns are robust to delays, changes in costs and different yield scenarios. Farmers generally have incentives to sustain the investments, as net profits are higher when using the resilient agricultural methods. However, some of the on-farm costs are

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

##### 4.1 Environmental and social risks and impacts.

The proposal emphasizes improved farming practices and water management, as these expected benefits of the project will increase the resilience of smallholder farmers to climate change. The project will also set up girls' schools and women's open schools will be set up to provide training to farmers on such practices. In addition, extension services will be provided to farmers in the target communities to improve their adaptive capacity.

Some of the potential environmental and social risks that have been identified include increased agricultural productivity and (ii) bonded labour and age-inappropriate work. Furthermore, increased agricultural production may result in increased pesticide use, and the construction of new irrigation infrastructure could lead to more intensive water use, potentially localized and temporary air and noise pollution, and health safety risks. Measures have been proposed to mitigate these risks.

The installation of evapotranspiration equipment and agrometeorological stations may require the acquisition of land by the Pakistan Meteorological Department. With respect to land acquisition, the project will follow the relevant laws and regulations and will acquire land and follow national standards to compensate them. The ESF will apply the most stringent standards of the national standard for compensation and resettlement. The proposed compensation standard complies with the GCF standard on land acquisition and involuntary resettlement.

The project triggers a number of environmental and social safeguards standards, including:

- **Age-inappropriate work:** The project will ensure that bonded labour and age-inappropriate work is not included in the non-eligibility list of activities and are excluded from project support. Project locations with bonded labour and/or child labour will be identified during the environmental and social screening process. If such locations are found, the project will not proceed; otherwise, age-appropriate work will also be advised. The ESF is advised to monitor the practice of bonded labour and child labour in the project area and ensure that such practices will be prohibited by the project in the non-eligibility list.

- **Resource efficiency and pollution prevention:** The project will not procure pesticides for agricultural purposes, except for emergency health and safety purposes. To mitigate the risk of increased use of pesticides to increase agricultural productivity, the project will provide basic occupational health and safety training on pesticide handling and storage for farmers' purposes. Additionally, the ESF has annexed guidelines on pesticide management, which will be used to guide farmers in using pesticides during the project.

### Gender policy

#### 4.2 Gender policy

- The AIF has submitted a gender assessment and thus complies with the operational guidelines of the National Gender Policy and Action Plan. The gender assessment provides the context of gender issues in Pakistan and the rural areas of Punjab and Sindh districts targeted by the project.
- Evidence was gathered in relation to the legal and regulatory framework for gender equality, women’s access to productive resources, and women’s participation in agriculture.
- It was highlighted that women have less access to services (such as education and information) compared with men. Women also face unequal opportunities in terms of land ownership and unequal opportunities along gender lines. In addition, the gender assessment outlines the roles and responsibilities of men and women within households and communities.
- The gender assessment indicates that women have more tasks related to household chores than men and less time for income-generating activities. For example, many tasks concerning livestock management and vegetable cultivation for consumption are undertaken by women. However, agricultural extension services provided by mainly male extension officers often do not reach women farmers or their families.
- The gender assessment includes opportunities presented by the project to ensure women’s participation and access to project benefits.

#### 4.3 Risks

##### Overall project assessment (medium risk):

- Consultations were conducted with both female and male farmers, selected non-governmental organizations working in the target districts, and national, provincial and district authorities.
- Stakeholder engagement through consultations also gathered evidence concerning the issues related to gender equality and women’s empowerment and their respective vulnerabilities; this evidence supplemented the gender assessment.

- The AIF has included a project-level gender action plan that is aligned with the project targets and objectives. Performance indicators with sex-disaggregated targets have been integrated and are also included in the logic framework as they relate to the project.
- Indicators for women’s access to productive resources, land ownership, and poultry through women's open schools will be provided. The performance indicator for other activities related to the lack of financial resources available to women in project areas has also been provided. The gender action plan includes financial resources for the implementation of the action plan and ensures participation of women as beneficiaries and support women as beneficiaries of the project.
- This reflects the mainstreaming of gender equality into the project activities.

- Activities under the gender action plan aim to respond to issues discussed in the gender assessment and opportunities presented by the project to ensure women’s access to project benefits. These include providing training for women farmers on poultry rearing, dairy cattle, and poultry, through women's open schools.
- This addresses some of the issues related to the lack of financial resources available to women in project areas. In addition, the project will provide access to credit facilities for women farmers by establishing a revolving fund with interest-free loans under their field schools, which will contribute to farmers’ adaptive capacity for agricultural activities.

- Implementation arrangements involve two full-time project recruited gender and nutrition specialists who are responsible for implementing the overall gender action plan for the project working with project staff in the project management unit.


---
**Page 102**
---

### Project-specific execution risks (medium risk)

- Use of data in decision-making: component 1 of the project enhances information services by providing a secure and reliable platform for crop monitoring equipment, water management systems, and training on the use of data and technologies. The technologies and systems are expected to provide real-time data and be readily available for decision-makers at all levels. However, the project may face challenges if political decisions are beyond the scope of the project. The project may face challenges if the political environment is not conducive to the implementation of the proposed security measure; the project is proposing to help establish and strengthen systems that are requested and have been identified as priorities by stakeholders in Pakistan. The project will actively facilitate the use of data/information, such as information-sharing systems, government coordination mechanisms, and other tools. The project will also ensure that the AE also commits to monitoring and measuring the extent to which policymakers use the data and information generated by the system under component 1.

- Security: In some cases, the security situation in target areas is high. The AE has identified the security situation and potential flooding in target areas as the highest risk. The local security issues will be addressed through a combination of preventive measures and mitigation strategies. If the security concerns are likely to affect staff or partners in target areas. As for flooding, the preventive measures are highly dependent on existing irrigation infrastructure. The project will introduce flood protection measures such as the construction of embankments and the introduction of crops to minimize risks from the potential flooding.

- Middlemen: The project is aware that there exists a relationship between middlemen and the target farmers which could undermine the project activities. The middlemen are often the sole source of credit to farmers and have entrenched relationships with farmers, which can make it difficult for them to access financial services. The project will provide access to financial and supply chain services through financial and value-chain literacy training. In partnership with microfinance institutions, the project will inform farmers of the cost of


---
**Page 103**
---

### Economic and financial viability (as per its funding proposal)

The economic analysis of the project shows that it is financially viable for promoting climate-resilient agriculture through project activities.

- The results analyzed in an EIRB of 18.8% per cent over a 20-year period and a financial internal rate of return of 12.6 per cent over a 10-year period.
- The analysis also shows that the project is not sensitive to cost increases, decrease/delays in project benefits, and an exclusion of environmental benefits and value of carbon credits from the benefit-cost ratio (BCR) calculation.

### GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk)

The concentration risk for this proposal on the GCF portfolio risk remains non-material and within the risk appetite in terms of concentration level, results area or single proposal.


#### Compliance (high risk)

Compliance risks this proposal as a high risk due to current uncertainty as to Pakistan's ability to meet international anti-money-laundering and countering the financing of terrorism standards of the Financial Action Task Force (FATF). This is because of the potential for significant consequences, including "black-listing" from the global financial system. The AE has indicated that it is aware of this potential risk and has committed to fairly light conditions in the project agreement. However, given the nature of the project primarily relate to training and capacity-building, and do not include such elements as large infrastructure projects, the AE believes there will be no significant consequences through the project investment in actual decision-making.

## 4.5 Results monitoring and reporting

### As an adaptation project, the interventions expect to benefit 1.3 million direct beneficiaries and 20 million indirect beneficiaries. The project will deliver benefits directly to B per cent of the targeted rural population and indirectly to 100 per cent of the targeted rural population. Monitoring and evaluation (M&E) is a key component in ensuring that the intended outcomes of the indirect beneficiaries as per the gender-disaggregated metrics for the GCF core indicator.

### Overall the funding proposal and logic framework sufficiently apply GCF land-level impact assessment methodology, including the use of data-driven approaches to identify project network indicators and the project has incorporated baseline and data collection methods that can indicate progress towards achieving the project’s intended outcomes. The project also includes a technical assistance/training-based grant project, measurements for behavioural change have been integrated into the project design.

### Regarding section H1, the logic framework covers overall with GCF standards.

### The funding proposal theory of change would benefit from further details on the causal pathways at project level and in relation to the climate rationale (which are ideally tested in implementation). The project should also provide more detail on how it will use the data/evidence generated to attribute changes to GCF investment.

### Under section H2, the project should provide: (i) impact assessment tools for data collection and recommends that the AFU(i) clearly how it identified methodologies to be applied in collaboration with the Secretariat; and (ii) provide details on the intention to conduct the final independent evaluation as an impact evaluation rather than a process performance.


---
**Page 105**

### Legal assessment

#### a)
The accreditation master agreement was signed with the AE on 8 June 2018 and became effective on 4 October 2018.

#### b)
The AE has provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals necessary for its signature and ratification to implement the agreement.

#### c)
The proposed project will be implemented in Pakistan, a country in which GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, among other things, GCF is not protected from legal actions by third parties against it or its staff. The matter has been referred for further assessment. The Secretariat submitted a draft privileges and immunities agreement to the Government of Pakistan on 28 July 2015. No response from the Government of Pakistan has yet been received.

#### d)
The idea of the Independent Redress Mechanism and the independent integrity that have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where the GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. The Secretariat will therefore consider whether to provide such a mechanism only after GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expatriation in the country or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

### 4.7 List of proposed conditions (including legal)

#### a)
In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that any approval by the Board be made subject to the following conditions:

- Signature of the accreditation agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval; and
- Completion of legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the Secretariat.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP108

## Proposal name: Transforming the Indus Basin with Climate Resilient Agriculture and Water Management

### Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

### Project/programme size: Small

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Impact potential Scale: High

#### 1.1 Introduction
The Indus basin is the major breadbasket for Pakistan. While the fertile land enables agriculture to take place, the water of the glacier-fed Indus River system, supplied through an extensive network of canals and distributaries, is critical in this semi-arid and semi-arid environment. As a western Himalayan river, the Indus is also affected by the southwest monsoon and snowmelt from glaciers and snow ice. The area surrounding the Indus Basin has experienced drought and flood are known hazards. Several severe floods have been recorded in history but have not been experienced in Pakistan during the past 15 years.

#### 1.2 Current Situation
In spite of receiving flows from the Indus River system, Pakistan is one of the five most stressed countries for water resources. The total annual flow of the river has declined from 5,237m³ in 1986 to 1,188 m³ in 2018. In recent times, the country has been facing the adverse impacts of climate change. The average temperature is increasing and the maximum temperature in Pakistan, especially in the Punjab and Sindh provinces. Available meteorological data and records show that the mean annual temperature for these two provinces increased by 0.6°C during the past century. With such an effect from global warming, the Himalayan glaciers and snow in the mountains are melting fast. Unfortunately, the resultant change in the flow of the Indus River system is not in line with the increase in demand. The additional run-off from glacier/snow melt is not found to be in synchronisation with the crop calendar.

#### 1.3 Future Projections
As elsewhere in arid and semi-arid conditions, the target provinces of Punjab and Sindh are likely to face much increased surface temperature; a reduction in diurnal temperature range, for example, from 20°C to 5°C; and an increase in evaporation rates leading to a reduction in availability of water at both spatial and temporal scales. Higher potential evaporative transpiration (ET) rates will also lead to greater requirements for irrigation systems which are economically attractive to poor farmers. With crop agriculture being sensitive to any of these climate-change-induced phenomena in the arid and semi-arid Indus basin, a simultaneous occurrence of more than one such phenomenon is likely to be more frequent in future decades.

#### 1.4 Concluding Remarks
Acknowledging the fact that between 60% and 75 per cent of the total flow of the Indus River system is supplied by glaciers and snow ice; the long-term availability of water in the Indus Basin irrigation system is at severe risk. Moreover, the integrated monsoon occurring


---
**Page 107**
---

under climate change will result in increased rainfall? and subsequent run-off causing floods. The ET-related problems are projected to result in a 6 per cent increase in crop water requirement by 2025. Moreover, an increase in temperature will lead to a decrease in crop yield by 2030. These adverse impacts of climate change on agriculture are expected to make agriculture in Pakistan is likely to face devastating impacts that will affect the entire 207.8 million people who depend directly or indirectly on agriculture for their livelihoods. In particular, Sindh provinces depend on agriculture, the projected adverse impact will be severe in these two provinces.

5. Agriculture in Pakistan is critical for the economy, its overall food security, people's employment and even export revenues. The contribution of the agriculture sector to gross domestic product is 19.8 per cent. Agricultural production provides for over 70 per cent of national income and employs more than half of the country’s total labour force and about one-third of the total population. The sector employs over 60 per cent of the labour force and generates about 75 per cent of employment in rural areas. Agriculture is also a major source of export earnings, food and services in Pakistan. However, the agrarian economy not only engages millions of smallholder households, the majority of which are in Punjab and Sindh, it also draws its lifeline from the availability of water resources for irrigation and other uses. The country’s agricultural sector faces many challenges. Given the impact of climate change, not only will food production be severely affected, but rural people's livelihoods will be at risk.

6. Such a scenario under climate change warrants multifaceted immediate actions on the ground so that the Government of Pakistan, including the respective provincial governments, will have to take up the challenge and respond in an effective manner. The country can benefit by gaining advanced knowledge and science while poor smallholder farmers will benefit from climate-resilient agricultural practices based on scientific information, and extension support to irrigate their crop-folds based on scientific rationale and self-sustained irrigation management under a new policy regime.

7. The objective of the proposed project is to transform agriculture in the Indus basin by increasing resilience among the most vulnerable farmers and strengthening the Government of Pakistan's capacity to support commitments to adapt. The specific objectives of the project include:

(a) To develop the country’s capacity to generate and use information relevant to coping in agriculture and water management by putting in place state-of-the-art technologies;

(b) To build farmer’s resilience through skills, knowledge and technologies; and

(c) To create a wider enabling environment for continuous adaptation and expanded sustainable irrigation management.

9. The project, if approved, will be implemented in five districts of Punjab (i.e. Dera Ghazi Khan, Khushab, Narowal, Rahim Yar Khan and Sheikhupura), and two districts of Sindh province (i.e. Badin, Sanghar and Umer Kot). The proposal presents a workplan spanning ten years to accomplish the tasks, organized in three work streams.

10. The project aims at delivering institutional strengthening at all levels, especially the data acquisition and analytical capacity and human resources development of key institutions dealing with climate change adaptation and sustainable irrigation management. This will generate services for public use, which will indirectly benefit 16 million people.

The proportion of precipitation falling as rain is expected to increase from the 1971-2000 average of 58 per cent to 2031-2100 average of 66 to 75 per cent.


---
**Page 108**
---

people (1% million are women) within the target districts as well as others outside the target districts in Pakistan.

The project will directly benefit 1.3 million people representing about 200,000 households. The number of direct beneficiaries corresponds to about 5.9 per cent of the total population living in the target districts. The project is expected to contribute significantly to increased food supply and nutrition, in addition to taking preparatory measures upon receipt of climate information and warnings. An estimated 46,000 women will benefit from training and extension services (e.g., through community-based groups, farmer field schools, and training). The focus of field trainings will be the use of agricultural advisories and early warning systems for climate change adaptation. These measures are expected to not only enhance production by practicing appropriate agronomic measures but also guide farmers at local level in their decision-making process on how to prevent climate information services and equitable water allocation at local levels.

### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential

**Scale: High**

The water resource represented by the Indus basin is a lifeline for millions of people in Pakistan. However, due to the expansion of irrigated agricultural activities, gradual deterioration of the canal irrigation system due to inappropriate management, prevailing scarcity of water resources, and climate change, the Indus basin has become vulnerable which is related to climate variability and change, all individually or jointly simultaneously contribute to a water crisis in the region. The project will address these issues by promoting knowledge-based practices have been applied to water management. This project entails a shift from traditional irrigation systems towards more sustainable and efficient water use for agricultural production, which will contribute to resilience to climate change. As such, there is room for innovation in terms of developing an integrated water resource management system on the basis of science and advanced knowledge which, with the advent of greater scientific understanding of the water regime in the Indus basin, could have been implemented earlier.

The project is aimed at both the macro-level (i.e., institutional capacity and policy integration) as well as micro-level (i.e., individual farmers and households). The former will cover only eight vulnerable districts, which represent only 2.6 million of a total of 14 million people living in the target areas. However, the latter project has significant potential for scaling up, which is likely to be realized in future years given that the relevant stakeholders are already involved in the project and have shown interest in decision-making for safeguarding agriculture under climate change. The extension of resilient climate agriculture (CRA) and on-farm water management (DFWM) techniques in particular can potentially contribute significantly to food security, livelihoods, and income generation across the country.

The project emphasizes knowledge and learning, starting from the institutional level and including water users (e.g., farmers) at the grassroots level. The proposed project places particular emphasis on the importance of promoting a culture of innovation, sharing of knowledge and learning and to provide climate-informed extension and advisory services. The emphasis is also placed on the use of participatory approaches in the design and implementation which are conducive to their knowledge uptake. The tools and techniques that are likely to be employed are all either tested in Pakistan conditions and/or have received farmers' vetting

*The application of state-of-the-art technologies for generating information on ET-based water budgeting is indeed new in Pakistan; however, it has already been applied elsewhere.*

**Page 109**

13 Sustainable development potential

Scale: High

The project directly contributes to meeting the following Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) in Pakistan:

### 15. Suitable platforms are chosen to capture lessons learned and to share across stakeholders and governments. These include a regional portal for sharing information (through participation in regional/international forums), to learn from good practices.

#### The project will enhance the capacities of relevant institutions in terms of:
- Climate data generation and analysis
- Issuance of water/agricultural advisories
- Skills enhancement in climate change adaptation, including the use of mobile phone-based protocol and messaging, among others. Efforts will be made to fine-tune relevant policy and regulatory frameworks to ensure that they are aligned with the principles of equitable access to water resources and equitable management of water to optimize water usage in ameliorating drought conditions (and to avoid over-exploitation of groundwater resources, which is a major concern at the cost of deteriorating aquifers in water-scarce “tails” of the canal irrigation system).

#### The project will establish the necessary budgetary allocations with both federal and provincial governments to ensure continued delivery of the enabling conditions necessary to continue the implementation of the National Water Policy (NWP) 2018. These arrangements should contribute towards creating a common understanding regarding OGWAM, which should encourage stakeholders to participate in the management of water resources.

All of the above indicate that adequate arrangements are duly considered in the creation of an enabling environment for the project and its sustainability in irrigated farming systems across Pakistan, even beyond completion of the project.


---
**Page 110**
---

### SDG-2: Zero hunger by means of food production, food security and nutrition;
### SDG-5: Gender equality (concerns regarding women and minority groups);
### SDG-13: Enhanced climate action (by reducing vulnerability and increasing adaptive capacities).

The project is also likely to contribute indirectly to the following goal:
- **SDG 1: Ending poverty (through incremental increase in income)**

* Improved water management will contribute directly to increased productivity for participating poor farming households, especially due to improved management practices against drought-related crop losses. The additional income generated by these households will contribute to an increased return to labour and indirect contribution to local economies by means of additional gains in relation to agricultural input supply, trading and agro-processing.

* Water efficiency is likely to be a positive externality of the management of water at farm level through improved irrigation practices. This will reduce the need for groundwater abstraction from the "heads" and reduced dependence on groundwater abstraction at the water-deprived "tails". The latter will contribute directly to reducing greenhouse gas emissions (estimated to be 0.9 tonnes per hectare per annum in the project area). Improved water regimes will contribute to improved soil quality.

* Improved water management is likely to have a positive impact, particularly from intra-household inequitable food distribution, to the disadvantage of women in particular. Therefore, productivity gains essentially have the potential to contribute to the improvement of women's nutrition and health status. The project will also benefit directly due to the reduction in drought-related crop loss. Moreover, 60,000 women who are working as water users' representatives (WURs) will be trained through a capacity-building programme developed for their inclusion in extension activities. This will not only help them address issues of gender equality and empowerment but also contribute to their personal empowerment, simultaneously inspiring other women producers in their neighbourhood. The outreach programme is expected to influence women's knowledge in the target areas, which will also help to achieve social co-benefits.

23. **The equitable distribution of water on the basis of enhanced ET, knowledge and local planning on crop-rotation and irrigation will help achieve greater social harmony and reduction of social conflicts between farmers who are currently engaged in "water grabbing" at the "heads", resulting in social coherence and more equitable water regimes.**

24. The project proposes adding weather data stations under the Pakistan Meteorological Department (PMD) to provide real-time information on climate conditions, which is not yet provided by the PMD, which demonstrated a strong commitment to invest in operation and maintenance costs both during and beyond the project timeline. Such investments are vital towards ensuring sustainability of improvements being made under the project.

25. In view of the above analysis, it appears that the sustainable development potential of the project is high.


### 1.4 Needs of the recipient
**Scale: High**


---
**Page 111**
---

20. Although Punjab and Sindh are known as the predominant breadbasket for the 207.8 million Pakistani population, about 90 million people in the two provinces are estimated to be exposed to risks associated with climate change. In addition to such a huge population at risk of climate-related disasters, these provinces also have a large number of smallholder farmers living in poverty, leaving little capacity to adapt and overcome vulnerability. The plight of the smallholders is particularly important to note. Their adaptive capacity is significantly lower compared to other farmers.

21. The current ranking of the country, at 147 out of 188 countries ranked in the Human Development Index, clearly indicates that the climate vulnerable population in rural Punjab and Sindh needs immediate attention. These provinces are also among the most food insecure with limited production potential. The project targets eight vulnerable districts, which have a total of 16 million hectares under irrigation (of which only 50 per cent is irrigated). Of these, 24 per cent (and 72 per cent in Sindh) do not have a secure food supply, therefore experiencing occasional food insecurity. These farmers are also among the most vulnerable to drought and receive disproportionately low levels of nutrients compared to males.

26. Most agricultural producers are tenant farmers. Therefore, an excessive drought year results in their further economic marginalization. The increase in drought intensity in the recent past forced poor people to sell cheap labour in the form of bonded and child labour in both the provinces.

28. The Government of Pakistan has been tackling food insecurity and abject poverty, especially among smallholder farmers, for decades with limited success as the magnitude of adverse impacts often overwhelms the government's efforts. The nationally determined contribution (NDC) targets a reduction in per capita greenhouse gas emissions by 10 tonnes per annum to meet adaptation needs. The financial allocation needed to adequately address the complex set of issues in the Indus basin is not immediately available, due to a variety of investment constraints. However, it has been estimated that between 25 and 30 per cent of climate-related expenditure appears to be between 5.8 and 7 per cent of the total expenditures in the federal budget.

30. Since many of the capacity-building services are targeted at acquiring public goods and services (such as technologies, knowledge and information needed by vulnerable farmers), there is limited evidence that these investments have been translated into a greater chance of private sector crowding out. The absence of alternative sources of financing facilitates mobilization of GCF finance.

31. IFCG funding is multiplied, as proposed, the institutional and policy strengthening and extension related to CGIAR and OFWEM will remain specific barriers to the development of a new regime of knowledge-driven and climate-resilient agricultural practices, leading to greater water governance challenges in the Indus basin and further exacerbating the vulnerability of the Indus basin. The project offers an opportunity to remove critical barriers in irrigated agriculture that are preventing smallholder farmers from making more sustainable and resilient choices to tackle drought. The role of both climate-related agricultural interventions with adequate lead time and climate-resilient agricultural practices is crucial for the project to address climate-induced drought in the irrigated agricultural landscape of the Indus basin. The opportunity to mobilize GCF funds for such purposes should not be wasted.

32. From the above analysis, the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) finds the merits of the proposed project and recommends its approval.

1. **Country ownership** Scale: High

2. The development vision for Pakistan is expressed in the 2011 National Framework for Economic Growth, which emphasizes the need to address climate change in order to achieve the vision. The framework identifies key sectors that are vulnerable to climate change, paying particular attention to the agricultural, water and energy sectors. Meanwhile, Pakistan's National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) also highlights the vulnerability of the agriculture sector as well emphasized. In the NDC, Pakistan indicates its commitment to achieve the following:

3. * Behavioural changes in consumption patterns;
* Application of scientific and technological knowledge in adaptations at the national, provincial and sectoral levels;
* Integration of climate change concerns in economic planning and decision-making;
* Utilization of climate information at all levels; and
* Development of low carbon emission scenarios with possible options for implementation.

4. The objectives of such policies in national documents are in full coherence with the country's national development plans, policies and strategies.

5. The Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) is an international agency that has been working closely with Pakistan since 1960 to improve food security worldwide. FAO is the proposed accredited entity for the project and has a track record in the advancement of agricultural production in South Asian countries, especially in Pakistan. Since its beginning its involvement in Pakistan, FAO has implemented more than 25 projects covering an area of over 14 million hectares, evidence of its experience in the field of agricultural development in the country.

6. The agencies working towards implementation of the project are also experienced in delivering public goods and services in Pakistan. The Pakistan Meteorological Department (PMD), with a long history of providing weather forecasts to farmers for understanding climate variability and change. The existing weather stations are maintained by PMD, which has been collecting data on meteorological parameters since 1902. Other institutions such as Pakistan Agricultural Research Council, the National Agricultural Research Council, Pakistan Council for Research in Water Resources, Agricultural and Irrigation Departments of Punjab and Sindh, and the University of Agriculture Faisalabad have also been involved. These institutions will lend their technical expertise and personnel as well as local skills and equipment to support the project.

7. The existing climate change platform established by the PMD and other relevant institutions for the dissemination of climate information and advisories, the existing platform provided by the Pakistan Meteorological Department (PMD) and the recently established National Climate Change Council, established under the Pakistan Climate Change Act (2016) will be involved in the project. The Ministry of Climate Change has also proposed further collaboration with major projects led by the World Bank and other development partners in the Indus basin, as well as non-governmental organizations to deliver capacity-building activities.

8. The project has met the requirement for engaging civil society organizations and for identification of necessary actions through a participatory process. The inclusion of a separate goal for gender equality and women's empowerment indicates that gender monitoring was fully considered in the participatory appraisal stage.

## Efficiency and effectiveness

### Scale: High

The project budget estimates a cost of USD 47.69 million, where USD 34.99 million is requested as grant from GCF. The co-financing committed for the project is estimated at USD 12.7 million with a co-financing ratio of about 1:2.8 – which is quite impressive. As explained earlier, the country’s current level of investment in agriculture and food security in the NDC are inadequate to face the challenges of exacerbated drought resulting from climate change. The fact that the project has been included as one of the priority projects by the World Development Indicators signifies that the country has been struggling to invest at a level desirable for addressing these issues. It is also important to note that the majority of the revenue to be generated under the project are almost entirely related to provision of public goods and services, which makes it highly sustainable in terms of financial viability.

The project, if approved and subsequently implemented, will safeguard against a potential loss of production to the value of USD 66 million, when compared to an “no action scenario”. This is the cost of avoided loss. However, the losses likely to be incurred due to degradation of soil quality and water resources are estimated at USD 105 million in terms of avoided loss. In addition, increased production from the business as usual baseline would probably result in a reduction of greenhouse gas emissions by about 247 thousand tons CO₂e. However, the potential savings from burning less fuel for groundwater irrigation – considered to be the last resort in the project area – could also lead to an increase in methane and nitrous oxide emissions. All these aspects suggest that the project is on a strong financial footing.

Indeed, the overall net present value and internal rate of return for the project are positive for both these cases. This suggests that, with the GCF co-financing, the savings from the project will be sufficient to cover the cost of building housing units which will make an incremental net profit, in addition to meeting their sustainability development goal-related requirements.

The project appears cost-effective when one estimates a delivery cost of USD 39 per direct beneficiary. The reach of the project coverage appears comparable to GCF-financed projects that have been implemented in other countries. The average cost per direct target beneficiaries can be assessed by comparing the cost spent per beneficiary for the Sindh resilience project, which exhibits USD 26 per beneficiary, while the Strengthening Markets for Agriculture and Rural Transformation project in Punjab (SMART) project costs USD 130 per beneficiary. The average cost per beneficiary for the proposed project


---
**Page 114**
---

appears close to the lower end of the spread in recently implemented projects in the Indus basin.

The project is targeting the application of best practices, be it in institutional strengthening or in advancing CRA and OFWM on the ground. The best example of the application of best practices would be the use of modern analytical methods for water distribution by adopting modern analytical methods, which will eventually be put into practice to bring better governance in the distribution of irrigation water at various scales and also to address prevailing inefficiencies in the system.

The project is expected to have a significant impact on the agricultural sector. The technologies involved include various technologies, some of which are state-of-the-art and will be extremely useful for national level projects. The project will also provide a platform for the sustained use of the equipment for weather data acquisition beyond the project timeline; there is a commitment from the donor to cover the operation and maintenance costs, thus improving effectiveness of the service.

In view of the above assessment, it appears to the ITAP that the overall effectiveness and efficiency of the project is high.


## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The ITAP recommends the Board approve the project without conditions.

1. **Introduction**

### 1.1 Project Introduction from Gender Perspective

The proposed project, "Transforming the Indus Basin with Climate Resilient Agriculture and Climate-Smart Water Management," aims to strengthen the resilience of agricultural producers in Pakistan's Indus River Basin in the face of climate change. The Basin spans over 18 million hectares, the vast majority of which is cultivated for food production, providing about 60 percent of Pakistan’s total water supply and more than 90 percent of Pakistan’s food production. The Basin is inhabited by more than 30 million people, and approximately 75 percent of them are directly dependent on agriculture.

The project will target the most vulnerable districts or the two major provinces of the Basin: Punjab and Sindh.

At the national level, the project will engage the government institutions responsible for monitoring weather and climate change with tools and mechanisms to gather, interpret, and disseminate information related to climate change. The information will be shared with the authorities, in charge of agriculture and water resources, as well as with farmers’ organizations and other stakeholders.

Female officials with technical background are very few at the relevant institutions, and the female technical community has to be strengthened.

Trainings on on-farm techniques will benefit 100,000 farmers from the target districts and emphasize adaptive agricultural practices for crops, livestock, and major crops and animals. It takes care of jointly by women and men through mostly segregated tasks, but most interventions rarely give satisfactory attention to distinct contributions of women farmers and to the benefits of coordination between women and men.

Livestock is a neglected sub-sector compared to crop agriculture, but it is very important one in terms of national economy as well as food, nutritional and financial security of the farmers. Although women are in charge of the animals on a daily basis with little involvement of men, it is the latter that hold the decision-making power over livestock resources and their use. This situation is not favorable for the empowerment of women. Field consultations strongly suggest that female farmers are more open than men to cultivating new crops and raising livestock whose economic significance is yet unproven, as long as they do not need to justify their actions in front of male family members. Women’s access to land, water resources, security, are such crops and have been embraced already by some women farmers in the region. They offer alternative livelihoods for women who are increasingly migrating from agriculture into non-agricultural activities and into urban areas.

One of the largest obstacles in leveraging these aspects of female farmers is their limited access to knowledge and resources, which is linked to their weak social capital.

The project will promote the distribution of timely information on climate and agriculture as well as sharing of knowledge and skills through their direct involvement in the project with other farmers in the Basin. The rural real estate market is dominated by men, and land ownership is suited for men, but not for women. It also has diminished expectation of education for women than for men, leading to a lack of access to information and resources. This situation is further exacerbated by the National Climate Change Policy of the importance of gender mainstreaming, recently launched activities in the Basin, and the need to address the increasing demand for information tailored to content and skills required for agriculture. These factors create gender-differentiated preferences in terms of mode of communication, which need to be taken into account for effective implementation of the project.

Your women farmers are a valuable asset to your communities. By providing them with access to information technology; they represent valuable assets that the entire female community can benefit


---
**Page 122**
---

from.

The project will also raise awareness on various subjects related to adaptation to climate change, including gender, along the entire value chain – from policy, public administration, education to agricultural input, product, credit and insurance and consumption – so that adaptation to climate change by farmers and other stakeholders is more inclusive.

Climate-resilient agriculture will contribute to food and nutritional security, leading to climate resilient communities. Women empowered through more dynamic participation in agriculture would result in more gender-neutral food distribution within households and better health for both women and children, especially for girls, reducing malnutrition and stunting that prevail in the rural areas of Pakistan.


# 1.2 Objectives of the Assessment

## 1.2.1 Rationale
Gender equality and women's empowerment constitute fundamental dimensions of human development. As a significant portion of the humanity does not enjoy progress, we cannot claim to have achieved human development.* The importance of engaging all stakeholders – women, men, minority groups, youth, and children – in agriculture is widely recognized as essential for sustainable, and Pakistan has ratified many international agreements and goals related to the subject, including the Universal Declaration of Human Rights, the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), and the Beijing Platform for Action. The Government officially committed itself to achieving gender equality in all facets of life.

The Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) recognizes that gender equality is key to its mandate to achieve food security for all. As evidenced by FAO Policy on Gender Equality* and Regional Strategy and Action Plan for Asia and the Pacific, 2017-2035*, FAO sees rural women as a critical agent of change whose engagement is indispensable to meet the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), not only the one on gender, but any of the 17 SDGs.


## 1.2.2 Objective
The objective of the Gender Assessment is to provide the foundation for effective gender mainstreaming in the Climate Change Fund (CCF) project: "Transforming the Indus Basin with Climate Resilient Agriculture and Climate-Smart Water Management." The Analysis examines the socioeconomic conditions of women and men, their access to resources, and the barriers they face in accessing these resources. It also allows a strategic approach to the integration of gender dimensions into the project, summarized as the Gender Action Plan (GAP). The underlying theory of change is: the project will create effective opportunities for women and men to participate equally in agriculture and water management processes, which will allow communities in the Indus Basin to adapt to climate change in a sustainable manner.


# 2. METHODOLOGY
The Gender Assessment focuses on the situation of rural women in the areas targeted by the project: Punjab Districts of Dera Ghazi Khan, Kharian, Lodhran, Multan; and Muzaffargarh, and South Districts of Badin, Sanghar and Umerkot.

It draws on the information obtained through consultations with farmers and provincial government officials, gender analysis of the county recently produced* and other relevant information sources – such as research articles, policies and statistics on agriculture, climate change and gender – contributions from the FAO Pakistan Office and Regional Office for Asia and the Pacific (RAP).

The purpose of the consultations was to extract key issues pertaining to the lives of female farmers in the rural areas through basic questions on the topic (see Section 8.1). The key issues were then assessed in detail with respect to their relevance to the target districts of Punjab, Sindh and Balochistan: Overview and 6 Gender in Punjab and Sindh. The detailed assessment led to identification of risk and opportunities for women farmers in each district, formulation of gender-sensitive policy recommendations, establishment of implementation modalities and specifying beneficiaries (see Section 7).

A total of 696 farmers in the target districts were consulted, of which 543 were women. Women’s views on various issues were collected through meetings exclusively for women, participants and organizers also included representatives from the national and provincial governments, civil society organizations, the institutions consulted were the Ministry of Climate Change (MoCC), Irrigation Research Institute, International Food Policy Research Institute (IFPRI), National Agriculture Research Council (NARC), intergovernmental organizations such as the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP), Pakistan Support Group (PSG), Potsher Organization for Development Advocacy, Pakistan Poverty Alleviation Fund (PPAF), and other national and international organizations including the provincial agriculture departments (Punjab and Sindh). On-Farm Water Management Department in Multan (Punjabi), Agriculture Extension Wing of Department of Agriculture in Multan (Punjabi), Livestock and Dairy Development Department in Multan (Punjabi), Animal Husbandry Department in Multan (Punjabi) and women’s organizations NGOs working in the target districts (see Section 8.2).

To the greatest extent possible, the Assessment is based on official statistics and published research findings pertaining to target districts and field consultations conducted in those districts. Where relevant information was not available for a particular district or province, it has been extrapolated from the entire target provinces, or of the whole nation. Where no such information is available, the Assessment may refer to the general consensus among the professionals in the field or anecdotes.

### 3. RELIGIOUS, LEGAL AND ADMINISTRATIVE FRAMEWORK

#### 3.1 Protection of Women and Gender Equality: Pakistan

The holy Qur’an establishes complete and genuine equality between women and men.* Not only the holy Qur'an*, but also the Islamic law (Sharia) guarantees equal rights for both sexes. The Pakistan Ordinance (1947) established Pakistan’s national order, and at the same time, granted full suffrage to women in all matters of public life. The 1956 Constitution of Pakistan provided for universal adult elections, and the Constitution of Pakistan (1973) defined that there will be no discrimination on the basis of sex alone and that “steps shall be taken to ensure full participation of women in all spheres of national life.” Pakistan ratified several international conventions related to gender equality. These include: The International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights (1966), the Beijing Platform of Action


---
**Page 124**
---

### The National Policy for Development and Empowerment of Women (2010)

The National Policy for Development and Empowerment of Women was formulated in 2010 to formalize the education of the National Policy on Development and Empowerment of Women. The momentum was further strengthened by Criminal Law (Amendment) Act (2008), Protection of Women from Domestic Violence Act (2005), Prevention of Anti-Women Practices - Criminal Law (Third Amendment) Act (2011), and Gender Crime Cell was established within the National Police Service Commission. The government has also made efforts to widely publicize the data.

Pakistan Vision 2025: One Nation vision proposes seven pillars for the future of the country, the first of which is "People First": developing social and human capital and empowering women associated with SDG goals of poverty, health, education and gender. A comprehensive national policy on gender, however, does not exist to date.

#### 3.2 Protection of Women and Gender Equality: Punjab and Sindh

The Punjab Government created a Women Development Department in 2012 together with its first Women Commissioner. The department was established to address social and economic issues faced by women in the province. The actions taken include (i) the amendment of Land Revenue Laws to facilitate the accession of women to land, (ii) the enactment of the Punjab Anti-Harassment Act 2013, (iii) the Punjab Act Against Women Act; (iv) the enactment of the 2014 Punjab Commission on Status of Women Act; and, (v) the subsequent establishment of the Commission. While initiatives for non-farming women outnumber those for farming women, the department has provided training to 53,789 women through training, and 12,000 shepherds and sheep/goats were distributed to them. In addition, 110,000 plots were allocated on joint ownership of husbands and wives.

In 1976 the Women Development Cell was established in the Planning and Development Department of Sindh with a mandate to work through an institutionalized system. Following its downsizing, the Women Development Department was created in 2003 to focus on women's empowerment and gender equality; it has produced a Provincial Policy for the Development of Women

# 3.3.2: Gender and Agriculture Policy

The draft Agriculture and Food Security policy defines the government’s primary function as a regulator and facilitator, which creates an enabling and proactive environment for the following goals:

(i) inclusive growth in agriculture and food security;

(ii) improvement of resource scarcity and degradation issues particularly related to land and water; and,

(iii) rapid productivity gains. The draft policy recognizes that women are key actors in agriculture and their role is significant on gender or, it suggests targeted productivity enhancement programs for landless, small farmers and livestock breeders with a specific focus on women: rural poultry, bee keeping, certified nurseries, tree seedlings, etc.

The draft policy also recognizes that women’s roles as food producers are indeed well known around the world as tasks compatible with women's household responsibilities.


# 3.3.2: Gender and Livestock Policy

In comparison with crop agriculture, livestock tends to be neglected, although half of value-added in the agricultural sector comes from livestock. It also serves as a life-sustaining mechanism for many rural farmers by acting as: savings; readily accessible cash tools; and, means for food and nutrition security. Most of the regulatory frameworks of the animal data from the 19th century, however, when rural and market contexts were different, and those that exist today are based on operational guidelines that exist on vaccination, breed improvement or veterinary health maintenance.


# 3.3.2: Gender and Climate Change Policy

The National Climate Change Policy formulated in September 2013 recognizes women as central agents of change and emphasizes the need for their full participation of women and female gender equality in all policies, initiatives and decisions relating to climate change. At the same time, it acknowledges the vulnerability of women during extreme weather events and disasters, and consequently, the need to address these issues through a gender-sensitive approach. The policy also recognizes that women contribute change adaptation at national and regional levels, motivated by its recognition of women’s contribution to National Forests Management.

# Social Participation of Pakistan Women

## Rural Women’s Voice in Politics

About a fifth of parliamentary seats is held by women in Pakistan; women’s political representation is relatively strong in the region. The female parliamentarians have been instrumental in bringing about several policy developments. In 2015, they successfully prevented attacks using acid against women.[1] But further integration of female farmers’ concerns and needs into the political agenda remains insufficient.

In October 2017, Islamabad hosted the ILO Asia Pacific Women’s Conference on Rural Women’s Empowerment. The conference recognized women working in farms or with livestock as agriculture workers and called for the development of a national policy to address their concerns. No tangible results have come about as of early 2018.

As the Ministry of Finance put it, “agriculture is the lifeblood of Pakistan’s economy,”[2] contributing to 19 percent of the Gross Domestic Product.[3] When we consider its role in providing raw material, such as cotton and wool for textile production, and food grains for domestic consumption, agriculture’s importance becomes even more apparent. Footwear exports totalled nearly USD $16 billion, equal to 61 percent of all merchandise exports.[4] No major crop is cultivated and harvested by men alone; and crop harvesting is almost exclusively done by women in the rural areas.

Women are absent while that cannot be said for the absence of men.[5] Existing policies, plans and programmes are not gender-sensitive. Women’s participation in decision-making processes is minimal rather perfunctorily about it, if at all. In the rural areas, the landowners, who are all male, exercise power and influence over women and their families; and landless farmers, as financial and other resources are concentrated among them. They are also absent from the political system of the country, more than half of the total seats in the national assembly are claimed by them.

## Community-Based Organizations

Social capital, which includes social community networks, is crucial in bringing about changes to women’s lives; it gives bargaining power in formal negotiations, provides resources, a base for bartering and information sharing. In Pakistan, however, due to the lack of education, poverty, and illiteracy, community networks are largely absent in the rural areas among women.

In the targeted districts of Punjab and Sindh, 22709 Community-Based Organizations (CBOs) and 54 Local Support Groups (LSGs) were established by various NGOs across the Rural Social Level Organisations in Pakistan.[6] had been developed by various NGO at the most local administrative level (Union Council) with a cumulative membership of 340,000 as on May 2017. The organizations may be segregated by gender or mixed among the CBOs; 59 percent were exclusively for women, 19 percent mixed, the rest were for men.

### 4.3. Female Professionals

In accordance with the social status of women and the opportunities that it grants, female professionals are scarce in technical fields related to the project. The government jobs related to gender are also dominated by men.

The Ministry of Climate Change employs a female and 39 male professionals at the national level.44 The Pakistan Meteorological Department, which operates at the national level, has 5 female and 50 male staff members.45 Pakistan Council of Research in Water Resources has 4 female and 18 male professionals at the national level.46 The Irrigation Department employs a female and 27 male professionals in Sindh.47 The Indus River System Authority counts no females and four male professionals.48 Faisalabad District has one female professional, while all other districts have zero women employed by Office in Lahore.49 And 1 female and 35 male district officers are deployed at district level.50 No female and an average of 27 males are employed as irrigation engineers in Sindh.51 No female irrigation professional is employed at the Irrigation Departments of Punjab.52

# 4.3: Information Technology

The Ministry of Information Technology and Telecommunication has 2 female and 29 male professionals. Among the managers at Punjab Information Technology Board, one is a female (12% are women), who is the Chief Finance Officer. No similar department appears to form part of the Government of Sindh.

The female-male ratio at the Department of Computer Science, University of Agriculture, Faisalabad, was 1 to 14 for teaching staff and 1 out of 236 or 1.1% for students, close to an even distribution between genders. Information Technology University has over 5 females and 28 male members in the IT field among its 59 teaching staff. University Institute of Information Technology at Pr Mehr Ali Shah Arid Agriculture University has a female-male ratio of 3:4. The Punjab Agricultural University, Department of Computer Science and Computer Science of the University of Veterinary and Animal Sciences, Lahore counted no female students or faculty in their respective departments. It is expected that the younger generation is likely to become much more gender balanced in the IT professional arena.

# Gender in Rural Pakistan: Overview

The vast majority of the rural population in Pakistan depend on natural resources for their livelihood, but poor women are disproportionately affected by poverty and lack access to education and other segments of the population. They do not assume official roles and have little decision-making power in the public sphere. The gender gap is further exacerbated by the fact that women bear a disproportionate burden of household responsibilities.

The practice prevails due to cultural traditions that accord unequal opportunities along the gender line throughout life, from birth through old age. This inequality has serious consequences for women, further exacerbating the gap with the rest of the population. Their lives are characterized by (i) poverty from exploitative wages; (ii) health from indoor use of solid fuel for cooking, lack of safe water supplies when exposed to contaminated water sources and poor sanitation facilities; (iii) limited access to education in food distribution within households; (iv) insufficient exploitation and appreciation of their knowledge, skills and contributions; and (v) lack of autonomy.

The Human Development Index (HDI) is at 0.543, 147th among 188 countries and territories. The Gender Inequality Index value is 0.546, 139th out of 159 countries; the Gender Development Index value is 0.288, 151st out of 159 countries with the lowest equality in HDI achievement between men and women. Given that these measurements are for the
