### 5.1 Basic Statistics

#### 5.1.1 Population
Women in Pakistan represent 49 percent of the population,[2] and well over half of the women live in the rural areas,[3] comprising nearly one-third of the total population.

#### 5.1.2 Literacy and Education
The urban and rural gap in literacy appears widening, as well as that of gender.[4] Girls' education is limited; only about a third of girls aged five to nine years old are enrolled in school. The proportion of the national population, aged ten years and older who have ever attended school, was 62 percent for men and 51 percent for women in 2017. The literacy rate among young adults (aged 15–24) is 93 percent for men and 72 percent among women.[5] In 2016, the national estimate for out-of-school ratio for primary school-age children was over 20 percent and that for girls nearly 30 percent.[6] Over a quarter of adult women (aged 25–49) have never attended any level of education. The National Education Policy 2017 to Free and Compulsory Education Act enacted in 2012 mandates school attendance and guarantees free education until 16 years of age.

#### 5.1.3 Poverty and Wealth
Poverty in the country has not seen a steady decline in the past five decades, and a large proportion of the population hovers around the poverty line; more than half of the national population had experienced poverty for ten or more years prior to 2012.[7] Poverty and wealth in the rural areas are household-level phenomena that are often linked with gender roles. Women play a major role, aided by male relatives who accompany her when the need arises, such as traveling.[8] The most fundamental reason for women's poverty is their lack of access to land and other productive resources. Landless women are more likely to be poor than landowning women;[9] shercrapowers (who are without land) than among landowners, and the majority of the rural poor are landless.[10]

#### 5.1.4 Marriage
Many girls are married off at a young age to much older men without their consent, particularly in the rural areas. In 2016, 18 percent of women aged 18–23 (in the country [urban and rural Pakistan]) were married before turning 18,[11] with a decline observed over time.[12]

# 5.2 Labour Division and Decision Making

## 5.2.1 One Agriculture

A common perception exists for division of labour according to gender, although no household may be exactly alike. While some work may be performed by both women and men, certain tasks are often considered exclusively men's: input purchase, pesticide application, machinery use, and on-field irrigation. Women tend to perform more labour-intensive tasks such as weeding, harvesting, and post-harvest processing of crops. In contrast, men become more active after a machine for that task becomes available (e.g., ploughing, tillage, planting). Field work is often divided by gender in Pakistan: women are responsible for the initial stages of crop production, whereas women focus more on later stages and tasks that require dexterity or are time-consuming: planting, transplanting and harvesting crops; tending animals; and, food and seed processing and storage.

## 5.2.2 Livestock

Rural women carry heavy responsibilities in management of livestock, which contributes to nearly half of the total value added by livestock production (1987–1988). Women's contribution is higher than men's in the livestock sector that makes up 11 percent of GDP. Many products are exported, where "meat & meat preparations" and "milk & milk products" account for 50% of total agricultural exports. The value added by livestock production was US$ 374 million (2008–2009) and achieved an average per annum growth rate of 39 percent; the trade is mainly with the Gulf region to

With respect to livestock, men are in charge of fodder production, feed and medicine purchase, stallfeeding, milking, breeding and curing.131 Women are responsible for collecting fodder, water, the animals' overage care, cleaning stalls, selling dairy products and marketing.132 Most other goods processed or produced by women are usually sold by men, who keep the cash generated.

### 5.2.3: Household Chores

Pakistani women in the rural areas spend on average 85 hours per week on household chores.133 The unbalanced division of labor between men and women is more pronounced among the poor and even more pronounced in some regions. In the Middle East, North Africa and South Asia, girls aged 5–14 spend an average of 20 percent or less of their time on household chores; boys that age spend about half as much. The average is that girls aged 5–10 and 10–14 spend 30 percent and 50 percent more of their time, respectively, than boys of the same age.134 Household responsibilities deprive girls of important opportunities to attend school or marry their childhoods.135 They also perpetuate the gender stereotypes and the burden on women and girls.136

### 5.2.4: Supplementary Income

Both women and men in rural areas supplement household income by working as day-laborers in agriculture and non-agricultural jobs. The overwhelming majority of women's such jobs involve harvesting, in particular cottons. As a rule, rural women participate in the family's income-generating activities.

The society does not allow women to relocate to the cities for work, and they engage in construction works and tile making.137 Provided that the commute to the location does not compromise their household responsibilities. Such opportunities are difficult to find, and the lack of employment for women causes severe impacts on their well-being.138 In Pakistan, 50 percent of rural women aged between 20–49 years old had worked as non-agricultural employees.139 In the off-season, some rural women and children go to nearby better-off villages and cities to beg for food and money.

### 5.2.5: Decision Making

Generally in South Asia, women are much more likely to be involved in decisions that are perceived as routine or non-economic, such as food purchases, than those that are considered major events.140 There appears no differences in women's decision-making power among Hindus and Muslims in the Region.

### 5.3 Agriculture Resources and Service

#### 5.3.1 Land and Livestock

In agricultural households, land mostly belongs to male members of the household. Women, even if they have rights, are given less access to land. Nationwide, 15 percent of urban households and 44 percent of rural households own land. In 2012-2013, 89 percent of ever-married women aged 15–49 did not own a house, and 96 percent did not own any land.

Proportionately more of rural than urban households own livestock: 64 percent and 12 percent, respectively. Women’s access to livestock is even less in rural areas (30 percent) compared with urban areas (5–point increase) in urban areas between 2006-07 and 2012-2013.

#### Data regarding women's livestock ownership is unavailable, but it is considered to be a low as that of land.


#### 5.3.2 Agriculture Extension Services

Agriculture extension services are essential to transfer effectively the latest knowledge and research results to the farmers for the sake of better farm management; their services are essential for improvement of rural livelihoods and food security. Despite the decentralization of governmental functions, strong mandates of Provincial Departments concerned, and innovations recently introduced, the services in


---
**Page 134**
---

### 3.3.1. Livestock Extension Services

The public sector livestock sector suffers from inadequate and tardy disbursement of government funds. As a result, staff mobilization is limited, equipment is often out of order, and supply of vaccines and medicines is chronically insufficient.

To compound the difficulties, no official system exists for cost recovery for services provided by extension officers. The private sector has been able to provide some veterinary consultations and medicines, but the farmers consider the fees charged excessive (Rs 500-1,000), and the quality of service poor. In addition, the number of livestock extension officers is inadequate; female staff members are very few, many positions remain vacant, deployment of available personnel is neither strategic nor rational (i.e., based on animal counts, area covered, or workload), supervision is weak, and there is a lack of appropriate incentives.

The private sector in the field is limited to animal health, and very few private agents have been trained on livestock production extension. The sector remains unregulated, which leaves the competency of workers unchecked. In fact, many of the para-veterinary workers are trained through donor-funded projects but not necessarily certified by the relevant authorities. Due to the lack of a regulatory framework, multiplicity of factors at work, farmers are often unable to tell the quality of the services provided.

To overcome the scarcity of trained veterinary staff, the number of veterinary training institutes and universities needs to be increased; especially those for women.


### 3.3.4. Agricultural Credit

Pakistan has a network of financial institutions in the agriculture sector. It ranges from commercial banks and development finance institutions to microfinance institutions. Among them, the First Women Bank promotes women's participation in agricultural activities by providing credit facilities with low interest rates, participation and contribution are significant.

The International Labour Organization has provided funding for nearly 3000 women in the rural areas to combat child labour in agriculture.

The reasons include lack of formal institutions, high interest rate, distance from home bank, untimely disbursement, complex procedure, unlawful demands of officials, and lack of collateral that is asked by non-microfinance institutions.149 Credit for livestock is much harder to obtain than credit for agriculture because the latter can be mortgaged as security against loans. 3.3.5 Agricultural Insurance
Due to marginal involvement of the private sector and lack of reliable data on calamities, cropping pattern, and so on, the agricultural insurance sector remained underdeveloped for a long time. In 2008, however, the Government introduced a micro-insurance scheme for small farmers in Punjab by allowing them requesting a loan from a financial institution for any of the five major crops: wheat, rice, sugarcane, cotton and maize. The scheme was extended to other provinces later on. It covers losses due to droughts, buffaloes, small ruminants and poultry, written on a small-scale by various private insurance companies.

Unfortunately, the Government's payment of premium on behalf of smallholder farmers is not done in a structured manner. In 2013, only about 5 percent of the total number of insured farmers received their claims. The claim-to-premium ratio has remained at approximately 50 percent. Consequently more insurance companies are now offering crop insurance to small farmers. Approximately 18 insurance companies offered crop loan insurance in 2013 as compared to only four in 2008.

In April of July 2017, there were 347,260 micro-insurance policy holders in Pakistan, but only 1 percent were on agriculture and livestock despite the fact that half of the policy holders resided in rural areas.154

3.3.6 Mobile Phone and the Internet
Pakistan offered one of the most affordable mobile-broadband prices in Asia and the Pacific in 2015, despite its low Gross National Income per capita (GNP p.c.). prepaid mobile-broadband plans at prices below US$8 per month were available for sale in Pakistan. The number of internet users increased from only 175 in 2014 to 369 million in 2016, terms of ICT Development Index,157 less than 10 percent of the population


---
**Page 136**
---

owned a mobile phone in 2015 and 18 percent individuals had internet access in 2015.

The conservative segment of the Pakistani society is of the opinion that women would be negatively influenced by the vulgarity emitted through television and by the uncontrolled access via mobile phones to religiously inappropriate content. The media, therefore, has been a tool for social control. It justified domestic violence on the part of husbands if wives used mobile phone television or social media. Indeed, the gender gap in mobile-phone ownership in Pakistan (86% of men versus 27% of women) is one of the largest in the world. The country ranks last among all countries in the world at 11 percentage points. One of the contributing factors to the low rate is the lack of technical literacy and confidence among rural women to fully exploit the functions of a mobile device.

### 5.3.7. Traditional Media

In 2002, the Government of Pakistan liberalized the media, which led to a sharp increase in the number of television channels and sensationalism at the expense of quality journalism. Owing to the near monopoly over terrestrial broadcasting, the Pakistan Television Corporation – an autonomous public organization – dominates the airwaves. The government also licenses private radio stations, cable or satellite broadcasters. The mainstream media has an urban base, whose market trend and financial priorities shape the content it favors in the cities. The popularity of television programs is measured among viewers aged 15–49 years old. The channel was forced in 2016 to cut back on the coverage of flooding in Sindh due to its low ratings.

Despite the growth of television industry, FM radio is still popular in both urban and rural areas. Radio Pakistan remains the primary source of news and information on radio, because privately owned radio stations are not allowed to broadcast news or current affairs programs. Consequently, political voices off the mainstream are seldom broadcast. The Pakistan Broadcasting Corporation (PBC) broadcasts national news bulletins from Islamabad and regional news bulletins target local communities and broadcast music, talk shows, as well as youth- and women-oriented programs in local languages. No other program exists in Pakistan that could be called community news.

The rural population is not a homogenous audience. Rural women are more likely to communicate among themselves or to the urban population who tend to have more political and financial leverage. The available literature recognizes the rural population as distinct audience, but not rural women.


### 5.4. Water Supply and Sanitation

The lack of safe drinking water and appropriate sanitation facilities and low-quality health services affect both women's physical well-being and their risk of contracting diseases.

Sanitation is an issue that goes beyond physical health for women and girls; lack

# 5.5. Health

The health of Pakistani people is characterized by high population growth rate, high infant and child mortality rate, high maternal mortality ratio, and a high burden from communicable diseases.

The total public health expenditure as percent of GDP and the total capital outlay for health per capita are very low. Total public health expenditure as percentage of GDP was 0.91 percent in 2016-17, whereas the average of OECD countries was reported to be 8.04 percent on an average.

The country's public primary care is carried out by Basic Health Units, Rural Health Centres, and Maternal and Child Health Centres. These are located at a distance from each other in rural areas; hence basic obstetric care in the rural areas with community outreach programs offered through Lady Health Visitors (LHV). A large portion of medical consultations is estimated to take place in the private sector: two-thirds according to a report published by the Pakistan Medical Association in 2015. The LHV programme is not available in urban areas, and the regulations on the accreditation of doctors, nurses and LVs are rarely enforced.

Rural women are highly susceptible to diseases, and more so than rural men, due to their higher burden of on-farm and off-farm activities; less balanced nutrient intake; and early pregnancies. A great danger is posed by the lack of access to health care services in rural areas. The nearest health facility may be located at a distance from the nearest health facility, usually in an urban area, and on a donkey cart! Malnutrition, diarrhoea, acute respiratory illness, other communicable and vaccine-preventable diseases cause high infant and perinatal mortality rates. Lack of access to safe drinking water, poor sanitation, overcrowding, malnutrition and insufficient access to emergency obstetric care services cause high maternal mortality.

Malnutrition is widespread among children and women, especially of child-bearing age (45+), due to poor nutritional knowledge, heavy burden of responsibilities, low socioeconomic status, lack of leisure time, unequal distribution of food within the family, inadequate feeding practices, long birth intervals, large family size and poor health facilities. Due to malnutrition, 30-40 percent of Pakistani children are stunted.

Cooking and heating indoors constitute a major issue in Pakistan, as most rely on solid biomass fuels such as wood, charcoal, crop waste, dung, thrash and coal. Smoke as well as various pollutants produced under these conditions are associated with acute respiratory illness, pneumonia, chronic obstructive lung disease, cancer, and other health problems.


---
**Page 138**
---

possibly tuberculosis, asthma, cataracts, and low birth weight. Cooking is the responsibility of women, they run higher risks of illness from smoke than men.

### 5.6. Coping with Negative Shocks

According to a survey conducted in 2012, medical expenses represented by far the most common negative economic shocks that rural households experienced in the preceding two years, close to 60 percent of households reported such an experience. The second most common shock was loss of housing due to floods in 2010 and 2011; over a quarter of households in Pakistan was affected. However, more than half of these households were able to recover within six months.

The possibility of negative shocks was the strategy for most households, more than half that belonged to the lowest and more than 40 percent of those made up the highest quintiles of per capita expenditure. The tendency was even higher in flood-prone areas where 65 percent of households reported prepayment wedding and medical expenses; for weddings, 26 percent of households coped with additional members of household or borrowing from family and friends. For medical expenses, 30 percent used similar mechanisms: substitution of cheaper food, reduction of food consumption, informal borrowing, and assistance from other people and organizations.

### 6. GENDER IN PUNJAB AND SINDH

#### 6.1. Basic Statistics—Punjab and Sindh

##### 6.1.1 Population—Punjab and Sindh

Based on the 2017 Census, Punjab has a proportional population of 110 million and Sindh has a population of 48 million. The targeted districts of Punjab and Sindh contain 3.45 million households and 21.5 million people: 10.5 million females, 11.0 million males, and 739 transgenders.

In the two provinces, the average household size is 6.2 persons, with a significant difference between urban or rural areas. The population growth rate since 1998 has averaged 2.7 percent per year. Nearly half or more of the population live in rural areas: 63 percent of households in Punjab and 49 percent in Sindh. In rural Punjab, persons aged less than 50 years of age comprised 44 percent of total population; in rural Sindh it was 46 persons in the same year.

##### 6.1.2 Literacy Situation—Punjab and Sindh

In Punjab and Sindh, the literacy rate of the proposed project, the literacy rates were considerably lower in the rural areas than the national average: 58 percent in 2016-2017 and more for rural areas (49.3 percent) compared to urban Punjab and 19 percent in rural Sindh.

The targeted districts in Punjab have a literacy rate of young women aged between 11 years old and 13 years, respectively. In 2014, the literacy rate among young women aged 15-24 was 76 percent in Punjab and 80 percent in Sindh. The overall low literacy rates imply that the ability of the rural female population to use information

### 6.1.3 Poverty and Wealth—Punjab and Sindh

The official statistics show that 30 percent and 18 percent of households in Punjab and Sindh, respectively, own land, but those with legally recognizable ownership may be considerably less. The farmers who claim to own land may not have documents that prove the transfer or ownership; a situation that does largely prevail in rural areas. In addition, there are many cases where the government has expropriated the farms and do not consider the sale legitimate and have demanded eviction. Around 93 percent of women in Punjab and Sindh report that they do not own land (2014). The share of households owning more than one plot is 58.7 percent, with 6.3 percent owning two plots and 1.3 percent owning three or more plots. In rural areas, the share of households owning more than one plot is even lower at 57.9 percent and 0.4 percent, respectively. There is no household in the rural areas that is composed solely of men; and the statistics imply that women are excluded from the concept of household ownership of land and house.

Household ownership of livestock, the most common form of assets after land, is distributed between 22 percent and 32 percent in the targeted districts. Livestock acts as a buffer against income fluctuation.


---

and a means of investment and food security. It is also a route source of cash for many smallholder farmers to buy inputs such as seeds and pesticides, to pay for school fees, to purchase dairy groceries, medicines, and so on. Large bears can lead large family expenses; weddings and major medical treatments." Unlike the cash crops, the production of milk and dairy goods (butter, cheese, yogurt) is not a significant source of income in Punjab and Sindh.

In Southern Punjab and Sindh, about two-thirds of the households were below the poverty line for one or more of the three years, 2004, 2008, and 2010; only one-third never had experienced poverty. (17) Other provinces have not had such a prevalence of poverty. (17) The wealth disparity in the rural areas is large: according to the Pakistan Bureau of Statistics, 35 percent of the population lives on less than $1 per day and 20 percent live below the poverty line. (18) In Punjab and Sindh, 46 percent of households in Sindh in 2014. (17) Other words, a large segment of the population, especially in the rural areas of Punjab and Sindh, is highly vulnerable to climate change.

### 6.1.4 Marriage—Punjab and Sindh

In the Provinces of Punjab and Sindh, the percentage of women aged 20-39 who were first married before or at age 15 was 8 percent and 11 percent, respectively; in 2014 in Punjab and Sindh, 16 percent of women aged 15–19 and 15 percent of women aged 20–24 were married and their spouse was 10 or more years older. (17) The proportions were 12 percent and 15 percent, respectively, in Sindh. (18)

(19) Sindh, as the second largest province in terms of agricultural output, (20) produces approximately the rest of the national production of these agricultural goods.

Cotton is one of the major cash crops of Punjab and supports the vital industrial and export sector (in particular, the textile and clothing industries). (21) Of the total area dedicated to cotton production in 2011, approximately 80 percent was in Punjab and 20 percent was in Sindh. In Punjab and Sindh[10] are engaged in labour-intensive work of cotton-picking by hand at very low wages. Punjabi women’s participation in work on their own farm in commercial agriculture village is significantly greater than that found in irrigated villages (68% as compared to 40% for Central Punjab)[23]. For rice cultivation in Punjab, women spend 2-3 hours for every hour spent by men.[1] Women's main agricultural activities are weeding and harvesting. In Sindh, women spend more time on farm work than men do; however, women only: harvesting is done jointly, but women put in six days whereas men spare four of their ten days[4]. The gender division of labour is also reflected in the unequal distribution of land allocated to men's time allocation of ten hours.[10] This task differentiation leads to that of knowledge and skills; neither men nor women can grow cash crops all by themselves.

The cultivation of rice and cotton in Sindh casts a bulk of women’s work on semi-urban agricultural activities: 39 percent of the total working population is engaged in this activity[24]. Women are involved in various stages of rice and cotton cultivation and almost all of them in cotton picking, but only 10 percent participate in the selling of cotton.[1]

Women are also extensively engaged in the production of other cash crops: field preparation, seed preparation, planting, transplanting, weeding, sowing, fodder cutting, harvesting, threshing, sealing, storage and marketing. They are also involved in the cultivation of fruits and vegetables, as well as fodder[25] collection and a very good part of livestock rearing.[10] In addition to feeding animals, women collect firewood for cooking and heating their homes; they also collect water for use as fuel.[4] Rural women report 12-14 hours of work per day. Men are mainly responsible for input purchase, mechanized work, chemical handling, product sales and water management.

Work for landowners is mostly paid in kind. In northern Sindh: both male and female farmers are paid 40 kilograms of wheat upon harvesting less than an acre; without which the crop would not be sold[26]. Small farmers tend to receive less than one acre per year, while in Badin District, farmers are expected to harvest 5 kg of rice per day and 40 kg per acre.[1] The amount beyond this quota is kept by the farmer. In some villages, landowners provide food for their workers; however, the government provides flour on a monthly basis, but the ration is often not enough for household consumption; the village administration has to intervene in such cases[27]. In other villages, landowners provide the following distribution of profits:[10] The tenants also borrow money from the landowner through a supervisor.[4] In some villages, landlords allow women to operate kitchen gardens on the terms that they share half of the vegetables produced with him.[28]

### 6.2.3: Household Chores—Punjab and Sindh

All household chores fall on the shoulders of women: water fetching, food cultivation, collection of care for the children and the elderly; planning for and delivering food and clothes for the entire family; cleaning the house and its surroundings; managing handcrafts for home use and supplementary income. For men, home is a place to relax and spend time with friends and relatives. Women have little time for themselves or even a little break time for themselves. Interactions outside the household are usually left to men, who sell agricultural produce in markets and engage in other activities.

In Punjab it indicates that men spend only ten minutes to two hours per year for each of these two tasks.[25] In the Chakwal district of Sindh, the overwhelming bulk of household chores were carried out by rural women, while men participated in the education and socialization of the children; conflict management, and formal social matters.

### 6.2.4: Supplementary Income—Punjab and Sindh

Women receive as low as Rs FKR 150-200 (approximately USD 1.3–1.7) for picking-40 kilograms of cotton.[26] The major economic activity for women in Punjab and Sindh.

Embroidery, knitting and handcraft-making are an integral part of chores for many rural South households. In the Chakwal district of Sindh, traditional migration of men in search of work to urban areas has been reported during the off-

# 6.2.3: Decision Making – Punjab and Sindh

In the case of rice in Punjab, women make the decisions at most steps during production, ranging from preparing the seedbed to selling/burning. In contrast, women make decisions regarding only 2 of the 19 steps involved in cotton cultivation (planting and harvesting), with men making all other food preparation at home. Livestock management is considered to be mainly the responsibility of women, but these activities are also influenced by male dominance and traditional belief systems (as well as by age, if it concerns a young woman).

The landowners have the final say in which crop and variety to cultivate or not, although they may be open to suggestions from their wives. In some cases, however, they may decide against switching to cotton because of the farmers’ observation that the nearby farm with the same soil did not well with cotton. Another village in Sindh, the landowner did not allow female farmers to grow vegetables.

It is usually men who sell dairy and other goods procured or produced by women to outsiders and keep the cash income. According to the women consulted in the field, all spending decisions are made jointly for the benefit of the entire household. In rural Pakistan, 37 percent of married women aged 15–49 reported that they had no say over how money was spent at home. The same proportion (37%) of the same group responded that decisions were made mainly by the wife. With respect to their husband’s decision-making power in the household, 28% said he had more influence than his wife.

The rural women consulted did not see injustice or inconvenience in the current arrangement, but this needs to be interpreted in the context of their view of justice. In Punjab, in 40 percent of women believed that it was just for a husband to beat his wife if she argues with him and he is right. The most common accusation made by men against their wives was that they were not faithful (36%), followed by going out without telling her husband or arguing with him (26%). Agreed in the same year in Sindh, 37 percent of women said that it was just for a husband to beat his wife if she argues with him and he is right. The most common accusation made by men against their wives was that they agreed to hitting and beating if she argued with him; 36 percent if she went out without telling her husband, 35 percent if she does not perform household chores, almost 30 percent if the wife refuses to have sex with him.

In Pakistan, in 28% of women said that it was just for a husband to beat his wife. In Punjab, 28 percent of Sindh women, it was reasonable that husbands hit and beat their wives if they used mobile phones, consumed alcohol or smoked. When the two groups were compared, it emerged that the rural women were more accepting of domestic violence.

# 6.3 Agriculture Resources and Services – Punjab and Sindh

## 6.3.1 Land and Livestock – Punjab and Sindh

The majority of women in Punjab (97% and 68%, respectively) and Sindh (99% and 79%, respectively) do not own agricultural land. In terms of ownership by households, 31 percent in Punjab Province and 25 percent in Sindh Province are owned by women. The rates of livestock ownership by households range from 22 to 52 percent of agricultural households.

## 6.3.2 Agriculture Extension Services – Punjab and Sindh

The farmers in Punjab and Sindh are almost unanimously dissatisfied with the agriculture extension services. According to them, they almost never use an extension agent. One of the prominent reasons is that the extension agents are not trained properly and are not able to provide information up-to-date for people. Another is that the agents in both public and private sectors target large and medium scale farmers, who are more willing to and easily take up new approaches, casting aside the skeletons of the farmers. The farmers are also dissatisfied with the extension services provided by the officers working on private companies for information who may not be well informed and are likely to promote their own business.

The situation with respect to female field staff is improving, but still very few of them are active in either sector, public or private. In Punjab, the first female specialist in extension was hired in 2007, and since then the number of female specialists has increased to 85%. While male Agriculture Officers are involved in the promotion of cotton, wheat, and rice, female officers are more likely to be involved with farmers on clean cotton pickers and kitchen gardening. The qualification required for the post is a diploma that is conferred after three years of study, which has discouraged women from pursuing the career as extension officers. In Sindh, there were no female Agriculture Officers in 2015. However, Punjab as well as that of the Director General Agriculture – 48 in all - are occupied by men and none by a woman. The reason for this is that the majority of the farmers in both provinces are not part of extension services, in spite of their contribution to agriculture, including vegetable farming.

Mobile agricultural services have gained attention lately as a promising tool to improve the efficiency and cost-effectiveness of extension efforts; the Government of Punjab developed such services called AgriSmart. The database for the application will eventually comprise of farmer advisory services, plant clinics, livestock services, and other related information. The pilot project was launched in October 2017.

sampling and testing, fertilizer monitoring and testing, and research trials. The application has aimed at creating an accurate record of field staff activities and reducing their time spent on departmental tasks – which comes at the expense of field visits – from 67 percent to 15 percent. In Sindh, development of a similar service was explored under the Pakistan-Agriculture Private Sector Linkages Programme (PASL). Women farmers in this region are more likely than men to report that they do not have access to land ownership and literacy among women are considerably lower; (ii) female farmers need to connect with financial institutions for loans, but these institutions are reluctant to provide them credit; and, (iii) many rural women believe that mobile ownership is undesirable for them. Efforts of neither of the two initiatives were successful.

Under the name of e-Agriculture or mAgri, major private telecommunication companies, such as Telcom and Mobilink, have launched consultation and advisory services in collaboration with local governments and non-governmental organizations (NGOs) to provide mobile phone-based extension services. In Punjab, it distributed 125,000 smartphones to access their mobile platform[1] with real-time information on weather forecasts, new techniques, and weather forecasting websites. Similarly, in Azad Jammu and Kashmir, the government launched a similar service under the name of e-Agriculture. Al Ahsabari Kissan, utilizes internet-based services for providing extension services through SMSes. It disseminates information on various topics – optimized cultivation methods, modern farming techniques, health, weather, crop insurance, and market analysis. The service is available in 10 districts of the province. However, the content provided to be provided, none of them refer to information pertaining to agricultural tasks undertaken by female farmers or women’s role in agriculture.

A study conducted in Sindh showed that slightly under half of farmers, who used the mobile phone service for buying inputs and selling products, neither agreed nor disagreed as to whether the service expanded their market information.[2]

The field consultations revealed that the farmers are still exploring an effective use of these services. [3] Farmers in some villages were not aware about the existence of such services or had never attended meetings, and most women did not own mobile phones. Women in some villages were not allowed to, although they could access the service through their husbands’ or fathers’ smartphones. The farmers who used phone services as described above can be used effectively by these women. A handful of male farmers who accessed such services were also found to be using them for their wives and daughters. However, most did not volunteer to share information on or whether they shared the information with other villagers without access. Spread of new information appeared certain only when a risk-taking farmer manages to demonstrate its benefits.

6.3.1.2 Volunteer Extension Services – Punjab and Sindh

Considering the numerous responsibilities with respect to livestock that women shoulder as well as the fact that it is not acceptable for female farmers to interact with male outsiders, strengthening of female cadre in the field of livestock is nearly synonymous with strengthening of the sector, which is already contributing significantly to the national economy. The role of women in livestock production has been recognized by both women and men remain neglected, especially for women and more so than agriculture extension service.


---
**Page 146**
---

In Punjab districts, the animal-to-veterinarian ratios were over ten times more than what are considered acceptable internationally. The effectiveness of many veterinary hospitals was not up to the required levels in 2013. In 2014, there existed 566 Veterinary Hospitals and 1 664 Mobile Veterinary Dispensaries in Punjab where some 126 million animals received treatment, including vaccination and castration during 2014-15. Comparable data for Sindh was not available.

The Livestock and Dairy Development offices for the Dera Ghazi Khan District of Punjab Province provide a network of veterinary services to farmers through mobile veterinary units and veterinary hospitals. With the emergence of prevailing diseases like haemorrhagic septicemia and other cattle diseases, advice on breeds and feed, and animal health education are provided by the Mobile Veterinary Dispensary Scheme. The Mobile Veterinary Dispensary for each Union Council, seven female veterinarians in total are working for the Dispensaries. For each town, a Civil Veterinary Hospital has been established, and for each tehsil (rural areas), one Civil Veterinary Hospital is located. Each hospital has two veterinary assistants, ten each, who in turn train community members. Seven Mobile Veterinary Dispensaries have been established to serve the population of 100 villages. They acknowledged that more technical staff members are required for effective results.

Aware of the māgriculture trend, the Livestock and Dairy Development of Punjab is developing a mobile-based extension service in their domain. Recognising that mobile-phone initiatives to provide price, weather, crop advisories and such have had little impact on farm results, and reasoning that it may have been because farmers are not aware of the information provided, they are now developing an SMS platform for aggregated, locally relevant and reliable information on farm inputs – including artificial insemination services. The scheme is still in its pilot phase but has received positive feedback. However, the prominent role of women in the dairy industry is not discussed in relation to this development.

### 6.3.4: Agricultural Credit — Punjab and Sindh

During 2013-14, a total of Rs 238 266 million (USD 2 015 million) of loans were generated in Punjab; about two-thirds by commercial banks, a quarter by one of the public banks specialising in agriculture, Zara Finance Corporation, and the remaining by other public sector banks. Of these loans, only 43% were granted loans by the Bank in 2014-15: only ten were landless, the poorest farmers who are our targets under the Punjab Agricultural Credit Scheme. The majority of the borrowers were from the middle class at the greatest disadvantage. The same public bank disbursed PKR 7 522 million (USD 108 million) as loans to Sindh.

## Agriculture Insurance - Punjab and Sindh

During April-July 2017 in Punjab, 5,238 insurance policy holders were insured for the sum of PKR 134 billion (USD 1.26 billion). In Sindh, 9,891 policyholders had PKR 25,781 million (USD 222 million).

## Mobile Phones and the Internet - Punjab and Sindh

In 2010-11, Punjab and Sindh counted 62.30 million mobile subscribers; 57 percent and 28 percent of women in Punjab and Sindh respectively had used a computer while only 5.6 percent in Sindh. Regarding internet usage, 7.3 percent of the women population have ever used the internet in Punjab while in Sindh, it was 2.7 percent.

The young generation gives a strong prospect in this field. It is well known that mobile phone ownership is higher worldwide among younger women (15–34 years old) than older women (18 and older). In Punjab in 2014, 21 percent of young women aged 15-24 used a computer during the last 12 months and 14 percent of the same group during the previous month. 6.3.7: Traditional Media – Punjab and Sindh

In Punjab in 2014, more than one-third of Punjabi women did not have regular exposure to newspaper, radio or television. In Sindh, this figure was even higher at almost two-thirds. The situation for internet use was similar; the percent of women either watched television, read a newspaper or magazine or listened to the radio at least once a week.

6.4: Water Supply and Sanitation – Punjab and Sindh

6.4.1: Water Supply – Punjab and Sindh

The vast majority (95%) of rural households in targeted districts rely solely on groundwater from wells for all uses: drinking and washing for people and livestock, as well as for cooking. Water sources are usually located at a distance that can be reached within about half an hour on foot, but each one is shared by many households. The norm is to collect water twice daily, once in the morning when the household fetches the source and pumping the water. The norm for water collection is twice a day, but also as the need arises.

It is the responsibility of women and girls to fetch water, and some women and girls complain of headaches caused by carrying a heavy load on the head. Men may be involved, if no other member in the household is available or an animal-drawn cart is used for the job. Water is not treated before use, and some villagers have identified it as the cause of diarrhea, stomach pain and fever during summer. In the region, 20 percent of households reported that water was contaminated by animals, while 39 percent of households drink water contaminated by E. coli.

6.4.2: Sanitation – Punjab and Sindh

A quarter of the population in rural Punjab does not have access to toilet facilities, and about 35 percent of those who do use toilets are without access to improved sanitation facilities. Unimproved sanitation methods mostly use open pits, open field, and buckets. Open drains do not fare better with respect to hygiene, as they typically discharge into nearby land or depressions, streets, or water bodies.

# 6.5. Health—Punjab and Sindh

According to a recent study, 41 percent of the poorest rural women in Sindh are malnourished, compared with 19 percent of the Nigerian counterparts, while the figures for the richest urban groups in the two countries are comparable. The urban-rural gap is much more glaring for Sindhis in Pakistan than for Fulani in Nigeria. Women in Punjab and Sindh are also at a disadvantage when it comes to education for girls, due to food distribution being in favour of boys, and much more so for girls in rural areas; where traditional values persist.

Women suffer from: hand-washing, skin rashes, and allergies from exposure to pesticides, and finger cuts, both affecting their productivity and menstruation problems due to long hours of cotton picking by hand without proper nutrition. In general, the employers provide no safety measures against chemicals; women wrap their scarves around their mouth to prevent excess inhalation. (Using hours in the fields leave rural women with a high risk of lung cancer.) Women are also at risk from HIV/AIDS and other sexually transmitted diseases as well as on the children,126, and many children are stunted due to malnutrition.127 In Punjab, about one-third of all children under five years old (34 percent) were wasted or thin for their age (or short for their age) in 2014.128 In Sindh, 42 percent of the same age group were underweight, 17 percent severely so. The proportion was even higher among girls: 56 and 39 percent respectively.129 Among the same group in Punjab, 15 percent in Sindh were wasted (or thin for their height).130 The data disaggregated by wealth in Punjab showed that nearly half of the children living in the households in the lowest quintile were wasted or thin for their age, compared with only one-third among those in the highest quintile. Malnutrition is most evident physically, but it is also deeply connected to overall developmental of children. The villages reported that the Lints unit only lists for polio vaccination.

The majority of rural Punjab households used solid fuel in 2014; all of the lowest-quintile and 4 percent of the highest-quintile households.131 The situation is quite similar in Sindh: the quintile-based rates for Sindh were nearly 100 percent and 2 percent, respectively.

# 6.6. Coping with Negative Shocks—Punjab and Sindh

Climate change prominently features in the minds of both male and female farmers, manifested as higher temperature, growing water scarcity, increasingly irregular and unfamiliar rain patterns, failure in cotton


---
**Page 150**
---

ball formation, and increase of certain insects.[38] The farmers have noticed an exacerbation of waterlogging and salinity, which had been serious problems even before the onset of climate change.[39] Riverside villages are more prone to floods than before, but do not have any strategy than to flee to elevated grounds when they occur.[40]

Regional variation was reported in what constituted the major shocks in two years prior to 2012.[41] Medical expenses were the most important in Punjab (36%), followed by food costs (10%) and cut-off of credit facilities (8%). In Sindh, flood-related shocks were considered; lost or damaged home due to a flood (39%), loss of livestock (35%), and loss of crops (27%). In Balochistan, the most important shock was a flood.[41] Medical expenses were regarded major shocks by 36 percent of households, and weddings by only 1 percent. As described above, more than half of the poorest households did not have any strategy to cope with floods.

### Project Formulation and Implementation Principles

#### 7.1 Risks and Opportunities for Farmers Facing Climate Change

Rural women constitute the most vulnerable segment of the Pakistani society; they have the least physical, human and social capital to cope with negative extreme shocks, such as climate change. As with any vulnerability assessment, it is important to recognize that there are different levels of vulnerability within a community. In Pakistan, the privileged, most notably rural women and the rest, and push back any progress made with respect to human development and gender equality in the country, both of which are currently at unsatisfactory levels.

Climate change has already negatively affected water resources and agriculture and led to an increase in natural disasters that force people to evacuate in a short notice, such as floods. If the society as a whole is prepared for these changes, it will be able to mitigate their effects on women farmers who tend to have more physical strength than women, are only interested in saving large livestock from floods but not small animals owned by women; disaster poses greater financial risks for women.

Empowerment of women would help them cope with climate change.

As is often put, agriculture is the backbone of the national economy, and about half of the value-added in the sector is livestock derived. Nearly all of the responsibilities of livestock management fall on women, but they are not involved in decision-making processes related to livestock production. Allowing women to make decisions on livestock makes sense as it does in terms of food and nutritional security since dairy and poultry products are more nutritious than grains. Moreover, livestock serves as assets and cash flow during times of crisis; the income fluctuations associated with livestock provide financial security to households. In sustainable agriculture, livestock management is more than often a women's task.

Vegetable cultivation is another task that is considered women's if many more female farmers take up the practice, it will benefit the entire households' food and nutritional security. In general, women farmers are much more open than men to activities that are new and not linked to power; they provide a valuable and ready entry point for diversification, which is one of the key elements in climate-change resilience.


---
**Page 151**
---

### 2.1: Strengthening Female Technical Community

The female technical community is limited in disciplines that have traditionally been considered masculine, such as irrigation, land and water management, climatology, information technology, agriculture sciences, animal and veterinary sciences. The project will engage female professionals where they are underrepresented to provide them with the necessary skills and knowledge so that they are better prepared for their career development. The proportion of female students in these fields is increasing, providing an excellent opportunity to strengthen the female technical community in Pakistan.

### 2.2: Leveraging Gender-Differentiated Tasks and Interests

Women and men are largely engaged in different tasks, and women are more willing to take up new practices. They also have stronger interests in food and nutritional security. The project will leverage these differences by providing women with the necessary skills for their tasks and interests, both of which will boost resilience to climate change and improve the health of all household members. Most importantly, children's health and development, in which balanced nutrition plays a critical role, will be positively impacted.

### 2.3: Women’s Participation in Coping with Negative Shocks

The poorest households lack viable means to cope with shocks that climate change has brought about. The project's goal is to increase their resilience to climate change is precisely endowing them with a long-term solution to these problems. This will be achieved by providing women with the necessary wood available to each household by means of kitchen gardens and livestock rearing and to minimise the risk of having to consume less expensive food items or eat less altogether: if the products yield cash income, it would be possible for women to buy other food items that are more nutritious. In addition, endowing women with the ability to bring cash home is considered the surest way to empower them.

### 2.4: Sowing Seeds for Women’s Networks

Rural women face a big challenge in obtaining information, financial assistance and other resources as well as in connecting. One of the important factors in this equation is the lack of social networks; they greatly facilitate access to many kinds of resources. The project will establish Women Open Schools not only for technical information, but also as a base for networks that connect women farmers.

### 2.5: Assisting Women in Initial Investment

The lack of access to financial resources by women poses a great obstacle to tackling up climate resilient agriculture and animal husbandry production and animal rearing. The project will aim at lowering the barrier by providing seed packets, small ruminants and poultry through Women Open Schools where they learn how to grow vegetables and raise and care for small animals. This assistance will free women, who


---
**Page 152**
---

# 7.2.6: Tackling the Value Chain

No agricultural product or method is of great economic value without a value chain that accommodates it; all stakeholders from policy makers, input suppliers to landowners and so on need to be involved for the success of any project. The importance of gender equality in the design and implementation of national policy across all policy areas, the importance of gender- and age-disaggregated data will be stressed.

Landowners and farm managers will be targeted for actions to raise awareness on gender and climate resilient agriculture. The role of women as extension officers and other any conducted by the project will include gender awareness as a topic.

## 7.2.7: Improving Women's Access to Information

Women do not have good access to information and information technology, and despite the inclusion of the Ministry of Agriculture in various projects, women are largely excluded from the benefits especially of rural women’s strengths and constraints. The project will raise awareness among policy makers and community support professionals on women’s access to information: its importance and the need for it. Women’s networks will be established as a means of communication between women farmers as an important audience and produce programs that specifically target them. The establishment of female farmers’ networks would also contribute to improving women's access to information.

## 7.2.8: Creating a Niche for Young Women

Focusing on the considerable contribution of rural women to the livestock sector, the project will train young women in business management and Women in Open Schools. The project will also focus on their higher literacy, conventional and technical, and build their capacity in assisting older female farmers with computer-based information services. This activity will strengthen the information access of female farmers of all ages.

## 7.2.9: Improving Health of Girls

The empowerment of women under this project will enhance the nutritional security of women and girls; the stunting of children that prevails in the region will be reduced, and girls will be equipped with better prospects for life.

# 7.3: Principles of Project Implementation

## 7.3.1: Practical aspects of Female Farmers' Lives

The project will pay attention to the practical aspects of female farmers’ lives, such as their workload, restrictions on mobility, vulnerability due to age. The training sessions will be organised during off season for cash crops and at the village of residence or fairly close by, especially for young women considering that they are more likely to have a partner who is working in the city and would like them as possible to their residence, and in case they need to travel, a transportation fee will be provided for a male relative to accompany.

## 7.3.2: Effective Communication Methods

The vast majority of targeted women are illiterate and do not own mobile phones. Thus, the knowledge and skills will be transmitted through a female version of the Farmer Field School (Women Open School or WOS), which employs a participatory, adult learning methodology. Women from the villages not directly targeted by the project will be invited to visit nearby WOS sites for inspiration and learning by


---
**Page 153**
---

observation and by chatting with fellow female farmers. WGS Open Days: The project will also invite female farmers, regardless of their involvement in WGS, to participate in field day activities, which will be organised around demonstration plots. Radio programmes (rather than television shows or podcasts) and videos (to be shown at the WGSs) will be used to raise their awareness.

### 7.3: Network Creation

#### 7.3.1: Women Farmers – agriculture & livestock - will be involved wherever possible in activities for women farmers so as to create communication channels beyond their villages by utilising their mobility among villages and acting as messengers; the networks will eventually help in obtaining bargaining power in formal negotiations with government officials, private sector representatives, etc.

#### 7.3.4: Promotion of Mutual Understanding

The knowledge products produced for new farmers will be examined by female farmers and vice versa so that the two groups deepen their understanding of all activities in the field. The mutual understanding will avoid new activities being introduced without a clear understanding of how they work or what problems they solve, as well as a better understanding of pesticides, which is applied by men but whose toxic effects are felt mainly by women through prolonged exposure, for example during cotton picking.

#### 7.3: Monitoring and Evaluation

The monitoring and evaluation process will include a female gender and nutrition specialist who will ensure that the women farmers have the opportunity to report their own observations and frank opinions. The project will collect gender- and age-disaggregated data in connection with interventions for systematic use in planning, reporting, and evaluations.

### 7.4: Beneficiaries of the Project

#### Component 1 Establishment of Agriculture and Irrigation Management Information Systems for Resilience to Climate Change: This will benefit the government officials who are in charge of water resource management, agriculture and climate. Taking into account that there are next to no female government officials dealing with these issues, the project will train female university students, mapping in the related technical areas, and provide them with a platform for their professional development through the long-term process of strengthening the presence and contribution of female professionals in the country.

#### Component 2 Building on-Farm Resilience to Climate Change: Will target female farmers through Women Over Smallholder Farmers (WOSF) groups. The project will also train women on climate-resilient agriculture, weather, soil characteristics plant development, costs and yield, farm management and marketing skills, numeracy and financial literacy. Seed packets, small ruminants and poultry will be distributed to the women farmers in targeted villages. Women will be trained as facilitators to introduce new economic activities. Women from villages not targeted by the project will be invited to visit the School for Women Farmers (SFWF) at least once a year. Training on climate-resilient agriculture and climate resilient agriculture will be organised for landowners, farm managers, and financial-risk evaluation officers. Women in rural areas as well as women living in urban and peri-urban areas will be empowered technically, economically and socially and will enjoy a social environment that can turn their needs into opportunities. Women empowerment will also allow true progress of the society and ultimately benefit men.

Under Component 3 Creating an Enabling Environment for Continued Transformation, young women will be the chief agents of change in their communities. They will be trained to become climate-smart farmers such as weather forecasts, early warning, and agriculture advice. The purpose of the training is to create agents who can relay the information to other women farmers within their villages; the entire female farmer community will benefit from this. Further training targeting young women will be led by a facilitator, a major activity for rural women and for the national economy.

33

**Page 154**

8. FIELD CONSULTATION: QUESTIONS AND PARTNERS

### 8.1 Consultation Questions

The questions in this Section were used to identify the key issues in the lives of female farmers. Then literature search was conducted to further assess these issues in detail; the results are summarized in Section 7. The aim is to provide a basis for identification of risks and opportunities for female farmers facing climate change, and contributed to project formulation, establishment of implementation modalities and specifying beneficiaries (see Section 7).

The closer the institutions were involved with the farmers on the ground, the more they were convinced out of experience that it was a must to involve female farmers and implement gender-responsive activities for a project to succeed. They did not claim to have a systematic approach for tackling the issue, however.

Female farmers were involved in tasks that do not require muscle power or use of machinery. They were mainly responsible for work that required time and dexterity. As for livestock, they were in charge of the bulk of animal care activities such as feeding, milking, and cleaning. Female farmers were also responsible for the care of animals considered women’s, such as poultry and small ruminants. Their wages were understood to be lower than men’s accomplishing the same work; it was not possible to verify this claim as work is gendered in terms of tasks performed. Women were more attached to their livestock and trusted that men made wise financial decisions that benefitted the whole family.

Women were aware of climate change impacts, such as higher temperature and growing scarcity of water. They were also more interested than men in health, water and sanitation issues, directing the discussion on these topics when female farmers consulted did not speak on similar topics, except for baby delivery in the poorest Hindu village.

Access to education was positively related to the income level of villages, which in turn appeared linked to proximity to urban centers. In general, women had much less access to information than men, while some widows were empowered and had as much access as men.


#### 8.1.1 Core Consultation Questions for Institutions

- How has climate change affected your work?
- What are the major constraints in relation to your work?
- What are the actions taken to incentivize female farmer participation in training?
- What is the number of male professionals at your institution?
- Why is the number of female professionals at your institution low?
- How are female professionals encouraged to work for the institution?


#### 8.1.2 Core Consultation Questions for Female Farmers

- What are the main crops grown and women’s involvement in different stages of crop production other than planting and harvesting?
- Who owns which animals?
- How has climate change changed in the past five years?
- Which everyday tasks are performed by men? By women?
- Do you have access to water?
- How far is the drinking-water source? Whose responsibility is it to fetch water? How many times a day?
- How is the condition of drinking water? Has it affected health?


---
**Page 155**
---

- Do you treat water before drinking?
- How far is the nearest health care facility?
- What is the situation of sanitation in the village?
- Who sells agricultural and livestock products?
- Who decides what to plant?
- Who makes the decisions on spending?
- Are you involved in any type of cultivation?
- Do you have a mobile phone?

9. GENDER ACTION PLAN

The day-to-day implementation of the Gender Action Plan for the project, Transforming the Indus Basin with Climate Resilient Agriculture and Water Management, will be led by two full-time project- recruited Gender and Nutrition Specialists. These individuals will work in the Provincial Project Implementation Units (PPILs), and this will work under the direct supervision of the PPIL Director. The Gender Action Plan is designed to ensure that gender equality and women's empowerment are integrated into all aspects of the project, with a focus on close collaboration with other project- recruited staff who will lead day-to-day delivery of project activities at the federal, provincial and district levels. In this context, the General and Nutrition Specialists will be responsible for (a) working with other project- recruited staff to ensure gender considerations are integrated into all aspects of the project; (b) providing technical guidance and other specialists working at the federal level; project- recruited staff in the two PPILs, including the Provincial Coordinators and other technical specialists; and project- recruited staff in the eight District Project Implementation Units (DPIUs), including the Gender Training Teams (each of which will include two Women Open Schools [WOS] supervisors, who will also provide direct support for WOS – a key element of the gender action plan). The General and Nutrition Specialists will work collaboratively with the Responsible Entities (which play a key role in this aspect, as outlined in Section C.7 of the Funding Proposal and Sections 5 and 7 of the Feasibility Study) to ensure gender- differentiated needs are adequately integrated into the comprehensive support they provide in collaboration with this GCP project.

Results that are not shaded in green include indicators and targets that are specific to the Gender Action Plan. The Gender and Nutrition Specialists will bear overall responsibility for delivering the Gender Action Plan and will be directly responsible for (a) working with other project- recruited staff and partners to ensure gender considerations and support are integrated into project support in a manner that ensures achievement of the targets included in this plan; and (b) monitoring progress against these targets and reporting to the FAO [as] National Monitoring Specialist in particular.
